A Logic-based Algorithmic Meta-Theorem for Treedepth: Single Exponential FPT Time and Polynomial Space Thanks: G.S. was supported by the project BOBR that is funded from the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme with grant agreement No. 948057. In particular, a majority of work on this manuscript was done while B.B. and G.S. were affiliated with University of Warsaw. This project was initiated during the research stay of V.C. at the University of Warsaw.
Benjamin Bergougnoux, Vera Chekan, Giannos Stamoulis

Abstract
For a graph G G , the parameter treedepth measures the minimum depth among all forests F F , called elimination forests, such that G G is a subgraph of the ancestor-descendant closure of F F . We introduce a logic, called neighborhood operator logic with acyclicity, connectivity and clique constraints ( 𝖭𝖤𝖮 𝟤 ​ [ 𝖥𝖱𝖾𝖼 ] + 𝖠𝖢𝖪 \mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK} for short), that captures all NP-hard problems—like Independent Set or Hamiltonian Cycle —that are known to be tractable in time 2 𝒪 ⁡ ( 𝗍𝖽 ) ​ n 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) 2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)} and space n 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) n^{\mathcal{O}(1)} on n n -vertex graphs provided with elimination forests of depth 𝗍𝖽 \mathsf{td} . We provide a model checking algorithm for 𝖭𝖤𝖮 𝟤 ​ [ 𝖥𝖱𝖾𝖼 ] + 𝖠𝖢𝖪 \mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK} with such complexity that unifies and extends these results. For 𝖭𝖤𝖮 𝟤 ​ [ 𝖥𝖱𝖾𝖼 ] + 𝖪 \mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+K} , the fragment of the above logic that does not use acyclicity and connectivity constraints, we get a strengthening of this result, where the space complexity is reduced to 𝒪 ⁡ ( 𝗍𝖽 ​ log ⁡ ( n ) ) \mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}\log(n)) . With a similar mechanism as the distance neighborhood logic introduced in [Bergougnoux, Dreier and Jaffke, SODA 2023 ], the logic 𝖭𝖤𝖮 𝟤 ​ [ 𝖥𝖱𝖾𝖼 ] + 𝖠𝖢𝖪 \mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK} is an extension of the fully-existential 𝖬𝖲𝖮 𝟤 \mathsf{MSO_{2}} with predicates for (1) querying generalizations of the neighborhoods of vertex sets, (2) verifying the connectivity and acyclicity of vertex and edge sets, and (3) verifying that a vertex set induces a clique. Interestingly, 𝖭𝖤𝖮 𝟤 ​ [ 𝖥𝖱𝖾𝖼 ] \mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}] , the fragment of 𝖭𝖤𝖮 𝟤 ​ [ 𝖥𝖱𝖾𝖼 ] + 𝖪 \mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+K} that does not use clique constraints, is equivalent (up to minor features) to a variant of modal logic—introduced in [Pilipczuk, MFCS 2011 ]—that captures many problems known to be tractable in single exponential time when parameterized by treewidth. Our results provide 2 𝒪 ⁡ ( 𝗍𝖽 ) ​ n 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) 2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)} time and n 𝒪 ⁡ ( 1 ) n^{\mathcal{O}(1)} space algorithms for problems for which the existence of such algorithms was previously unknown. In particular, 𝖭𝖤𝖮 𝟤 ​ [ 𝖥𝖱𝖾𝖼 ] \mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}] captures CNF-SAT via the incidence graphs associated to CNF formulas, and it also captures several modulo counting problems like Odd Dominating Set . To prove our results, we extend the applicability of algebraic transforms such as the inclusion-exclusion principle and the discrete Fourier transform. To our knowledge, this is the first time, the discrete Fourier transform have been used to obtain space-efficient algorithms on graphs of bounded treedepth. To achieve the logspace complexity for 𝖭𝖤𝖮 𝟤 ​ [ 𝖥𝖱𝖾𝖼 ] + 𝖪 \mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+K} , we also use the technique from [Pilipczuk and Wrochna, ACM Trans. Comput. Theory 2018 ] based on Chinese remainder theorem.



1
Introduction

Many NP-hard problems—like
Independent Set
and
Hamiltonian Cycle
—are tractable in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{tw})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
with
\mathsf{tw}
and
n
being the treewidth and the number of vertices of the input graph, respectively (see
[
8
,
41
,
40
,
19
,
17
,
35
]
for examples).
However, the space usage of these algorithms is exponential in the width of the decomposition.
In practical applications, this is often a prohibitive factor, because a machine
is likely to simply run out of space much before the elapsed time exceeds tolerable limits.
Therefore, a natural question arises: Can we reduce the space complexity of
these algorithms while keeping their time complexity?
Lokshtanov, Mnich, and Saurabh
[
29
]

sketched that many problems admitting a
2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{tw})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time algorithm can be solved in polynomial space and time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{tw}^{2})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
Unfortunately, some complexity-theoretical evidences
[
1
,
37
]
suggest that this increase in the running time might be inevitable, and problems like
Independent Set
are probably not solvable by any algorithm using
2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{tw})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time and
2^{o(\mathsf{tw})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
space.

Polynomial space and treedepth.

This reduction of space complexity is possible when we consider
treedepth
: a shallow restriction of treewidth. Formally, the treedepth of a graph
G
is the minimum depth of a rooted forest
F
—called an
elimination forest
of
G
—in which
G
can be embedded by bijectively mapping the vertices of
G
to the nodes of
F
such that the endpoints of every edge of
G
are in an ancestor-descendant relation in
F
.
This parameter was introduced in
[
33
]
by Nešetřil and Ossona de Mendez; see e.g.
[
34
, Chapter 6]
for a general discussion on the notion of treedepth.
The treedepth of a graph is never smaller than its treewidth, but it is also never larger than its
treewidth times
\log n

[
9
]
where
n
denotes the number of vertices in
G
.
Several NP-hard problems such as
Independent Set
or
Hamiltonian Cycle
can be solved in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and polynomial space (i.e.,
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
) where
\mathsf{td}
is the treedepth of the input graph, assuming that we are given a decomposition certifying this value of
\mathsf{td}
, i.e., an elimination tree of
G
of depth
\mathsf{td}

[
22
,
27
,
32
,
37
]
.
In certain cases, the space complexity can even be as low as
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}+\log n)
or
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}\log n)

[
37
]
.
Typically, the main idea is to reformulate the classic bottom-up dynamic programming approach so that it can be replaced by a simple top-down recursion. This reformulation is by no means easy—it often involves a highly non-trivial use of algebraic transforms or other tools of algebraic flavor, such as inclusion-exclusion branching.
These algorithmic applications of treedepth lead naturally to the following question:

What families of problems can be solved in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and polynomial space, assuming that an elimination forest of depth
\mathsf{td}
is provided with input?

We provide a wide family of such problems
by introducing a logic called
neighborhood operator logic with acyclicity
(
\mathsf{A}
),
connectivity
(
\mathsf{C}
), and
clique
(
\mathsf{K}
)
constraints
(
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
for short) and we show that the model checking problem, for every fixed
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
formula, can be solved in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and polynomial space, provided an elimination tree of depth
\mathsf{td}
is part of the input.
For
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+K}
, i.e., the same logic without the acyclicity and connectivity constraints, we can further reduce the space complexity of our model checking algorithm to
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}\log n)
.

Before proceeding to the description of our logic and a more detailed discussion about our results, let us mention here that the assumption of having an elimination tree as part of the input is an essential part of this question.
To our best knowledge, the current state-of-the-art in polynomial space algorithms for computing an elimination tree of depth
\mathsf{td}
of a graph of treedepth
\mathsf{td}
is the one of Nadara, Pilipczuk, and Smulewicz
[
31
]
, which runs in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}^{2})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
(and polynomial space).
Recently, Bonnet, Neuen, and Sokolowski
[
10
]
showed that the 1.0003-approximation of treedepth is NP-hard.
And it is a major open question whether one can obtain a constant-factor approximation of treedepth in time
2^{o(\mathsf{td}^{2})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

Neighborhood operator logics.

The logic
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
extends fully-existential
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
with the additional ability to directly reason about the generalized neighborhoods of sets.
The central building block in our neighborhood logic is the following neighborhood operator:

Definition 1.1
.

Given a graph
G
,
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{N}
,
U\subseteq V(G)
and
F\subseteq E(G)
, we define the
(\sigma,F)
-neighborhood
of
U
in
G
as

N^{\sigma}(U,F)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigl\{v\in V(G)\,:\,\lvert\{u\in U\,:\,uv\in F\}\rvert\in\sigma\bigr\}.

Moreover, we define the
\sigma
-neighborhood of
U
, denoted by
N^{\sigma}(U)
, as the set
N^{\sigma}(U,E(G))
.

Observe that
N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(U)
with
\mathbb{N}^{+}=\{1,2,3,\dots\}
corresponds to the usual open neighborhood of a set of vertices and
N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(U,F)
corresponds to the open neighborhood of
U
in the graph
(V(F),F)
.
In this paper, we deal with neighborhood operators
N^{\sigma}(U,F)
where
\sigma
is
finitely recognizable
:

Definition 1.2
.

A subset
\sigma
of
\mathbb{N}
is
finitely recognizable
if there exist integers
d,p\in\mathbb{N}
with
p>0
such that for every
n\geqslant d
, we have

n\in\sigma\text{ if and only if }n+p\in\sigma.

(1)

We denote by
p(\sigma)
and
d(\sigma)
the smallest
p
and
d
satisfying (
1
), respectively. Moreover, we call
p(\sigma)
the
period
of
\sigma
.

We use this neighborhood operator to define so-called
neighborhood terms
.
These are built from vertex set variables, vertex set constants (i.e., unary relations) or other neighborhood terms by applying neighborhood operators of form
N^{\sigma}(\cdot)
as well as standard set operators such as intersection (
\cap
), union (
\cup)
, subtraction (
\setminus
) or complementation (denoted by a bar on top of the term).
The second arguments of our neighborhood operators are
edge set terms
, those are built from edge set variables and edge set constants (i.e., binary relations)
by applying the above-mentioned standard set operators.
Then
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
is the extension of the fully-existential
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
by allowing the following:

•

Size measurement of terms
: We can write, for example,
|t|\leq m

to express that a neighborhood term or an edge set term
t
should have size at most
m
.

•

Comparison between terms
: We can write, for example,
t_{1}=t_{2}
or
t_{1}\subseteq t_{2}
to express that
t_{1}
should be equal or contained, respectively, in
t_{2}
where
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are two neighborhood terms or two edge set terms.

We define
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+K}
as the extension of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
with predicates
\mathsf{clique}(t)
that verify that a neighborhood term
t
induces a clique.
Furthermore, we define
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
as the extension of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+K}
with predicates
\mathsf{con}(t)
and
\mathsf{acy}(t)
that verify the connectivity and acyclicity, respectively, of a neighborhood term or an edge set term
t
.
We refer to
Section

4
for the formal definition of this logic.
Let us give some example of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
formulas that capture some well-known problems.
The very simple formula

\mathbf{independent}(X)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(X)\cap X=\emptyset

expresses that no vertex from
X
intersects the neighborhood of
X
, i.e., that
X
forms an independent set.
Thus, the
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
formula
\exists X\ |X|\geq t\land\mathbf{independent}(X)

expresses the existence of an independent set of size at least
t
.
Similarly, the formula

\exists Y\ |Y|\leq t\land\mathbf{independent}(\mathtt{V}\setminus N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(\mathtt{V},Y))

(where
\mathtt{V}
stands for the whole vertex set) expresses the existence of an edge dominating set of size at most
t
, where
V\setminus N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(\mathtt{V},Y)
is the set of vertices that are not incident to an edge of
Y
(where
V
denotes the set of all vertices).
With
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
, we can trivially express the
Clique
problem. Moreover, we can express connectivity problems, for example, for
Hamiltonian Cycle
we can use the formula

\exists Y\ \mathsf{con}(Y)\land N^{\{2\}}(\mathtt{V},Y)=\mathtt{V},

where
N^{\{2\}}(\mathtt{V},Y)=\mathtt{V}
expresses the property that every vertex of the graph is incident to exactly two edges of
Y
.
For
Max Cut
to express the existence of a cut of size at least
k
, we can use the formula:

\exists Y\exists A\exists B\ A\cup B=\mathtt{V}\land A\cap B=\emptyset\land N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(A,Y)\subseteq B\land N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(B,Y)\subseteq A\land|Y|\geqslant k.

For any fixed integer
r
we can also express the existence of an induced
r
-regular subgraph with at least
k
vertices using the following formula:

\exists X\exists Y\ N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(\mathtt{V},Y)=X\land N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(X,\mathtt{E}\setminus Y)=\emptyset\land N^{\{r\}}(X,Y)=X\land|X|\geqslant k.

Here,
X
and
Y
are the vertex and the edge set of the sought subgraph, respectively.
We first ensure that the set of the endpoints of the edges in
Y
is precisely
X
, then that all edges induced by
X
belong to
Y
, and finally that the graph
(X,Y)
is
r
-regular and contains at least
k
vertices.

Observe that finitely recognizable sets allow us to express problems based on modulo counting such as the ones defined in
[
3
,
24
]
such as
Odd Dominating Set
, or more generally the existence of a
(\sigma,\rho)
-set
[
39
]
where
\sigma
and
\rho
are finitely recognizable:

\exists X\ X\subseteq N^{\sigma}(X)\land\overline{X}\subseteq N^{\rho}(X).

And even more generally, we can model a generalization of this problem called
(\sigma,\rho)
-
MinParDomSet
introduced by Greilhuber and Marx
[
23
]
(they only consider it for (co)finite sets
\sigma
and
\rho
) where one allows a set of at most
k
vertices to violate the
(\sigma,\rho)
-constraints, i.e.,

\exists X\ \lvert(X\setminus N^{\sigma}(X))\cup(\bar{X}\setminus N^{\rho}(X))\rvert\leqslant k.

In
Section

4.1
, we provide more examples of problems captures by our logics:
locally checkable partitioning
problem,
CNF-SAT
and
Max-CNF-SAT
(via the incidence graph of a formula).

Our results.

In order to describe the running time of our model checking algorithms, we need to introduce the following notation. Given a
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
formula
\varphi
, we define
d(\varphi)
as the maximum of
d(\sigma)

over all sets
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{N}
that occur in
\varphi
, and we define the value
p_{\varphi}
as the least common multiple of
p(\sigma)
over the sets
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{N}
that occur in
\varphi
.
First, we show that given a
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+K}
formula, the model checking problem can be solved in single-exponential time and logarithmic space.
Formally, we prove the following theorem:

Theorem 1.3
.

There is an algorithm that given a formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+K}
with
\ell
size measurements, a graph
G
and an elimination tree
T
of
G
of depth at most
\mathsf{td}
, runs in time
((d_{\varphi}+1)\cdot p_{\varphi})^{\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|\cdot\mathsf{td})}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(\ell)}
and space
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}\cdot|\varphi|\cdot(\log{d_{\varphi}}+\log{p_{\varphi}}+\log n+\log{|\varphi|}))
and decides whether
G\vDash\varphi
holds.

When the formula contains acyclicity and connectivity constraints, we prove the existence of the following model checking algorithm:

Theorem 1.4
.

There is a Monte-Carlo algorithm that given a formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
with
\ell
size measurements and
q
acyclicity constraints,
a graph
G
and an elimination tree
T
of
G
of depth at most
\mathsf{td}
, runs in time
((d_{\varphi}+1)\cdot p_{\varphi})^{\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|\cdot\mathsf{td})}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(q+\ell)}
and space polynomial in
n+\lvert\varphi\rvert+d_{\varphi}+p_{\varphi}
and decides whether
G\vDash\varphi
holds.
The algorithm cannot give false positives and may give false negatives with probability at most
1/2
.

Let us remark that to handle connectivity and acyclicity constraints we rely on the Cut&Count technique by Cygan et al.
[
17
]
.
This technique works under the assumption that a solution (if it exists) is unique.
The standard way of ensuring this is the classic Isolation lemma
[
30
]
: one samples the weights in a certain probabilstic way so that, with high probability, the solution becomes unique.
The application of Isolation lemma is the source of both (1) randomization and (2) the increase in space complexity as storing the sampled weights requires polynomial space.
As soon as the weights are fixed, our algorithm is deterministic and runs in logarithmic space similarly to
theorem

1.3
.

Another aspect we would like to remark is the dependency on the input formula
\varphi
in the exponent of
n
.
As stated in both theorems, the exponents of
n
depend on
\ell
or
\ell+q
where
\ell
denotes the number of size measurements and
q
the number of acyclicity constraints in the formula
\varphi
.
In all natural problems captured by our logic, the numbers of size measurements and acyclicity constraints are bounded by very small constants, like 1 or 2, and therefore, the degree of
n
is independent of the natural problem we solve.

Algorithmic meta-theorems and related logics.

We would like to point out that our main result reads as an
algorithmic meta-theorem
, i.e., a general statement of the form “All problems with property
P
, restricted to a class of inputs
I
, can be solved efficiently”.
The archetypal, and possibly most celebrated algorithmic meta-theorem is commonly known as
Courcelle’s theorem

[
13
,
15
,
14
]
(see also
[
11
,
2
]
) which
states that all problems expressible in
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
logic are linear time solvable
on graphs of bounded treewidth.

Designing and proving algorithmic meta-theorems is a very active area of research bringing close topics related to logic, combinatorics (especially graphs), and algorithms; see
[
28
,
25
,
26
,
36
,
38
]
for related surveys.
A remarkable feature of our meta-theorem is that it has a
quite moderate run time dependence on the length of the input formula (assuming a constant number of size measurements).
In stark contrast,
the run time of model checking algorithms for
\mathsf{FO}
and
\mathsf{MSO}
often heavily depends on the formula length and the width parameter.
Even non-elementary tower functions
2^{2^{\cdot^{{}_{\cdot^{{}_{\cdot}}}}}}
,
where the height depends on the length of the formula,
are quite common and necessary
[
21
]
.
The efficiency of our model checking algorithm is shared with the model checking algorithm for the
existential counting modal logic
(
\mathsf{ECML}
for short)—introduced by Pilipczuk
[
35
]
—and the model checking algorithm for the
distance neighborhood logic
(
\mathsf{DN}
for short)—introduced by Bergougnoux, Dreier and Jaffke
[
6
]
—whose model checking problem admits a single-exponential algorithm for treewidth and mim-width, respectively (both model checking algorithms can handle connectivity and acyclicity constraints).

In fact,
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
,
\mathsf{DN}
and
\mathsf{ECML}
are closely related, let us elaborate on that.
Our logic
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
relies on the same neighborhood operators mechanism as
\mathsf{DN}
introduced by Bergougnoux, Dreier and Jaffke
[
6
]
.
Let
\mathsf{NEO_{1}}[\mathsf{FcoF}]
be the restriction of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
without edge set terms and where every set
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{N}
is finite or co-finite (i.e., they forbid the “periodicity”). Then,
\mathsf{DN}
is equivalent to the extension of
\mathsf{NEO_{1}}[\mathsf{FcoF}]
with neighborhood operators
N^{\sigma}_{r}(U)
with
r\in\mathbb{N}
that evaluate as the
\sigma
-neighborhood of
U
in the
r
-th power of
G
.

In
[
6
]
, the authors provide a model checking algorithm for
\mathsf{DN}
running in time
n^{\mathcal{O}(d\cdot w\cdot\lvert\varphi\rvert^{2})}
where
\lvert\varphi\rvert
is the size of the input formula,
d
is the maximum
d(\sigma)
over the sets
\sigma
that occur in
\varphi
, and
w
is the mim-width of a given decomposition.
The running time of their model checking algorithm is also upper bounded by efficient FPT functions
in terms of the treewidth, cliquewidth, and rankwidth of the given decomposition.
1
1

1

Assuming that the input formula has a constant number of size measurements (e.g.,
t\leqslant m
) involving an non-constant integer (e.g.,
m
).

On the other hand,
\mathsf{ECML}
is variant of modal logic which captures a good number of problems known to be tractable in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{tw})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
where
\mathsf{tw}
is the treewidth of the input graph.
The evaluation of formulas in modal logic is tied to an active vertex that changes over time and is not explicitly represented by a variable of the formula.
Roughly, an
\mathsf{ECML}
formula is of the form
\exists\bar{X}\ \xi\land\forall v\ \psi
where:

•

\exists\bar{X}
is a sequence of existentially quantified vertex set and edge sets variables,

•

\xi
an arbitrary quantifier-free arithmetic formula over the parameters and the cardinalities of fixed and quantified sets,

•

\forall v
is simply a way of initializing the active vertex, and

•

\psi
is a quantifier-free
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
formula where we can use the central operators of
\mathsf{ECML}
that are
\square^{\sigma}
and
\diamondsuit^{\sigma}
with
\sigma
a finitely recognizable set.

When the active vertex is
v
,
a formula
\square^{\sigma}\varphi
shall be read as “the number of neighbors of
w
that satisfy
\psi
as an active vertex belongs to
\sigma
”,
while
\diamondsuit^{\sigma}\varphi
is equivalent to
\neg\square^{\sigma}\neg\psi
.

Somewhat surprisingly, the authors of
[
6
]
prove that
\mathsf{ECML}
and
\mathsf{DN}
are semantically close in the sense that the operators
\square^{\sigma}
and
\diamondsuit^{\sigma}
are equivalent to our neighborhood operator
N^{\sigma}(\cdot)
. In fact, their arguments prove that
\mathsf{ECML}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
(assuming that the arithmetic formulas are restricted to what we can do with our size measurements).
Nonetheless, we introduce
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
because the neighborhood operators is a simpler mechanism to deal with in both the design of the model checking algorithm and expression of the problems.

Comparing our logic to
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
and the case of universal quantifiers.

Let us briefly mention here how the expressive power of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
compares to the expressive power of
\mathsf{FO}
,
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
, and
\mathsf{CMSO_{2}}
; the latter being the enhancement of
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
with modular counting predicates (i.e., predicates expressing that for a given set variable
X
and a fixed integer
p>1
,
|X|\equiv 0\mod p
).
Note that every formula
\varphi
of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
can be rewritten to an equivalent formula
\varphi^{\prime}
of
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
but this transformation comes with a size blow-up that depends on the maximum of values
d(\sigma)
, over all
\sigma
appearing in
\varphi
.
Moreover, each size measurement
\lvert X\rvert\leqslant m
can also be expressed in
\mathsf{CMSO}_{2}
, but the size blow-up will depend on
m
which can be prohibitively big, e.g.,
O(n)
.
From another viewpoint, while in bounded treedepth graphs the expressive power of
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
and
\mathsf{FO}
coincide
[
18
]
, it is unclear to which variant of
\mathsf{FO}
our logic
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
maps to.
Also, we would like to stress that
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
is an extension of fully-existential
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
that allows limited use of universal quantifiers, “hidden” in the neighborhood operators, the size measurements, and the comparison between terms. The extension of the logic with acyclicity, connectivity and clique constraints, captures even more problems and all these extra features allow further use of universal quantifiers in restricted forms.
This extension, while allowing to encode more problems and get further algorithmic results, also investigates the potential of our techniques to the regime of universal quantification.

Organization of the paper

In
section

2
we present the main ideas behind our algorithm on a concrete formula.
Then in
section

3
we introduce notions and present some algebraic results used in the paper.
In
section

4
we formally define our logic, provide an equivalent core fragment of this logic on which we focus in the remainder, and present some problems expressible in the logic.
In
section

5
we define the partial solutions, indices and polynomials on which our algorithm relies on.
In
section

6
we present the recursive equalities and in
section

7
we put them together to obtain our algorithm for
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
.
After that in
section

8
we explain how to extend the algorithm to handle clique, acyclicity, and connectivity constraints.
Finally, in
section

9
we conclude with some open questions.

2
Technical Overview

In this section, we briefly present our logic as well as the main techniques behind our model-checking algorithm.
We first explain how our algorithm works with a concrete formula, and then, we briefly discuss what changes in the general case.
So fix a graph
G
on
n
vertices and an elimination tree
T
of
G
of depth
\mathsf{td}
and the following formula

\varphi=\exists Y\ N^{\sigma}(\mathtt{V},Y)=\mathtt{V}\land|Y|\leqslant k

where
\sigma
is the set

\sigma=\{3\}\cup\{i\,:\,i\geqslant 4\text{ and }i\text{ is even}\}.

By
definition

1.1
, the set
N^{\sigma}(\mathtt{V},Y)
with
\mathtt{V}=V(G)
is the set of all vertices
v
in
G
such that the number of edges in
Y
incident with
v
is at least
4
and even, or it is precisely
3
.
So the property
N^{\sigma}(\mathtt{V},Y)=\mathtt{V}
states that for every vertex
v
in
G
, the number of incident edges from
Y
is at least
4
and even, or it is precisely
3
.
Finally,
|Y|\leqslant k
bounds the size of
Y
by
k
.

According to
definition

1.2
we have
d(\sigma)=4
and
p(\sigma)=2
. Observe that, for any integer
i\geqslant 4
, whether
i
belongs to
\sigma
depends solely on its parity, i.e., rest modulo
2
.
The definition of
\sigma
implies that for every vertex
v
, two values are relevant in order to know whether
v\in N^{\sigma}(\mathtt{V},Y)
.
First, we are interested in the number of incident edges in
Y

counting up to
d(\sigma)
= 4
, i.e., whether
v
has
precisely

i
incident edges for some value
0\leqslant i\leqslant d(\sigma)-1=3
, or it has
at least

d(\sigma)=4
such edges.
We will use the so-called
neighborhood functions
for this, such a function maps every vertex to a value between
0
and
d(\sigma)=4
.
Second, it is useful to know the
parity
of the number of edges in
Y
incident to
v
.
We will use the so-called
mod-degrees
for this, such a function maps every vertex to a parity from
\mathbb{F}_{p(\sigma)}=\{0,1\}
.

Our goal is to develop a space- and time-efficient top-down recursion along
T
to check whether a set
Y
with desired properties exists.
Each recursive call of our algorithm is associated with a node
u
of
T
, a set of edges
\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
and two sets of vertices: a
tail
and a
subtree
.
The edge set of
G
is partitioned into certain sets
\mathsf{sheaf}(w)
among the leaves
w
of
T
in such a way that both end-points of every edge in
\mathsf{sheaf}(w)
belong to the path from
w
to the root (see
Section

3.2
for a formal definition).
Then,
\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
is defined as the union of
\mathsf{sheaf}(w)
over the leaves
w
of
T
in
\mathsf{subtree}[u]
.
The subtree of a recursive call represents the set of already
processed
vertices. On the other hand, the vertices of the tail represent
active
vertices that may be incident to
still unprocessed
edges outside
\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
. With a node
u
, two types of recursive calls are associated, namely when
u
is treated as processed and when it is not processed.
In the former case, the subtree of a recursive call associated with
u
is
\mathsf{subtree}[u]
, namely the set of descendants of
u
in
T
including
u
, and it is
\mathsf{subtree}(u)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{subtree}[u]\setminus\{u\}
in the latter case. Similarly, its tail is
\mathsf{tail}(u)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{tail}[u]\setminus\{u\}
, namely the set of proper ancestors of
u
in
T
, or
\mathsf{tail}[u]
, respectively.

Every recursive call takes as input an
index
that stores the information about the tail, and each index is
compatible
with a set of
partial solutions
.
We will gradually present the components of an index and a compatible partial solution in the context of a recursive call associated with a vertex
u
of
T
, a tail
\mathsf{tail}
and a subtree
\mathsf{subtree}
.
The first component of a partial solution is a set of edges
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)\subseteq\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
corresponding to a partial interpretation of the edge set variable
Y
.

Let us explain why an index
I
cannot simply use a function
h\colon\mathsf{tail}\to\{0,1,2,3,4\}

to keep track of the number (up to
d(\sigma)=4
) of edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
incident to each vertex of
\mathsf{tail}
.
If
u
is an internal node of
T
with children
v_{1},\dots,v_{t}
, then
\mathsf{sheaf}(v_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{sheaf}(v_{t})
is a partition of
\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
, and for each child
v_{i}
of
u
and each
v\in\mathsf{tail}
, there are
d(\sigma)+1
options for the number (up to
d(\sigma)=4
) of edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)\cap\mathsf{sheaf}(v_{i})
incident to
v
.
In this case, the recursive call associated with
I
would require to consider
(d(\sigma)+1)^{|\mathsf{tail}|\cdot t}
combinations of indexes of the children of
u
.
Since
t
can be in
\Theta(n)
, this is a prohibitively large for the running time we aim at.
One might, instead, process the children of
u
one after another but then keeping track of all indexes of the children processed so far requires
\mathcal{O}((d(\sigma)+1)^{\mathsf{td}})
space.

So we have to proceed differently in order to control the number of edges in
Y
incident to each vertex. For this, the second component of a partial solution is a neighborhood function
\hat{\mathbb{E}}\colon\mathsf{tail}\cup\mathsf{subtree}\to\{0,\dots,4\}
, and each value
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)
is an
expectation
on the number of edges of
Y
incident to
v
when the whole graph is processed, and not among the edges processed so far.
For this reason, the value
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)
remains fixed during subsequent recursive calls.
And when the whole graph is processed, our algorithm ensures that we
filtered out
(via counting tricks) all partial solutions where there exists at least one vertex
v\in V(G)
with
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)<d(\sigma)
and the expectation
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)
is not equal the number of edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
incident to
v
.
Due to the special meaning of the value
d(\sigma)=4
, for vertices
v
with
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)=d(\sigma)
the filtering only ensures that after the whole graph is processed, there are at least edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
incident to
v
.
Each index has a neighborhood function
\mathbb{E}\colon\mathsf{tail}\to\{0,\dots,4\}
to keep track of the values
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)
on the vertices
v\in\mathsf{tail}
.
Note that, in this concrete example, the domains of
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
and
\mathbb{E}
could be restricted to
\{3,4\}
because every vertex needs to be incident to exactly 3 or at least 4 edges in
Y
but we stick to the set
\{0,\dots,4\}
to remain consistent with our algorithm.

We first focus on ensuring that each
v\in\mathsf{tail}
has at least
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)
incident edges when
v
is processed. For this, we use an auxiliary graph in which every vertex
w
of
G
is replaced by
d(\sigma)=4
copies
w^{1},\dots,w^{4}
of itself.
Then if we choose, in a certain way, for every edge from
Y
exactly one copy of this edge in the auxiliary graph (such choices will be parts of our
partial solutions
), then the fact that
v
has at least
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)
incident edges is equivalent to the fact that the first
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)
copies of
v
are incident to at least one edge in this auxiliary graph.
The following idea is based on
inclusion-exclusion
and generalizes a technique used in
[
32
]
for
Hamiltonian Cycle
.
To obtain the number of
partial solutions
in which the first
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)
copies of
v
are incident to at least one edge:

•

We first take the number of partial solutions where all of the first
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)
copies of
v
are
allowed
to have incident edges.

•

Then we subtract, for each
j\in\{1,\dots,\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)\}
, the number of partial solutions where the
j
-th copy of
v
is
forbidden
to have incident edges.
Observe that we subtracted the partial solutions that
forbid
edges incident with, say, the first and the second copy of
v
at least twice.

•

So now for each pair
j_{1}\neq j_{2}\in\{1,\dots,\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)\}
, we add back the number of partial solutions where both the
j_{1}
-th and the
j_{2}
-th copy are
forbidden
to have incident edges.

•

The process is repeated for every possible choice of
not allowed
copies.

This motivates the second part of the index, namely, a neighborhood function
\mathtt{alw}\colon\mathsf{tail}\to\{0,\dots,4\}
which determines which copies of the vertices in
\mathsf{tail}
are
allowed
to have incident edges.
Finally, as already motivated above, the third component of the index is a mod-degree
\mathtt{pd}\colon\mathsf{tail}\to\mathbb{F}_{p(\sigma)}=\{0,1\}
.
Unlike the
expectation

\mathbb{E}
, the value
\mathtt{pd}(u)
is the
real
parity of the number of edges in
Y
processed so far and incident with
u
, we will see below that an algebraic discrete Fourier transform allows us to handle mod-degrees efficiently.
Altogether, an
index
for
\mathsf{tail}
is a tuple
(\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd})
where
\mathbb{E}
and
\mathtt{alw}
are neighborhood functions and
\mathtt{pd}
is a mod-degree on
\mathsf{tail}
.

Now we are ready to formally define partial solutions as well as the notion of
compatibility
between partial solutions and indexes.
A
partial solution
for
\mathsf{tail}(u)
is a triple
(\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y),\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
where

•

\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
is a subset of edges from
\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
.

•

\hat{\mathbb{E}}
is a neighborhood function on
\mathsf{tail}(u)\cup\mathsf{subtree}[u]
.

•

\vec{\mathcal{W}}
is a set of edges of the auxiliary graph such that for every pair
\{v,w\}
of distinct vertices of
G
, there exists a pair
v^{i}w^{j}\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}
if and only if we have
vw\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
.
Furthermore, we require that for every pair
\{v,w\}
, there exists at most one pair
v^{i}w^{j}\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}
.
Informally speaking,
\vec{\mathcal{W}}

assigns
every edge from
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
to precisely one edge between the copies of its endpoints.

•

Additionally, we require the following for every
processed
vertex
v\in\mathsf{subtree}[u]
.
First,
v
has at least
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)
incident edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
—this ensures that the
expectation

\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)
is a lower bound on the number of incident edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
. Secondly, either
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)=3
, or
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)=4
and
v
has an even number of incident edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
—i.e., assuming that the expectation is correct, the vertex
v
has a number of incident edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
from
\sigma
as desired.

Let now
I\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd})
be an index of
\mathsf{tail}(u)
. We say that the partial solution
P\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y),\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
of
u
is compatible with
I
if the following properties are satisfied:

•

\hat{\mathbb{E}}
and
\mathbb{E}
are equal on
\mathsf{tail}(u)
.

•

For every
v\in\mathsf{tail}(u)
and every edge
v^{i}w^{j}\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}
, we have
i\leqslant\mathtt{alw}(v)
, i.e., there are only edges incident to
allowed
copies.

•

For every vertex
v\in\mathsf{tail}(u)
, the number
\mathtt{pd}(v)
is equal, modulo
p(\sigma)=2
, to the total number of edges in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
incident to the copies of
v
. Observe that by definition of a partial solution, this is simply the parity of the number of edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
incident to
v
.

To keep track of the size of
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
, we define the value
c_{u}(P)=|\vec{\mathcal{W}}|
.
Furthermore, we define two following values:

a_{u}(P)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathsf{subtree}[u]\colon\\
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)<d(\sigma)=4\end{subarray}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)\text{ and }b_{u}(P)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathsf{tail}(u)\cup\mathsf{subtree}[u]\colon\\
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)<d(\sigma)=4\end{subarray}}\lvert\{\{v^{i},w^{j}\}\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}\}\rvert.

The following observation is crucial.
The definition of a partial solution ensures that every vertex in
\mathsf{subtree}[u]
has at least
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(u)
incident edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
.
Also recall that the edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
are in bijection with the edges in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
, and the bijection maps the endpoints of every edge to their copies.
Therefore, we always have
a_{u}(P)\leqslant b_{u}(P)
.
Furthermore, the equality is achieved precisely if
every vertex
v\in\mathsf{subtree}[u]
with
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)<d(\sigma)=4
has
exactly

\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v)
incident edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
.
Therefore, keeping track of the values
a_{u}(P)
and
b_{u}(P)
permits us to keep the partial solutions for which
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
correctly guesses the number of incident edges (except for the special value
d(\sigma)=4
for which we only ensure the existence of at least
d(\sigma)=4
incident edges).
We now define the polynomial

\mathcal{P}_{u}(I)=\sum_{P}\alpha^{a_{u}(P)}\beta^{b_{u}(P)}\gamma^{c_{u}(P)}

where the sum is over all partial solutions of
\mathsf{tail}(u)
compatible with
I
.
Crucially, computing the polynomial
\mathcal{P}_{r}(\emptyset)
for the root
r
of
T
suffices to solve the problem.
Indeed, we have
G\models\varphi
if and only if there is a monomial
q\cdot\alpha^{i}\beta^{i}\gamma^{j}
in
\mathcal{P}_{r}(\emptyset)
with
j\leqslant k
and such that for the coefficient
q
we have
q\neq 0
.
Here choosing the same exponent of
\alpha
and
\beta
ensures, as argued above, that the
expectations
made by
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
reflect the correct number of incident edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
when it is strictly smaller than
d(\sigma)
.
And the exponent of
\gamma
reflects the cardinality of
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
so we only allow the sets of size
j\leqslant k
.

We can similarly define partial solutions and indexes for
\mathsf{tail}(u)
by replacing
\mathsf{tail}(u)
by
\mathsf{tail}[u]
and
\mathsf{subtree}[u]
by
\mathsf{subtree}(u)
, respectively, in the definitions above, we then denote the corresponding polynomials by
\mathcal{P}_{u}[\cdot]
.

Let us now explain the recursive computation of this root polynomial.
The following equality holds for every (non-leaf) node
u

\mathcal{P}_{u}[I]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathtt{pd}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{pd}_{t}\colon\mathsf{tail}[u]\to\{0,1\}\colon\\
\mathtt{pd}_{1}+\dots+\mathtt{pd}_{t}\equiv_{2}\mathtt{pd}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathcal{P}_{v_{i}}(\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd}_{i})

if
v_{1},\dots,v_{t}
denote the children of
u
in the elimination tree
T
.
Vaguely speaking, this holds mainly for the following reasons:
(1) the sets
\mathsf{subtree}[v_{1}],\dots,\mathsf{subtree}[v_{t}]
partition the set
\mathsf{subtree}(u)
,
(2) the sets
\mathsf{sheaf}(v_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{sheaf}(v_{t})
partition the set
\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
, (3) to obtain degree (modulo
2
) of a certain vertex in
\mathsf{tail}[u]
in the edge set
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
, we can simply sum up its degrees restricted to the edges sets
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)\cap\mathsf{sheaf}(v_{1}),\dots,\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)\cap\mathsf{sheaf}(v_{t})
, and
(4) when multiplying the polynomials, their degrees are added so that the values
a_{u}[\cdot]
,
b_{u}[\cdot]
, and
c_{u}[\cdot]
are computed correctly.
The above formula has the problem that it requires to branch over
2^{|\mathsf{tail}[u]|\cdot t}
options with
t
possibly in
\Theta(n)
.
But we can show that for the discrete Fourier transforms
\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{u}[\cdot]
and
\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{v_{i}}(\cdot)
of
\mathcal{P}_{u}[\cdot]
and
\mathcal{P}_{v_{i}}(\cdot)
, respectively, (see
Section

3.3
and
Definition

6.3
for a formal definition) we have

\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{u}[I]\equiv_{p}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{v_{i}}(I)

for a prime number
p
satisfying certain technical preconditions.
For this reason, our algorithm works with discrete Fourier transforms and thus quickly combines the children of
u
without any branching.

To compute the polynomial
\mathcal{P}_{u}(I)
from some polynomials of form
\mathcal{P}_{u}[\cdot]
, we use the following equality:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{u}(\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd})=

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leqslant i\leqslant 3}S_{\pm}(3,i)\cdot\mathcal{P}_{u}[\mathbb{E}[u\mapsto 3],\mathtt{alw}[u\mapsto i],\mathtt{pd}[u\mapsto 1]]\cdot\alpha^{3}+

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leqslant i\leqslant 4}S_{\pm}(4,i)\cdot\mathcal{P}_{u}[\mathbb{E}[u\mapsto 4],\mathtt{alw}[u\mapsto i],\mathtt{pd}[u\mapsto 0]].

Let us sketch what all of those values are and why the formula is correct.
For an integer
i
and a function
f\in\{\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd}\}
, the function
f[u\mapsto i]
has the domain
\mathsf{tail}[u]
, agrees with
f
on
\mathsf{tail}(u)
, and maps
u
to
i
. Since the vertex
u
moves to the
processed
part, by definition of a partial solution we want to ensure that either (1)
u
has precisely
j=3
incident edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
, in particular this number is odd, (this corresponds to the first summand); or
(2)
u
has at least
j=4
incident edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
and this number is even (this corresponds to the second summand).
And in both cases we need to ensure that
u
has at least
j
incident edges in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
, i.e., each of the
j
copies of
u
is incident to a least one edge in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
.
The function
S_{\pm}(j,i)
is the coefficient used to apply the inclusion-exclusion sketched earlier: to ensure that all
j
copies of
u
are incident to an edge in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
, we
allow
edges to be incident to various subsets of the copies of
u
, and properly combine those options via the coefficients
S_{\pm}(k,i)
.
Finally, the first summand is multiplied by
\alpha^{3}
to correctly update the value
a_{u}(\cdot)
, i.e., reflect that
u
now belongs to the subtree and
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(u)=3<4=d(\sigma)
.
In terms of running time, the branching factor of this computation is only constant.

The last missing piece for the recursive algorithm is the non-recursive computation of the polynomial
\mathcal{P}_{u}[I]
for some leaf
u
.
We do not provide the details here due to their technicality but we can show that for the discrete Fourier transformed polynomial
\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{u}[I]
, the computation can be carried out in polynomial time by considering every edge independently and correctly initializing the exponents of
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
depending on whether the edge is taken into
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
.
Altogether, the ideas presented so far suffice to obtain a recursive algorithm with only a constant branching factor and the recursion depth bounded by the depth
\mathsf{td}
of the elimination tree
T
, i.e., with the running time of
2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.
At every level of recursion, we keep track of at most
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
coefficients of polynomials (this follows from the upper-bounds on the degrees of the formal variables
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\gamma
) each of length upper-bounded by
n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
so the space complexity is polynomial.

General case

We now briefly summarize which of the techniques from the paper were not covered by this example.

1.

As done in
[
6
]
, we define a simpler fragment
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
and show that it has the same expressive power. Moreover, we observe that the conversion of any
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
formula to a
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
formula
can be done with only a linear increase of the formula length, increasing the number of size measurements by at most two, and without altering the values
d_{\varphi}
and
p_{\varphi}
. In particular,
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
has no nested neighborhood operators, in fact, all of them are of the form
N^{\sigma}(X,Y)
where
X
and
Y
are a vertex set variable and an edge set variable, respectively.

2.

The neighborhood operators with various sets
\sigma
can occur in the formula. We show that we can deal with all of them by counting the number of neighbors (1) up to
d_{\varphi}
defined as the maximum of all
d(\sigma)
and (2) modulo
p_{\varphi}
where
p_{\varphi}
is defined as the least common multiple of all
p(\sigma)
.
Further, we use “private” functions
\mathbb{E}
,
\mathtt{pd}
,
\mathtt{alw}
for every pair
(X,Y)
such that
N^{\sigma}(X,Y)
occurs in the formula for some
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{N}
.

3.

To keep track of vertex set variables we extend the index by a so-called
vertex interpretation
, usually denoted by
\mathbf{f}
, which stores, for every vertex on the tail, to which vertex set variables it belongs.

4.

Dealing with neighborhood operators where the first operand is a variable creates asymmetries: whether a vertex
u
belongs to
N^{\sigma}(X,Y)
depends only on the edges
uv\in Y
where
v\in X
.
To deal with these asymmetries, the auxiliary graph becomes an auxiliary digraph and
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
becomes a function mapping each pair
(X,Y)
such that
N^{\sigma}(X,Y)
occurs in the formula to a subset of arcs of this digraph.

5.

The formula, in general, might be not in conjunctive normal form (unlike our example), and some of the vertex or edge set equalities might, in general, not hold in a solution. So we extend the index by a subset
\mathtt{F}
of set equalities occurring in the formula, it then determines which equalities are
allowed to be falsified
, and this value remains fixed during the recursive calls.
We then use another inclusion-exclusion to obtain partial solutions where
precisely
the equalities from
\mathtt{F}
are falsified.

6.

The formula might contain multiple size measurements, we introduce one formal variable
\gamma_{i}
per measured variable
X_{i}
.

7.

To decrease the space complexity to logarithmic in
n
, we make use of the technique of
[
37
]
based on the Chinese remainder theorem. This technique reconstructs a polynomial by evaluating it in many points modulo certain primes of length logarithmic in
n
.

8.

To handle connectivity and acyclicity constraints, we employ Cut&Count by Cygan et al.
[
17
]
.

3
Preliminaries

We use the convention that
0
belongs to
\mathbb{N}
and by
\mathbb{N}^{+}
we denote the set
\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
For a non-negative integer
i\in\mathbb{N}
, by
[i]
we denote the set
\{j\in\mathbb{N}^{+}\,:\,j\leqslant i\}
, in particular, it holds that
[0]=\emptyset
.
By
[i]_{0}
we then denote the set
[i]\cup\{0\}
.
We will also use the Iverson bracket notation: for Boolean predicate
p
, the value
[p]
is equal to
1
if
p
is true and equal to zero otherwise.

3.1
Graphs

We consider finite, simple, vertex and edge colored graphs
G
where
V(G)
and
E(G)
denote the vertex and edge set, respectively. We assume that a color is used either on vertices or edges.
For a graph
G
and color class
\mathtt{P}
, we denote the set of elements in
G
with color
\mathtt{P}
by
\mathtt{P}(G)
.
Note that color classes may be empty and that a vertex or an edge may have zero, one, or multiple colors.
We denote by
n
the order of
V(G)
, where the corresponding graph is always clear from the context.
For every vertex set
A\subseteq V(G)
we denote by
\overline{A}
the set
V(G)\setminus A
.

The subgraph of
G

induced
by a subset
A\subseteq V(G)
,
whose vertex set is
A
and whose edge set is
\{uv\in E(G)\,:\,u,v\in A\}
,
is denoted by
G[A]
.

3.2
Elimination trees and treedepth

Let
G=(V,E)
be a graph. A forest
T
is an
elimination forest
of
G
if the vertex set of
T
coincides with
V
and for every edge
uv\in E
, either
u
is an ancestor of
v
, or
v
is an ancestor of
u
in
T
.
We refer to the vertices of
T
as
nodes
.
The
depth
of
T
is the maximum number of vertices on any root-to-leaf path.
And the
treedepth
of
G
is the minimum depth over all elimination forests of
G
.
Every node in a tree of depth at most
d
has at most
d
ancestors so the following well-known property holds.

Observation 3.1
.

If a graph on
n
vertices admits an elimination forest of depth
d
, this graph has at most
d\cdot n
edges.

Along this work, we assume that the elimination forest consists of a single tree (and we then refer to it as
elimination tree
) as we can choose one dedicated root in an elimination forest and attach the roots of the remaining trees as its new children: the arising rooted tree is then elimination tree and its depth is at most one larger than the depth of the forest we started with.
The elimination tree of a graph will be fixed and we will use
\mathsf{td}
to denote its depth.

Let
T
be a elimination tree of a graph
G
. Following the standard terminology (see
[
27
,
32
,
37
]
) for algorithms on elimination trees, we define the following sets for every node
u
of
T
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{subtree}[u]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{v\colon v\text{ is descendant of }u\},

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{subtree}(u)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{subtree}[u]\setminus\{u\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{tail}[u]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{v\colon v\text{ is ancestor of }u\},

\displaystyle\qquad\mathsf{tail}(u)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{tail}[u]\setminus\{u\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{broom}[u]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{tail}[u]\cup\mathsf{subtree}[u].

We remark that we assume the convention that
u
is both an ancestor and a descendant of itself, i.e., both
u\in\mathsf{tail}[u]
and
u\in\mathsf{subtree}[u]
hold.
We fix an arbitrary left-to-right ordering of the children of every node.
Similarly to
[
32
]
we define the following.
For a node
u
by
\mathsf{left}(u)
we denote the leftmost leaf descendant of
u
, that is, the leaf obtained by starting at
u
and iteratively moving to the leftmost child of the current vertex until a leaf is reached.
For a leaf
w
of
T
, we define

\mathsf{sheaf}(w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{ab\,:\,ab\in E(G),a\text{ is an ancestor of }b,\mathsf{left}(b)=w\}

and finally, for a node
u
we define

\mathsf{sheaf}(u)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\bigcup_{w\in\mathsf{subtree}[u],w\text{ is a leaf of }T}\mathsf{sheaf}(w).

See
Figure

1
for an illustration.

w
w^{\prime}

Figure 1
:
Illustration of an elimination tree of a graph with two distinguished leaves
w
and
w^{\prime}
; the edges of the tree are depicted in light grey. The vertices
b
with
\mathsf{left}(b)=w
are depicted in blue. The (thick) blue edges are the edges of
\mathsf{sheaf}(w)
and the (thick) magenta edges are the edges of
\mathsf{sheaf}(w_{2})
.

First, observe that
(\mathsf{sheaf}(w))_{w\text{ is a leaf}}
is a partition of
E(G)
.
Second, for every non-leaf node
u
with children
v_{1},\dots,v_{t}
, the sets
\mathsf{sheaf}(v_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{sheaf}(v_{t})
partition
\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
and the sets
\mathsf{subtree}[v_{1}]
,
\dots,\mathsf{subtree}[v_{t}]
partition
\mathsf{subtree}(u)
.
And finally, the following holds:

Observation 3.2
.

For every node
u
of
T
, we have
V(\mathsf{sheaf}(u))\subseteq\mathsf{tail}[u]\cup\mathsf{subtree}[u]
.

The notion of partial solutions we use in our proofs is defined with respect to a triple
\mathsf{tss}=(\mathsf{tail},\mathsf{subtree},\mathsf{sheaf})
—called a
triad
(in
G
)—where
\mathsf{tail}
and
\mathsf{subtree}
are disjoint subsets of
V(G)
and
\mathsf{sheaf}
is a subset of
E(G[\mathsf{tail}\cup\mathsf{subtree}])
.
The elements of this triad are then referred to as respectively the
tail
, the
subtree
, and the
sheaf
of
\mathsf{tss}
.
We define the
broom
of the triad
\mathsf{tss}
as the set
\mathsf{tail}\cup\mathsf{subtree}
.
In our model checking algorithm, we deal with the triads that are naturally induced by the elimination tree. For every node
u
of
T
, we define the triads
\mathsf{tss}[u]=(\mathsf{tail}[u],\mathsf{subtree}(u),\mathsf{sheaf}(u))
and
\mathsf{tss}(u)=(\mathsf{tail}(u),\mathsf{subtree}[u],\mathsf{sheaf}(u))
.
Observe that
\mathsf{tss}[u]
and
\mathsf{tss}(u)
have the same sheaf and the same broom which we refer to as
\mathsf{broom}[u]
.
Notice that, for the root
r
of
T
, we have
\mathsf{tss}(r)=(\emptyset,V(G),E(G))
.

3.3
Discrete Fourier transforms

For integers
a,b,c
with
c>0
we write
a\equiv_{c}b
to denote that two integers
a
and
b
leave the same rest when divided by
c
, i.e., are equal modulo
c
.
Also if
a\in[c-1]_{0}
, we sometimes write
a=b\pmod{c}
to denote that
a
is the rest of
b
modulo
c
.
We naturally extend this notation to polynomials and functions.
For a positive integer
r
, by
\mathbb{Z}_{r}
we denote the ring of integers modulo
r
.
For a prime number
p
, by
\mathbb{F}_{p}
we denote the field of integers modulo
p
, and by
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}
we denote the multiplicative group of this field.
If
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}
contains a number
\omega
such that
\omega^{r}\equiv_{p}1
and
\omega^{j}\not\equiv_{p}1
for all
1\leqslant j<r
, then
\omega
is called the
r
-th root of unity
.
The
r
-th root of unity of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}
does not necessarily exist and it is, in general, not unique.

For the remainder of this subsection we fix a finite set
D
, a positive integer
r
, and a prime number
p
such that in
\mathbb{F}^{*}_{p}
the
r
-th root of unity, denoted by
\omega=\omega_{r,p}
, exists.
If multiple such roots of unity exist, we fix one arbitrary (it will be the smallest one in our applications). For a mapping
h\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
the
discrete Fourier transform
(also called
DFT
) of
h

\mathsf{DFT}_{D,r,p}(h)\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
and the
inverse discrete Fourier transform
(also called
inverse DFT
) of
h

\mathsf{DFT}_{D,r,p}^{-1}(h)\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
are defined as

\mathsf{DFT}_{D,r,p}(h)(y)\equiv_{p}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{\sum_{z\in D}q(z)\cdot y(z)}\cdot h(q).

and

\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}_{D,r,p}(h)(y)\equiv_{p}\frac{1}{r^{|D|}}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{\sum_{z\in D}-q(z)\cdot y(z)}\cdot h(q).

for every
y\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
, here
\frac{1}{r^{|D|}}
denotes the inverse of
r^{|D|}
in
\mathbb{F}^{*}_{p}
.
For
y,q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
we use the notation
q\cdot y
for
\sum_{z\in D}q(z)\cdot y(z)
.
With these shortcuts we have

\mathsf{DFT}_{D,r,p}(h)(y)\equiv_{p}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{q\cdot y}\cdot h(q)\text{ and }\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}_{D,r,p}(h)(y)\equiv_{p}\frac{1}{r^{|D|}}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{-q\cdot y}\cdot h(q).

The following two classical results will be useful for us.
We refer, for example, to the survey by van Rooij
[
40
]
for the proofs and applications in the area of fast algorithms on tree decompositions.
First, the inverse DFT is indeed the inverse of the DFT:

Theorem 3.3
.

It holds that
\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}_{D,r,p}(\mathsf{DFT}_{D,r,p}(h))\equiv_{p}h
and
\mathsf{DFT}_{D,r,p}(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}_{D,r,p}(h))\equiv_{p}h
.

For
y,q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
we define
y+q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
as
(y+q)(d)\equiv_{r}y(d)+q(d)
for every
d\in D
.
Further, for
h,g\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
we define
h\cdot g\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
via
(f\cdot g)(y)\equiv_{p}h(y)\cdot g(y)
for every
y\in D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
.
Then the following holds

Theorem 3.4
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{N}
, let
h^{1},\dots,h^{t}\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and let
y\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
. Then it holds that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{1},\dots,y^{t}\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}\\
y^{1}+\dots+y^{t}\equiv_{r}y\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}h^{i}(y^{i})\equiv_{p}(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}_{D,r,p}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathsf{DFT}_{D,r,p}(h^{i})\Bigr))(y),

Now we extend the notions and results to the case where the codomain of
h
is not
\mathbb{F}_{p}
but rather the set of polynomials with coefficients from
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
Let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}
be pairwise distinct formal variables.
The set
\mathbb{F}_{p}[\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}]
consists of all polynomials in formal variables
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}
with coefficients from
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.
For a mapping
h\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}[\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}]
, we define the
DFT
of
h

\mathsf{DFT}_{D,r,p}(h)\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}[\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}]
and the
inverse DFT
of
h

\mathsf{DFT}_{D,r,p}^{-1}(h)\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}[\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}]
as

\mathsf{DFT}_{D,r,p}(h)(y)\equiv_{p}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{q\cdot y}\cdot h(q)\text{ and }\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}_{D,r,p}(h)(y)\equiv_{p}\frac{1}{r^{|D|}}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{F}_{p}}\omega^{-q\cdot y}\cdot h(q)

for all
y\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
.
Observe that in the case
\ell=0
the codomain of
h
is simply
\mathbb{F}_{p}
so we indeed extend the above case.
Thanks to the linearity of DFT and its inverse,
Theorems

3.3
and
3.4
hold for polynomials as well. A formal proof of this claim is available in
Appendix

A
.

3.4
Inclusion-exclusion

We will also make use of the folklore inclusion-exclusion principle (we refer to
[
16
]
for a proof and applications). In particular, we will rely on its intersection version:

Theorem 3.5

(​​
[
16
]
, Theorem 10.2)
.

Let
U
and
Y
be finite sets and let
A_{y}\subseteq U
for every
y\in Y
.
Then it holds that:

\lvert\bigcap_{y\in Y}A_{y}\rvert=\sum_{X\subseteq Y}(-1)^{|X|}\cdot\lvert U\setminus\left(\cup_{x\in X}A_{x}\right)\rvert.

4
Neighborhood Operator Logics

In the following, we introduce the syntax and semantics of
\mathsf{NEO}
logics. As explained in the introduction, these logics are based on the
\mathsf{DN}
logic introduced in
[
6
]
.
These logics are obtained by extending fully-existential monadic second-order (
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
) logic.
Remember that
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
allows quantification over vertices, edges, sets of vertices and sets of edges;
together with an adjacency relation
E(\cdot,\cdot)
and equality relation
=
between vertices, unary vertex relations (i.e., colors)
as well as the containment relation
\in
of vertices/edges in sets.
Fully-existential
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
is the restriction of
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
to existential (first-order and second-order) quantifiers, while furthermore requiring that only quantifier-free formulas may be negated.
Set variables are denoted by upper-case letters (
X,Y,Z,\dots
) while unary and binary relations (or colors) are denoted by typewriter letters (
\mathtt{P},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R},\dots
).
The letters
\mathtt{V}
and
\mathtt{E}
are special unary and binary relations reserved for the set of all vertices and all edges, respectively.

Syntax.

We first define
edge set terms
using the following rules:

1.

Every edge set variable
Y
is an edge set term.

2.

Every binary relational symbol
\mathtt{P}
is an edge set term.

3.

\emptyset
and
\mathtt{E}
are edge set terms.

4.

If
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are edge set terms then
\overline{t_{1}}
,
t_{1}\cap t_{2}
,
t_{1}\cup t_{2}
and
t_{1}\setminus t_{2}
are also edge set terms.

Secondly, we define
neighborhood terms
using the following rules:

1.

Every vertex set variable
X
is a neighborhood term.

2.

Every unary relational symbol
\mathtt{U}
is a neighborhood term.

3.

\emptyset
and
\mathtt{V}
are neighborhood terms.

4.

N^{\sigma}(t)
,
N^{\sigma}(t,Y)
and
N^{\sigma}(t,\mathtt{F})
are neighborhood terms for every
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, neighborhood term
t
, edge set variable
Y
and binary relational symbol
\mathtt{F}
.

5.

If
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are neighborhood terms then
\overline{t_{1}}
,
t_{1}\cap t_{2}
,
t_{1}\cup t_{2}
and
t_{1}\setminus t_{2}
are also neighborhood terms.

Then
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}
is the extension of fully-existential
\mathsf{MSO_{2}}
by the following two rules:

6.

If
t
is a neighborhood term or an edge set term and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, then
|t|=m
,
|t|\leq m
and
|t|\geq m
are formulas called
size measurements
.

7.

If
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are both neighborhood terms or edge set terms, then
t_{1}=t_{2}
,
t_{1}\subseteq t_{2}
and
t_{1}\supseteq t_{2}
are formulas.

We define
\mathsf{NEO_{1}}
as the restriction of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}
where we forbid variables for edges and edge sets.
Given
i\in\{1,2\}
, and a family
\mathsf{F}
of subsets of
\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\mathsf{NEO}_{i}[\mathsf{F}]
the restriction of
\mathsf{NEO}_{i}
such that, for all neighborhood operators
N^{\sigma}(\cdot)
we have
\sigma\in\mathsf{F}
.
In this paper, we consider the following families of subsets of
\mathbb{N}
:

•

\mathsf{FcoF}
consists of all the non-empty subsets of
\mathbb{N}
that are finite or co-finite.

•

\mathsf{FRec}
consists of all the subsets
\sigma
of
\mathbb{N}
that are
finitely recognizable
(see
Definition

1.2
).

On top of this, given a fragment of
\mathsf{L}
of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}
, we define
\mathsf{L\mathsf{+AC}}
as the extension of
\mathsf{L}
with the following additional rule:

8.

If
t
is a neighborhood term or an edge term, then
\mathsf{con}(t)
and
\mathsf{acy}(t)
are formulas.

Similarly, we define

\mathsf{L\mathsf{+K}}
as the extension of
\mathsf{L}
with the following additional rule:

9.

If
t
is a neighborhood term, then
\mathsf{clique}(t)
is a formula.

And we define
\mathsf{L\mathsf{+ACK}}
as the extension of
\mathsf{L}
with the rules 8. and 9.

Let
\xi
be a formula or a term of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+ACK}
.
We denote the set of variables that occur in
\xi
by the ordered tuple
\mathsf{var}(\xi)
and we denote by
\mathsf{free}(\xi)
the set of free variables of
\xi
.
We denote by
\mathsf{var}^{V}(\xi)
the set of variables for vertices and vertex sets, and we denote by
\mathsf{var}^{E}(\xi)
the set of variables for edges and edge sets.
Moreover, we denote by
\mathsf{color}^{V}(\xi)
and
\mathsf{color}^{E}(\xi)
the sets of unary and respectively binary relational symbols that occur in
\xi
.
We define the
length

|\varphi|
of a
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+ACK}
formula
\varphi
to be the number of symbols of
\varphi
.
Note that every number or set (as occurring for example in a size measurement
t\leq m
or in a superscript of a neighborhood term
N^{\sigma}(\cdot)
) is
one
symbol.

Semantics.

Next, we define the semantics of
\mathsf{NEO}
logic.
We consider
vertex and edge colored
graphs.
This means, each vertex (respectively edge) of a graph may be in zero, one or more unary (resp. binary) relations (
\mathtt{P},\mathtt{Q},\mathtt{R},\dots
). Our notion of interpretation of a logical formula uses two functions, one for interpreting the variables for vertices and vertex sets, and another for the variables for edges and edge sets. We do so to improve the readability of the model checking section.

Definition 4.1
.

Given a graph
G
and
A\subseteq V(G)
. A vertex interpretation of a formula
\varphi
on
A
is a function mapping each vertex variable of
\varphi
to a vertex in
A
and each vertex set variable of
\varphi
to a subset of
A
.
Given
F\subseteq E(G)
, an edge interpretation of
\varphi
on
F
is a function mapping each edge variable of
\varphi
to an edge in
F
and each edge set variable of
\varphi
to a subset of
F
.

An
interpretation
of a formula
\varphi
is a tuple
(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})
consisting of a graph
G
, a vertex interpretation
\mathbf{f}
of
\varphi
on
V(G)
(mapping every vertex set variable to a subset of
V(G)
, and every vertex variable to a vertex of
G
)
and an edge interpretation
\mathbf{g}
of
\varphi
on
E(G)
(mapping every edge set variable to a subset of
E(G)
, and every edge variable to an edge of
G
).
Given an interpretation
(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})
, we define the semantics of neighborhood and edge set terms.

1.

[\![X]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=\mathbf{f}(X)
if
X\in\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
or
[\![X]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=\mathbf{g}(X)
if
X\in\mathsf{var}^{E}(\varphi)
,

2.

[\![\mathtt{P}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=\mathtt{P}(G)
,

3.

[\![\emptyset]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=\emptyset
,
[\![\mathtt{V}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=V(G)
, and
[\![\mathtt{E}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=E(G)

4.

[\![N^{\sigma}(t,\star)]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=N^{\sigma}([\![t]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})},[\![\star]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})})
, and

[\![N^{\sigma}(t)]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=N^{\sigma}([\![t]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})})
, where the neighborhood operators on the right are evaluated in
G
(see
Definition

1.1
),

5.

[\![\ \overline{t}\ ]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=V(G)\setminus[\![t]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}
,

[\![t_{1}\star t_{2}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=[\![t_{1}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}\star[\![t_{2}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}
for
\star\in\{\cap,\cup,\setminus\}
,

\mathsf{NEO_{2}}
inherits the semantics from
\mathsf{MSO_{1}}
, with the following semantics of the additional rules.

6.

[\![|t|\prec m]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=1
if
|[\![t]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}|\prec m
and
[\![|t|\prec m]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=0
otherwise, for
\prec\in\{=,\leq,\geq\}
,

7.

[\![t_{1}\prec t_{2}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=1
if
[\![t_{1}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}\prec[\![t_{1}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}
and
[\![t_{1}\prec t_{2}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=0
otherwise, for
\prec\in{\{=,\subseteq,\supseteq\}}
.

For
\mathsf{NEO}\mathsf{+ACK}
, the semantics of the additional rule are as follows.

8.

[\![\mathsf{con}(t)]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=[
the subgraph of
G
induced by
[\![t]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}
is connected
]
,

[\![\mathsf{acy}(t)]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=[
the subgraph of
G
induced by
[\![t]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}
is acyclic
]
,

[\![\mathsf{clique}(t)]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=[
the subgraph of
G
induced by
[\![t]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}
forms a clique
]
.

4.1
Problems captured by our logics

We provide here some examples of problems that are captured by
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
and not yet mentioned in the introduction including
SAT
and
Max-SAT
on CNF formulas.

As demonstrated in the introduction, we can express any
(\sigma,\rho)
-set property—as introduced by Telle and Proskurowski
[
39
]
—where
\sigma
and
\rho
are finitely recognizable sets.
With the same arguments as used in
[
6
]
for the
\mathsf{DN}
logic, we can generalize the latter formula to express the more general
locally checkable vertex partitioning
problems from
[
39
]
, that ask, given a
q\times q
matrix
D
whose entries are finitely recognizable sets, for a partition
(X_{1},\dots,X_{q})
of
V(G)
such that for every
i,j\in[q]
, we have
X_{i}\subseteq N^{D[i,j]}(X_{j})
.

Now, we show that we can express
SAT
via the
signed incidence graph
of a CNF formula.
Given a CNF formula
\xi
, we define its signed incidence graph
G_{\xi}
as follows.
The vertices of
G_{\xi}
are the variables and the clauses of
\xi
, and the latter are colored with
\mathtt{C}
.
The edges of
G_{\xi}
are defined as follows.
For every variable
x
and every clause
C
of
\xi
, we have

•

If
x
appears positively in
C
, then
xC
is an edge of
G_{\xi}
colored with
\mathtt{P}
.

•

If
x
appears negatively in
C
, then
xC
is an edge of
G_{\xi}
colored with
\mathtt{N}
.

Then, we can express
SAT
, i.e., the existence of a model for
\xi
with the following formula

\exists X\subseteq\overline{\mathtt{C}}\land\mathtt{C}=N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(X,\mathtt{P})\cup N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(\overline{X},\mathtt{N}).

Basically, in this formula
X
is the set of variables that a model of
\xi
sets to true, and every clause needs to be adjacent to either a variable in
X
via an edge labeled
\mathtt{P}
or a variable not in
X
via an edge labeled
\mathtt{N}
.
Moreover, we can express
Max-SAT
, i.e., the existence of an interpretation of
\xi
that satisfies at least
k
clauses with the following formula

\exists X\subseteq\overline{\mathtt{C}}\land\lvert N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(X,\mathtt{P})\cup N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(\overline{X},\mathtt{N})\rvert\geqslant k.

4.2
Core logic

Some of the operations in our logic can be understood as “syntactic sugar”, that does not increase the expressiveness, but merely reduces some friction when expressing problems.
To facilitate our proofs, we consider a smaller fragment
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}
of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}
, which we call
core neighborhood operator logic
, that has the same expressive power as
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}
,
and a similar equivalent fragment
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+AC}
for
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}

\mathsf{+AC}
.
Similarly, we also consider the fragments
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+K}
and
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+ACK}
.

Definition of core logics.

We first define
primitive formulas
.

1.

If
X
is a vertex set variable and
\mathtt{U}
is a unary relational symbol,
\emptyset
, or
\mathtt{V}
, then
\mathtt{U}=X
is a
primitive formula
.

2.

If
Y
is an edge set variable and
\mathtt{B}
is a binary relational symbol,
\emptyset
, or
\mathtt{E}
then
\mathtt{B}=Y
is a
primitive formula
.

3.

If
X
,
Y
and
Z
are vertex set variables, then
X=Y
,
\overline{X}=Y
,
X\cup Y=Z
, and
X\cap Y=Z
are
primitive formulas
.

4.

If
X
and
W
are vertex set variables,
Y
an edge set variable and,
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, then
N^{\sigma}(X,Y)=W
is a
primitive formula
.

5.

If
X
is a vertex or edge set variable and
m\in\mathbb{N}
then
|X|\leq m
,
|X|\geq m
, and
|X|=m
are
primitive formulas
.

6.

If
X
is a vertex or edge set variable then
\mathsf{acy}(X)
and
\mathsf{con}(X)
are
primitive formulas
.

7.

If
X
is a vertex set variable then
\mathsf{clique}(X)
is a
primitive formula
.

Let
\mathsf{core}
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}
be the fragment of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}
containing all formulas of the form

\exists X_{1}\,\dots\exists X_{k}\,\psi
, where
\psi
is a Boolean combination
of primitive formulas as described by the items 1. to 5. and such that
\mathsf{var}(\psi)=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\}
.
We define
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+AC}
(resp.
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+K}
) to be the fragment of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+AC}
containing all formulas of the form

\exists X_{1}\,\dots\exists X_{k}\,\psi
, where
\psi
is a Boolean combination
of primitive formulas as described by the items 1. to 6. (resp. 1. to 5. as well as 7.) and such that
\mathsf{var}(\psi)=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\}
.
Finally, we define
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+ACK}
to be the fragment of
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+ACK}
containing all formulas of the form

\exists X_{1}\,\dots\exists X_{k}\,\psi
, where
\psi
is a Boolean combination
of primitive formulas as described by the items 1. to 7. and such that
\mathsf{var}(\psi)=\{X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\}
.
The following observation proves that
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+ACK}
is equivalent to
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+ACK}
, a similar statement for the
\mathsf{DN}
logic can be found in
[
6
]
.
We only provide a sketch of proof as most of the arguments can be found in
[
6
, Subsection 3.1]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For every formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+ACK}
one can compute in time
O(|\varphi|^{2})
an equivalent formula
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+ACK}
such that

•

|\varphi^{\prime}|\leq 10|\varphi|
,

•

the sets
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{N}
occurring in
\varphi^{\prime}
are exactly the ones occurring in
\varphi
, and

•

\varphi^{\prime}
has at most two size measurements more than
\varphi
.

Moreover, if
\varphi\in\mathsf{NEO_{2}}
then
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}
; and if
\varphi\in\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+K}
then
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+K}
.

Proof.

We start by converting
\varphi
into prenex-normal form, i.e., of the form
\exists\bar{X}\,\xi
, where
\exists\bar{X}
is a sequence of existentially quantified variables and
\xi
is quantifier-free.

Next, we get rid of every vertex variable.
Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{t}
be the vertex variables of
\varphi
, we transform
\exists\bar{X}\,\xi
by replacing each
x_{i}
by a new vertex set variable
X_{i}
, and we make the following translations:

x_{i}\in Y
translates to
X_{i}\subseteq Y
,

x_{i}=x_{j}
translates to
X_{i}=X_{j}
, and

E(x_{i},x_{j})
translates to
X_{i}\subseteq N^{\{1\}}(X_{j})
.
Then we replace
\xi
by

\exists\bar{X}\,\xi\land X_{1}\neq\emptyset\land\dots\land X_{t}\neq\emptyset\land\lvert X_{1}\cup\dots\cup X_{t}\rvert\leqslant t.

This will make sure that every model of the new formula interprets the new variables
X_{1},\dots,X_{t}
as singletons.
Similarly, we can get rid of edge variables.

Now, we replace every
t_{1}\supseteq t_{2}
by
t_{2}\subseteq t_{1}
.
After that, replace every
t_{1}\subseteq t_{2}
with
t_{1}\cap t_{2}=t_{1}
.
Then, we replace every neighborhood term
N^{\sigma}(t)
by
N^{\sigma}(t,\mathtt{E})
.
The above operations conserve a formula in prenex-normal form that we denote by
\exists\bar{X}\,\psi
, where
\psi
is quantifier-free.
Then we can exhaustively apply the following simplification:
If
\psi
contains a term
N^{\sigma}(t,t^{\prime})
,
N^{\sigma}(t^{\prime},t)
,
t\cup t^{\prime}
,
t^{\prime}\cup t

t\cap t^{\prime}
,
t^{\prime}\cap t
,
\overline{t}
,
\mathsf{con}(t)
,
\mathsf{acy}(t)
,
t^{\prime}=t
, or
|t|\prec m
for
\prec\in\{\leqslant,=,\geqslant\}
, where
t
is not a variable
then we can replace
\psi
by

\exists Y\,t=Y\land\psi^{\prime}
, where
Y
is some unused variable and
\psi^{\prime}
is obtained from
\psi
by replacing
t
with
Y
.
For example, the formula
N^{\sigma_{1}}(N^{\sigma}(Y,\mathtt{B}),W)=\overline{Z}
is replaced with

\exists X_{1}\exists X_{2}\exists X_{3}\ N^{\sigma_{1}}(X_{1},W)=X_{2}\land N^{\sigma}(Y,X_{3})=X_{1}\land\overline{Z}=X_{2}\land\mathtt{B}=X_{3}.

Let
\varphi^{\prime}
be resulting formula.
The final transformation iteratively remove nested neighborhood operators, and equalities that are not primitive formulas. We deduce that
\varphi^{\prime}
is a
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}\mathsf{+ACK}
formula.
It can be checked that this procedure can be carried out in time quadratic in
|\varphi|
and
\lvert\varphi^{\prime}\rvert
is at most a factor of
10
longer than the original one.
Moreover, we did not change the set of
\sigma
-s occurring in the formula, and we introduce at most two new size measurements (for the vertex variables and edge variables).
Finally, since we did not introduce new
\mathsf{con},\mathsf{acy},\mathsf{clique}
constraints, the last statement of the lemma is trivially satisfied.
∎

5
Partial Solutions and Related Notions

Let
\varphi
be a
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]

formula, we fix it for the rest of this paper.
We also fix a vertex and edge colored graph
G
and we will show how to check whether
G\models\varphi
given an elimination tree of depth
\mathsf{td}
of
G
in time
2^{\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td})}n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and space
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}\log(n))
.
Here
\mathcal{O}
hides some factors depending on the formula
\varphi
.
In
theorem

7.7
we will explicitly provide the dependence of time and space complexity on
\varphi
.

Definition 5.1
.

We define

as the set of all pairs
(X,Y)
with
X\in\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
and

Y\in\mathsf{var}^{E}(\varphi)

such that
N^{\sigma}(X,Y)
occurs in
\varphi
.
2
2

2

This notation is explained by the lack of imagination of the authors during the last day of a research visit. It stuck. We adore cats!

We denote by
\mathsf{EQ}^{V}(\varphi)
the set of equalities in
\varphi
between neighborhood terms and by
\mathsf{EQ}^{E}(\varphi)
the set of those between edge set terms.
And we use
\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
to denote the set
\mathsf{EQ}^{V}(\varphi)\cup\mathsf{EQ}^{E}(\varphi)
.
Given an interpretation
(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})
of
\varphi
, we denote by
\mathbf{\bot}(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})
the set of equalities
t_{1}=t_{2}
in
\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
such that
[\![t_{1}=t_{2}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}=0
.
And we let

X_{1},\dots,X_{\ell}
be all (vertex set and edge set) variables of
\varphi
that occur in at least one size measurement of
\varphi
.

5.1
Formula parameters and auxiliary digraph

In this subsection, based on
G
and
\varphi
we define two crucial integers
d_{\varphi}
and
p_{\varphi}
as well as an auxiliary digraph
\vec{G}_{\varphi}
relying on which we will later define our notion of partial solutions.

We define
d_{\varphi}
as the maximum over all
d(\sigma)
for the sets
\sigma
occurring in
\varphi
; moreover, we define
p_{\varphi}
as the least common multiple of all
p(\sigma)
for all sets
\sigma
occurring in
\varphi
(see
Definition

1.2
for the definitions of
d(\sigma)
and
p(\sigma)
).
To simplify our arguments, we assume that
d_{\varphi}\geqslant 1
. This is safe as we can always increase
d_{\varphi}
artificially without changing the correctness of any of the following statements including the following key observation.

Observation 5.2
.

Let
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{N}
be a set that
occurs in
\varphi
. And let
U\subseteq V(G)
,
F\subseteq E(G)
,
v\in V(G)
, and
i\in[0,p_{\varphi}-1]
be such that
\lvert N(v,F)\cap U\rvert\geqslant d_{\varphi}
and
i\equiv_{p_{\varphi}}\lvert N(v,F)\cap U\rvert
.
Then we have
\lvert N(v,F)\cap U\rvert\equiv_{p(\sigma)}i+(d_{\varphi}\cdot p_{\varphi})
.
And therefore,
v\in N^{\sigma}(U,F)
if and only if
i+(d_{\varphi}\cdot p_{\varphi})\in\sigma
.

Our algorithm makes use of the following auxiliary digraph associated with
G
and
\varphi
.

Definition 5.3
.

We define
\vec{G}_{\varphi}
as the directed graph with vertex set
V(\vec{G}_{\varphi})=\{v^{1},\dots,v^{d_{\varphi}}\,:\,v\in V(G)\}
and arc set
\vec{A}(\vec{G}_{\varphi})=\{(u^{1},v^{i})\,:\,uv\in E(G)\land i\in[d_{\varphi}]\}
; see
Figure

2
for an illustration.
For a set
W\subseteq E(G)
of edges, we denote by
\vec{W}
the set of arcs
\{(u^{1},v^{i})\,:\,uv\in W\land i\in[d_{\varphi}]\}
.

We say that a set
\vec{S}\subseteq\vec{A}(\vec{G_{\varphi}})
of arcs is
simple
if for every edge
uv\in E(G)
, there is at most one
i\in[d_{\varphi}]
such that
(u^{1},v^{i})\in\vec{S}
and at most one
j\in[d_{\varphi}]
such that
(v^{1},u^{j})\in\vec{S}
.

u
v
u^{1}
u^{2}
\dots
u^{d_{\varphi}}
v^{1}
v^{2}
\dots
v^{d_{\varphi}}

Figure 2
:
Illustration of the transformation from a graph
G
to the graph
\vec{G}_{\varphi}
. The edge
uv\in E(G)
on the left corresponds to the arcs
(u^{1},v^{1}),\ldots,(u^{1},v^{d_{\varphi}})
and
(v^{1},u^{1}),\ldots,(v^{1},u^{d_{\varphi}})
on the right.

5.2
Neighborhood functions, mod-degrees and partial interpretations

The following definition introduces two important notions—neighborhood functions and mod-degrees—that play a key role in our algorithm. Indeed, they are important parts of our partial solutions, but also of the indexes of our recursive algorithm.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
\mathsf{dom}\subseteq V(G)
and
3
3

3

Here, we use
\mathsf{sheaf}
to denote an edge subset of
G
