to prepare the reader that this definition will be applied for the sheaf of some node of the given elimination tree of
G
.

\mathsf{sheaf}\subseteq E(G)
.
A mod-degree on
\mathsf{dom}
is a function
\mathtt{pd}\colon\mathsf{dom}\times\to\mathbb{Z}_{p_{\varphi}}
. We denote by
\mathtt{PD}(\mathsf{dom})
the set of all mod-degrees on
\mathsf{dom}
.
A
neighborhood function
on
\mathsf{dom}
is a function
\mathbb{E}\colon\mathsf{dom}\times\to[0,d_{\varphi}]
.

Let us give some intuitions about how we use these two kinds of functions. Roughly, mod-degrees are used to handle the periodic part of the sets
\sigma
occurring in
\varphi
while neighborhood functions handle the part between 0 and
d_{\varphi}
.
We use mod-degree to control, for each
v\in\mathsf{dom}
and
(X,Y)\in
, the number of edges in
Y
between
v
and
X
modulo
p_{\varphi}
. We will apply the discrete Fourier transform to mod-degrees to accelerate the so-called join-computation combining the subtrees rooted at the children of some node of an elimination forest (see
section

6.5
).
We use neighborhood functions for two purposes:

•

The
guessing
neighborhood functions denoted by
\mathbb{E}
or
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
are used to guess, for each
v\in\mathsf{dom}
and
(X,Y)\in
, the number of expected edges in
Y
between
v
and
X
.
In fact, when
\mathbb{E}(v,(X,Y))<d_{\varphi}
, we expect that there will be
exactly

\mathbb{E}(v,(X,Y))
edges in
Y
between
v
and
X
.
However, when
\mathbb{E}(v,(X,Y))=d_{\varphi}
, we expect
at least

d_{\varphi}
such edges—we emphasize this special meaning of the value
d_{\varphi}
as it will be used several times in definitions and intuitions.
In both cases, this number is an expectation and we will first consider some partial solutions that do not respect this at all, but then we will filter these renegade partial solutions with inclusion-exclusion principle and counting.

•

The
restricting
neighborhood functions denoted by
\mathtt{alw}
are used to forbid a partial solution to have some arcs in
A(\vec{G_{\varphi}})
.
We use this kind of neighborhood functions to apply the inclusion-exclusion principle in order to kill some of the renegade partial solutions mentioned above.

Given a guessing neighborhood function
\mathbb{E}
and a mod-degree
\mathtt{pd}
, we know the set of vertices from
\mathsf{dom}
that is expected to belong in a neighborhood term
N^{\sigma}(X,Y)
occurring in
\varphi
.
In addition to a vertex interpretation and an edge interpretation of
\varphi
, we can interpret all the terms and equalities of
\varphi
as follows.

Definition 5.5
.

Given a vertex interpretation
\mathbf{f}
of
\varphi
on
\mathsf{dom}
, an edge interpretation
\mathbf{g}
of
\varphi
on
\mathsf{sheaf}
, a neighborhood function
\mathbb{E}
on
\mathsf{dom}
, and a mod-degree
\mathtt{pd}
on
\mathsf{dom}
, we define

•

[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=[\![t]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}\cap\mathsf{sheaf}
for every edge set term
t
,

•

[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=[\![t]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g})}\cap\mathsf{dom}
for every neighborhood term
t
that is not a neighborhood operator
N^{\sigma}(C)
with
C\in
.

•

For every
C\in
and every
\sigma\subseteq\mathbb{N}
that occurs in
\varphi
,

\displaystyle[\![N^{\sigma}(C)]\!]^{(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=

\displaystyle\Bigl\{v\in\mathsf{dom}\,:\,\mathbb{E}(v,C)<d_{\varphi}\text{ and }\mathbb{E}(v,C)\in\sigma\Bigr\}\cup

\displaystyle\Bigl\{v\in\mathsf{dom}\,:\,\mathbb{E}(v,C)=d_{\varphi}\text{ and }\mathtt{pd}(v,C)+(d_{\varphi}\cdot p_{\varphi})\in\sigma\Bigr\}.

Also, we set

•

\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{t_{1}=t_{2}\in\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)\,:\,[\![t_{1}]\!]^{(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})}\neq[\![t_{2}]\!]^{(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})}\}
.

•

\mathbf{\bot}^{E}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})=\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})\cap\mathsf{EQ}^{E}(\varphi)
and
\mathbf{\bot}^{V}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})=\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})\cap\mathsf{EQ}^{V}(\varphi)
.

Observe that since
\varphi
is a
\mathsf{core}
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}
formula,
Definition

5.5
provide an exhaustive way of interpreting the edge terms, neighborhood terms, and the equalities of
\varphi
.

Remark 5.6
.

Observe that
[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})}
does not depend on
\mathbf{g}
when
t
is a neighborhood term, and it is also the case for
\mathbf{\bot}^{V}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})
. Thus, we sometimes simply write
[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})}
and
\mathbf{\bot}^{V}(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})
. For the same reasons, we sometimes write
[\![X]\!]^{(\mathbf{f})}
and
[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{g})}
when
X
is a vertex set variable and
t
is an edge term—sometimes, we add the superscript
G
to emphasize what the underlying graph is.
Moreover, we sometimes use
\mathbf{\bot}^{E}(\mathbf{g})
as a shortcut for
\mathbf{\bot}^{E}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})
.

The rest of this subsection is dedicated to introducing some notations and lemmas that will be used later on.
Since some of the equalities in
\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
might be negated or joined by disjunction in
\varphi
, a model of
\varphi
might, in general, falsify some of those equalities.
For this reason, we will be interested in the set of equalities from
\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
falsified by a certain partial solution (which will be formally defined in the next subsection).
The following definition and lemma below will later be useful to argue about the equalities falsified by a restriction of a partial solution:

Definition 5.7
.

Let
\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}\subseteq V(G)
. Given a vertex interpretation
\mathbf{f}
on
\mathsf{dom}
, we define the
restriction

\mathbf{f}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}}
of
\mathbf{f}
on
\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}
as the vertex interpretation on
\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{f}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}}(X)=\mathbf{f}(X)\cap\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}
for every
X\in\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
.
Moreover, for every neighborhood function or mod-degree
\mathbf{h}
on
\mathsf{dom}
, we define the
restriction

\mathbf{h}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}}
of
\mathbf{h}
on
\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}
as the neighborhood function or mod-degree, respectively, such that
\mathbf{h}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}}(u,C)=\mathbf{h}(u,C)
for every
u\in\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}
and every
C\in
.

Similarly, let
\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{sheaf}\subseteq E(G)
.
Given an edge interpretation
\mathbf{g}
on
\mathsf{sheaf}
, we define the
restriction

\mathbf{g}_{|_{\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}}}
of
\mathbf{g}
on
\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}
as the edge interpretation on
\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{g}_{|_{\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}}}(X)=\mathbf{g}(X)\cap\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}
for every
X\in\mathsf{var}^{E}(\varphi)
.
And for a mapping
\vec{\mathcal{W}}\colon\to 2^{\vec{\mathsf{sheaf}}}
, we define the
restriction

\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{|_{\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}}}\colon\to 2^{\vec{\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}}}
on
\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}
such that
\vec{\mathcal{W}}_{|_{\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}}}(C)=\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)\cap\vec{\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}}
for all
C\in
.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{dom}\subseteq V(G)
and let
\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{sheaf}\subseteq E(G)
.
Further let
\mathbf{f}
be a vertex interpretation of
\varphi
on
\mathsf{dom}
, let
\mathbf{g}
be an edge interpretation of
\varphi
on
\mathsf{sheaf}
, let
\mathbb{E}
be a neighborhood function on
\mathsf{dom}
, and let
\mathtt{pd}
be a mod-degree on
\mathsf{dom}
.
Then it holds that

\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{f}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}},\mathbf{g}_{|_{\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}}},\mathbb{E}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}},\mathtt{pd}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}})\subseteq\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd}).

Proof.

It suffices to observe that, by
Definition

4.1
, for every neighborhood term
t
, we have

[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{f}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}},\mathbf{g}_{|_{\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}}},\mathbb{E}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}},\mathtt{pd}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}})}=[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})}\cap\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}

and that for every edge set term
t
, we have

[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{f}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}},\mathbf{g}_{|_{\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}}},\mathbb{E}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}},\mathtt{pd}_{|_{\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}}})}=[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{pd})}\cap\mathsf{sheaf}^{\prime}.

∎

To argue about the equalities falsified by a union of partial solutions, we will also use the following definition and the lemma after it.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathsf{dom}\subseteq V(G)
, and let
\mathsf{sheaf}\subseteq E(G)
.
Further, let
\mathsf{dom}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{dom}_{t}
be a partition of
\mathsf{dom}
, let
\mathsf{sheaf}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{sheaf}_{t}
be a partition of
\mathsf{sheaf}
, and for every
i\in[t]
let
(\mathbf{f}_{i},\mathbf{g}_{i},\mathbb{E}_{i},\mathtt{pd}_{i})
be such that
\mathbf{f}_{i}
is a vertex interpretation of
\varphi
on
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
,
\mathbf{g}_{i}
is an edge interpretation of
\varphi
on
\mathsf{sheaf}_{i}
,
\mathbb{E}_{i}
be a neighborhood function on
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
, and
\mathtt{pd}_{i}
be a mod-degree on
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
.
Then we define

•

the vertex interpretation
\mathbf{f}_{[t]}
on
\mathsf{dom}
via
\mathbf{f}_{[t]}(X)=\bigcup_{i\in[t]}\mathbf{f}_{i}(X)
for all
X\in\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
,

•

the vertex interpretation
\mathbf{g}_{[t]}
on
\mathsf{sheaf}
via
\mathbf{g}_{[t]}(Y)=\bigcup_{i\in[t]}\mathbf{g}_{i}(Y)
for all
Y\in\mathsf{var}^{E}(\varphi)
,

•

the neighborhood function
\mathbb{E}_{[t]}
on
\mathsf{dom}
via
\mathbb{E}_{[t]}(v,C)=\mathbb{E}_{i}(v,C)
(for every
v\in\mathsf{dom}
and
C\in
) where
i\in[t]
is the unique value such that
v\in\mathsf{dom}_{i}
,

•

the mod-degree
\mathtt{pd}_{[t]}
on
\mathsf{dom}
via
\mathtt{pd}_{[t]}(v,C)=\mathtt{pd}_{i}(v,C)
(for every
v\in\mathsf{dom}
and
C\in
) where
i\in[t]
is the unique value such that
v\in\mathsf{dom}_{i}
.

Lemma 5.10
.

For objects from the above definition we have

\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{f}_{[t]},\mathbf{g}_{[t]},\mathbb{E}_{[t]},\mathtt{pd}_{[t]})=\bigcup_{i\in[t]}\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{f}_{i},\mathbf{g}_{i},\mathbb{E}_{i},\mathtt{pd}_{i}).

Proof.

It suffices to observe that, by
Definition

5.5
, for every neighborhood term or edge term
t
, we have

[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{f}_{[t]},\mathbf{g}_{[t]},\mathbb{E}_{[t]},\mathtt{pd}_{[t]})}=\bigcup_{i\in[t]}[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{f}_{i},\mathbf{g}_{i},\mathbb{E}_{i},\mathtt{pd}_{i})}.

∎

5.3
Partial solutions, indexes, and compatibility

To simplify the definitions and statements in this subsection, we fix a triad
\mathsf{tss}=(\mathsf{tail},\mathsf{subtree},\mathsf{sheaf})
of
G
.
To get some intuition, one should think of
\mathsf{subtree}
as the set of “finished” vertices, while the vertices in
\mathsf{tail}
are still “active” and may, for example, get further neighbors.
Moreover, we denote by
\mathsf{broom}
the broom of
\mathsf{tss}
, i.e.,
\mathsf{broom}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{tail}\cup\mathsf{subtree}
.
Now we are ready to present the notion of a partial solution used by our algorithm.

Definition 5.11
.

A
partial solution
for the triad
\mathsf{tss}
is a quadruple
(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
such that the following conditions are satisfied:

(Domain-respecting)

We have:

•

\mathbf{\hat{f}}
is a vertex interpretation of
\varphi
on
\mathsf{broom}
,

•

\mathbf{\hat{g}}
is an edge interpretation of
\varphi
on
\mathsf{sheaf}
,

•

\hat{\mathbb{E}}
is a neighborhood function on
\mathsf{broom}
,

•

\vec{\mathcal{W}}
maps each element
(X,Y)\in
to a
simple
subset of arcs from
\vec{\mathsf{sheaf}}
.

(Edge-consistent)

For every
(X,Y)\in
and every edge
uv\in\mathsf{sheaf}
the following equivalence holds: there exists
j\in[d_{\varphi}]
with
(u^{1},v^{j})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(X,Y)
if and only if
u\in\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
and
uv\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
.

(Respect-subtree-
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
)

For every
(X,Y)\in
, every
v\in\mathsf{subtree}
and every
j\in[\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))]
, the set
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(X,Y)
contains at least one arc incoming into
v^{j}
.

(Respect-broom-
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
)

For every
(X,Y)\in
, every
v\in\mathsf{broom}
and every
j\in[d_{\varphi}]
with
j>\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))
, the set
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(X,Y)
contains no arcs incoming into
v^{j}
.

We denote by
\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss})
the set of all partial solutions for
\mathsf{tss}
.

It is not difficult to see (we will formally prove it later though) that the properties
(Edge-consistent)
and
(Respect-subtree-

^
E

)
imply that for every vertex
v\in\mathsf{subtree}
and every pair
(X,Y)\in
, the vertex
v
has at least
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))
neighbors in
w\in\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
such that
vw\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
.
Later in the proofs of
lemma

5.20
and
lemma

5.22
we will see how how to ensure that (unless the special case
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))=d_{\varphi}
occurs), that
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))
is also an upper bound on the number of such neighbors, i.e., this is precisely this number.

Definition 5.12
.

Given
(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
satisfying
(Domain-respecting)
for
\mathsf{tss}
, we define the mod-degree
\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}
on
\mathsf{broom}
such that for every
w\in\mathsf{broom}
and
C\in
, we have:

\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}(w,C)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=|\{(z^{1},w^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)\colon z\in\mathsf{broom}\}|\pmod{p_{\varphi}}.

For a partial solution
(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
, in particular satisfying
(Edge-consistent)
, we will be able to use, for a vertex
v\in\mathsf{subtree}
and
(X,Y)\in
, the value
\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}(v,(X,Y))
as the number of neighbors
w\in\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
of
v
such that
vw\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
.
Altogether, assuming that
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))
is indeed also an upper bound on the number of such neighbors (which will be ensured later), we will be able to decide whether
v\in\mathsf{subtree}
belongs to
N^{\sigma}(\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X),\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y))
based on the values
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))
and
\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}(v,(X,Y))
.

This motivates the following definition of the indexes used by our recursive algorithm. Given two neighborhood functions
\mathtt{alw}
and
\mathbb{E}
on the same domain
\mathsf{dom}
, we write
\mathtt{alw}\leqslant\mathbb{E}
if for every
v\in\mathsf{dom}
and
C\in
, we have
\mathtt{alw}(v,C)\leqslant\mathbb{E}(v,C)
.

Definition 5.13
.

An
index
for
\mathsf{tss}
is a tuple
(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F})
where:

•

\mathbf{f}
a vertex interpretation of
\varphi
on
\mathsf{tail}
.

•

\mathbb{E}
and
\mathtt{alw}
are neighborhood functions on
\mathsf{tail}
such that
\mathtt{alw}\leqslant\mathbb{E}
.

•

\mathtt{pd}
is a mod-degree on
\mathsf{tail}
,

•

\mathtt{F}\subseteq\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
.

We denote by
\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{tss})
the set of all indexes for
\mathsf{tss}
.
We say that a partial solution
(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss})
is
compatible
with an index
(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F})\in\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{tss})
when the following conditions are satisfied:

(
\mathbf{f}
-consistent)

For every
X\in\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
, we have
\mathbf{f}(X)=\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)\cap\mathsf{tail}
.

(
\mathbb{E}
-consistent)

For every
v\in\mathsf{tail}
and every
C\in
, we have
\mathbb{E}(v,C)=\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)
.

(
\mathtt{pd}
-consistent)

For every
v\in\mathsf{tail}
and every
C\in
, we have
\mathtt{pd}(v,C)=\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}(v,C)
.

(Respect-
\mathtt{alw}
)

For every
v\in\mathsf{tail}
, every
C\in
, and every arc
(u^{1},v^{j})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
, we have
j\leqslant\mathtt{alw}(v,C)
.

(Respect-
\mathtt{F}
)

We have
\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}}_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}}_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}}},(\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}}})\subseteq\mathtt{F}
.

For every index
I\in\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{tss})
, we denote the set of all partial solutions in
\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss})
compatible with
I
by
\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss},I)
.
Moreover, we define
\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{tss},I)
as the subset of
\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss},I)
that contains all the partial solutions
(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
such that

\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}}_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}}_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}}},(\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}}})=\mathtt{F}
.

The neighborhood function
\mathtt{alw}
will be used in the so-called forget-computation (see
section

6.4
) to ensure that when a vertex is moved to the “finished” set
\mathsf{subtree}
, it satisfies
(Respect-subtree-

^
E

)
, i.e., certain copies of this vertex in
\vec{G}_{\varphi}
have incoming arcs.
This, in turn, ensures that the function
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
indeed provides lower bounds on the number of certain sets of relevant neighbors of this vertex.
The inclusion-exclusion (see
theorem

3.5
) allows to replace the task of ensuring the existence of incoming arcs by the task of forbidding such arcs. The neighborhood function
\mathtt{alw}
is then used to ensure that some copies of the vertex are forbidden to have incoming arcs.
We will provide all technical details in
section

6.4
.

Since
\mathtt{alw}
and
\mathtt{F}
are involved only in
(Respect-

alw

)
and
(Respect-

F

)
, respectively, we deduce the following inclusions.

Observation 5.14
.

Let
(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F})\in\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{tss})
.
For every neighborhood function
\mathtt{alw}^{\prime}
on
\mathsf{tail}
such that
\mathtt{alw}^{\prime}\leqslant\mathtt{alw}
, we have:

\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss},(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw}^{\prime},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F}))\subseteq\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss},(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F})).

Moreover, for every
\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathtt{F}
, we have:

\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss},(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F}^{\prime}))\subseteq\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss},(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F})).

We stratify the sets
\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss},I)
as follows.
The values
c_{1},\dots,c_{\ell}
simply keep track of the cardinalities of sets involved in size measurements in
\varphi
, while
a
and
b
will later be used to ensure that
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
provides correct estimates on the cardinalities of certain neighborhoods.

Definition 5.15
.

Given a partial solution
P=(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss})
, we define the following constants:

a_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{C\in}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathsf{subtree}\colon\\
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)<d_{\varphi}\end{subarray}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C),

b_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{C\in}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathsf{broom}\colon\\
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)<d_{\varphi}\end{subarray}}\lvert\{(u^{1},v^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)\}\rvert,

and for every
i\in[\ell]
,

c^{i}_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\lvert[\![X_{i}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}}_{|\mathsf{subtree}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})}\rvert.

Moreover, we use
\bar{c}_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)
to denote the tuple
(c^{1}_{\mathsf{tss}}(P),\dots,c^{\ell}_{\mathsf{tss}}(P))
.
Given an index
I\in\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{tss})
,
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bar{c}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
we define
\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss},I,a,b,\bar{c})
as the set of all partial solutions
P\in\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss},I)
such that
a_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)=a
,
b_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)=b
and
\bar{c}_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)=\bar{c}
.
We define
\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{tss},I,a,b,\bar{c})
from
\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{tss},I)
similarly.

The following lemma will be useful to bound the range of values we are interested in.
One should think of
\mathsf{td}
as the depth of an elimination tree of
G
which we will fix soon:

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
G
be a graph with at most
n\cdot\mathsf{td}
edges for some value
\mathsf{td}
.
Then for every
P\in\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss})
, the value
a_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)
is at most
|\varphi|\cdot d_{\varphi}\cdot n
and the value
b_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)
is at most
2\cdot|\varphi|\cdot n\cdot\mathsf{td}
.
Further, for every
i\in[\ell]
, we have
c^{i}_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)\leqslant n\cdot\mathsf{td}
.

Proof.

The size of is upper-bounded by
|\varphi|
, the sizes of
\mathsf{subtree}
and
\mathsf{broom}
are upper-bounded by
n
, and the size of
\mathsf{sheaf}
is upper-bounded by
n\cdot\mathsf{td}
.
From this the bound on
a_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)
follows immediately.
For every
C\in
, every edge
uv
of
G
contributes at most two arcs, namely one of form
(u^{1},v^{i})
and the other of form
(v^{1},u^{j})
(for some integers
i,j
), to the simple set
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
so the bound on
b_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)
follows as well.
And for
i\in[\ell]
,
c^{i}_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)
is the size of a set of vertices or edges, and thus it is upper-bounded by
n\cdot\mathsf{td}
.
∎

5.4
Relations between partial solutions and the formula satisfiability

Let
\mathsf{root}
denote the triad defined as
\mathsf{root}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\emptyset,V(G),E(G))
. Informally speaking, this corresponds to the case where all vertices and edges are “processed” already.
In this subsection, we will see how we can determine whether
G\models\varphi
holds from the cardinality
|\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{root},I,a,b,\bar{c})|
for some values of
I\in\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{root})
,
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bar{c}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
.

Given
\mathtt{F}\subseteq\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
and
\bar{c}=(c^{1},\dots,c^{\ell})\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
, we denote by
[\![\varphi]\!]^{(\mathtt{F},\bar{c})}
the logical value of the formula obtained from
\varphi
by (1) replacing every subformula
t_{1}=t_{2}\in\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
by 0 if it belongs to
\mathtt{F}
, and by 1 otherwise; and (2) replacing every subformula
\lvert X_{i}\rvert\prec m
with
\prec\in\{\leqslant,\geqslant,=\}
by 1 if
c^{i}\prec m
, and by 0 otherwise.

This subsection is dedicated to prove the following characterization for
G\models\varphi
based on our notion of partial solutions.
For simplicity, if the domain of some function is empty, we denote this function by
\emptyset
.
Similarly, we denote the unique vertex interpretation on the empty set by
\emptyset
.

Lemma 5.17
.

We have
G\models\varphi
if and only if there exist
\mathtt{F}\subseteq\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\bar{c}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
such that
[\![\varphi]\!]^{(\mathtt{F},\bar{c})}=1
and
\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{root},(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}),i,i,\bar{c})\neq 0
.

Observe that in this lemma we restrict ourselves to partial solutions
P
satisfying
a_{\mathsf{root}}(P)=b_{\mathsf{root}}(P)=i
for some
i\in\mathbb{N}
(cf.
definition

5.15
).
As we will see in the proof of this lemma, this restriction is a crucial trick to ensure that the neighborhood function
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
used in
P
provides correct estimates on the cardinalities of relevant neighborhoods.

In order to prove
Lemma

5.17
,
we first give an additional definition.

Definition 5.18
.

Given
\mathtt{F}\subseteq\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
and
\bar{c}=(c^{1},\dots,c^{\ell})\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
, we say that an interpretation
(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})
of
\varphi
realizes
(\mathtt{F},\bar{c})
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(Realize-
\mathbf{\bot}
)

\mathbf{\bot}(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})=\mathtt{F}
,

(Realize-
\bar{c}
)

[\![\lvert X_{i}\rvert=c^{i}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})}=1
for every
i\in[\ell]
.

Since in the above definition
\mathtt{F}
is the set of equalities falsified by the interpretation
(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})
, and
\bar{c}
determines the cardinalities of all sets involved in size measurements, the following observation immediately follows from the semantics of the logic:

Observation 5.19
.

We have
G\vDash\varphi
if and only if there exist
\mathtt{F}\subseteq\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
and
\bar{c}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
with
[\![\varphi]\!]^{(\mathtt{F},\bar{c})}=1
such that there exists an interpretation
(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})
of
\varphi
realizing
(\mathtt{F},\bar{c})
.

With this observation in hand, we can split the proof of the equivalence from
lemma

5.17
into two lemmas below.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
I=(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F})\in\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{root})
and
\bar{c}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
.
If an interpretation
(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})
of
\varphi
realizes
(\mathtt{F},\bar{c})
, then there exist functions
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
and
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
with the following properties.
Let
P\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
.
Then we have
P\in\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{root},I,a_{\mathsf{root}}(P),b_{\mathsf{root}}(P),\bar{c})
and
a_{\mathsf{root}}(P)=b_{\mathsf{root}}(P)
.

Proof.

Let
(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})
be an interpretation of
\varphi
that realizes the pair
(\mathtt{F},\bar{c})
. Also, let
c^{1},\ldots,c^{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\bar{c}=(c^{1},\dots,c^{\ell})\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
.
In the following, we construct a partial solution
P=(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
that belongs to
\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{root},I,a_{\mathsf{root}}(P),b_{\mathsf{root}}(P),\bar{c})
with
a_{\mathsf{root}}(P)=b_{\mathsf{root}}(P)
.
We construct
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
and
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
by doing the following for every
v\in V(G)
and
C=(X,Y)\in
.
Let
U(v,C)
be the set of all the vertices
u
in
\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
such that
uv
is an edge in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
. We set
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)=\min(d_{\varphi},\lvert U(v,C)\rvert)
.
For each
u\in U(v,C)
, we add one arc
(u^{1},v^{j})
to
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
in such a way thats for every
j
between
1
and
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)
, there is at least one arc
(u^{1},v^{j})
in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
, and there is no such arc for any
j>\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)
.
Since
\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}(v,C)=|\{(u^{1},v^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)\}|\bmod p_{\varphi}
, we deduce from
5.2
and the construction of
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
and
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
the following.

Observation 5.21
.

For every neighborhood term
N^{\sigma}(X,Y)
occurring in
\varphi
and
v\in V(G)
, we have
v\in[\![N^{\sigma}(X,Y)]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})}
if and only if either:

•

\mathbb{E}(v,(X,Y))<d_{\varphi}
and
\mathbb{E}(v,(X,Y))\in\sigma
, or

•

\mathbb{E}(v,(X,Y))=d_{\varphi}
and
\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}(v,C)+(d_{\varphi}\cdot d_{\varphi})\in\sigma
.

Now, we prove that
P\in\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{root})
, that is,
P
satisfies all the conditions of
Definition

5.11
.
By definition,
\mathbf{\hat{f}}
is a vertex interpretation on
V(G)
and
\mathbf{\hat{g}}
an edge interpretation on
E(G)
.
By construction,
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
is a neighborhood function on
V(G)
and
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
maps each
(X,Y)\in
to a simple subset of arcs from
\vec{E(G)}
so
(Domain-respecting)
is satisfied.
Now, for each
C=(X,Y)\in
, observe that:

•

By construction, for every
v\in V(G)
and
i\in[\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))]
, there is at least one arc in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(X,Y)
incoming into
v^{i}
; and for every
j\in[d_{\varphi}]
with
j>\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))
, there is no arc in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(X,Y)
incoming into
v^{j}
. Hence,
(Respect-subtree-

^
E

)
and
(Respect-broom-

^
E

)
are satisfied.

•

For every
uv\in E(G)
and every
X\in
, there exists
i\in[d_{\varphi}]
such that
(u^{1},v^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(X,Y)
if and only if
u\in U(v,C)
. Since
U(v,C)
is the set of all vertices
u\in\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
such that

uv\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
,
it follows that
(Edge-consistent)
is satisfied.

It follows that
P\in\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{root})
.
We need to prove that
P
belongs to
\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{root},I)
.
Since the tail of
\mathsf{root}
is empty,
(

f

-consistent)
,
(

E

-consistent)
,
(

pd

-consistent)
and
(Respect-

alw

)
are trivially satisfied by
P
.
We need now to prove that
(Respect-

F

)
is satisfied.
From
5.21
, we deduce that
[\![N^{\sigma}(X,Y)]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})}=[\![N^{\sigma}(X,Y)]\!]^{(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})}
for every neighborhood operator
N^{\sigma}(X,Y)
occurring in
\varphi
.
And for any neighborhood term
t
that is not a neighborhood operator, we have
[\![t]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})}=[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})}
simply by definition, and the same holds for every edge set term
t
.
Consequently, we have
\mathbf{\bot}(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})=\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})
.
From
(Realize-

⊥

)
, it follows that

\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})=\mathtt{F}

(2)

and thus
P
satisfies
(Respect-

F

)
.
This proves that
P
belongs to
\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{root},I)
.
Thanks to (
2
), we also know that
P
satisfies the equality requirement to be in
\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{root},I)
.
Since
(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})
satisfies
(Realize-

¯
c

)
, for each
i\in[\ell]
, we have that

[\![\lvert X_{i}\rvert=c^{i}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})}=1
, so consequently, we have
c_{\mathsf{root}}^{i}(P)=c^{i}
.
It follows that
P
belongs to
\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{root},I,a_{\mathsf{root}}(P),b_{\mathsf{root}}(P),\bar{c})
.

It remains to prove
a_{\mathsf{root}}(P)=b_{\mathsf{root}}(P)
.
For every vertex
v\in V(G)
and every
C\in
with
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)<d_{\varphi}
, we have
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)=\lvert U(v,C)\rvert
and by construction, there are exactly
\lvert U(v,C)\rvert
arcs in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
incoming into the vertices
v^{1},\dots,v^{d_{\varphi}}
. We conclude that

\displaystyle a_{\mathsf{root}}(P)=\sum_{C\in}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in V(G)\\
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)<d_{\varphi}\end{subarray}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(C))=\sum_{C\in}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in V(G)\\
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)<d_{\varphi}\end{subarray}}\lvert\{(u^{1},v^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)\}\rvert=b_{\mathsf{root}}(P).

∎

We now show that the converse of the above lemma holds as well, namely:

Lemma 5.22
.

Let
I=(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F})\in\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{root})
and let
\bar{c}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
.
Then, for every
(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{tss}(\mathsf{root}),I,a_{\mathsf{root}}(P),b_{\mathsf{root}}(P),\bar{c})
with
a_{\mathsf{root}}(P)=b_{\mathsf{root}}(P)
, the interpretation
(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})
of
\varphi
realizes
(\mathtt{F},\bar{c})
.

Proof.

First, let
c^{1},\ldots,c^{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\bar{c}=(c^{1},\dots,c^{\ell})\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
.
Also, let
P=(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
be a partial solution in
\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{root},I,a_{\mathsf{root}}(P),b_{\mathsf{root}}(P),\bar{c})
such that
a_{\mathsf{root}}(P)=b_{\mathsf{root}}(P)
.
By
Definition

5.15
, for every
i\in[\ell]
, we have
c^{i}_{\mathsf{tss}}(P)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\lvert[\![X_{i}]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}}_{|_{V(G)}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})}\rvert
.
As
\mathbf{\hat{f}}_{|_{V(G)}}=\mathbf{\hat{f}}
, it follows that
(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})
satisfies
(Realize-

¯
c

)
.

It remains to prove that
(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})
satisfies
(Realize-

⊥

)
, i.e.,
\mathbf{\bot}(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})=\mathtt{F}
.
As
P\in\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{root},I)
, we have
\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})=\mathtt{F}
, thus it is enough to prove that
\mathbf{\bot}(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})=\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})
.
To prove this, we now check that
[\![t]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})}=[\![t]\!]^{(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})}
holds for every neighborhood or edge term
t
occurring in
\varphi
.
By
Definition

5.5
this is trivially satisfied when
t
is not a neighborhood operator.
So we remain with the case that

t=N^{\sigma}(X,Y)
for some set
\sigma
occurring in
\varphi
and some
C=(X,Y)\in
, and this is why we need the following claim.

Claim 5.23
.

For each
v\in V(G)
and
C=(X,Y)\in
, the number of edges

uv\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)

with
u\in\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
is:

•

exactly
\lvert\{(u^{1},v^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)\}\rvert
,

•

exactly
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)
when
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)<d_{\varphi}
, and

•

at least
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)
when
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)=d_{\varphi}
.

Proof of the claim.

Let
v\in V(G)
and
(X,Y)\in
.
By
(Edge-consistent)
, for every edge
uv\in E(G)
, there exists an arc
(u^{1},v^{j})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(X,Y)
if and only if
u\in\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
and
uv\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
. As
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(X,Y)
is simple, we deduce that
\lvert\{(u^{1},v^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)\}\rvert
is exactly the number of edges
uv\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
with
u\in\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
.

Thanks to
(Respect-subtree-

^
E

)
, we know that for each vertex
v^{j}
with
j\leqslant\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))
, there exists an arc
(u^{1},v^{j})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(X,Y)
.
It follows that there are at least
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))
edges

uv\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)

with
u\in\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
.

It remains to prove that there are exactly
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))
of these edges when
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))<d_{\varphi}
.
This is where we use the fact that

\displaystyle a_{\mathsf{root}}(P)=\sum_{C\in}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in V(G)\\
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)<d_{\varphi}\end{subarray}}\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)=\sum_{C\in}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in V(G)\\
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)<d_{\varphi}\end{subarray}}\lvert\{(u^{1},v^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)\}\rvert=b_{\mathsf{root}}(P).

We already know that
\lvert\{(u^{1},v^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)\}\rvert\geqslant\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))
holds for every
v\in V(G)
and
(X,Y)\in
.
So the above equality can only hold when
\lvert\{(u^{1},v^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)\}\rvert=\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))
for every
v\in V(G)
and
(X,Y)\in
satisfying
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))<d_{\varphi}
.
And this concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

By
Definition

5.5
, a vertex
v
belongs to
[\![N^{\sigma}(X,Y)]\!]^{(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})}
iff one of the following holds:

•

\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))<d_{\varphi}
and
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))\in\sigma
, or

•

\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))=d_{\varphi}
and
\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}(v,(X,Y))+(d_{\varphi}\cdot p_{\varphi})\in\sigma
.

When
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))<d_{\varphi}
, by
5.23
, we get that
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))
is exactly the number of edges
uv\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
with
u\in\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
.
Consequently, when
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))<d_{\varphi}
, we have
v
belongs to
[\![N^{\sigma}(X,Y)]\!]^{(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})}
if and only if
v
belongs to
[\![N^{\sigma}(X,Y)]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})}
.

Now recall that by definition we have that
\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}(v,(X,Y))\equiv_{p_{\varphi}}\lvert\{(u^{1},v^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(X,Y)\}\rvert
.
By
5.23
,
\lvert\{(u^{1},v^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(X,Y)\}\rvert
is exactly the number of edges
uv\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)

with
u\in\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
.
Moreover, when
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))=d_{\varphi}
, this number is at least
d_{\varphi}
.
From
5.2
, we deduce that when
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,(X,Y))=d_{\varphi}
, then
v
belongs to
[\![N^{\sigma}(X,Y)]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})}
if and only if
\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}(v,(X,Y))+(d_{\varphi}\cdot p_{\varphi})\in\sigma
.
We conclude that
[\![N^{\sigma}(X,Y)]\!]^{(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})}=[\![N^{\sigma}(X,Y)]\!]^{(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})}
.
As discussed above, this implies that
(G,\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}})
realizes
(\mathtt{F},\bar{c})
and concludes this proof.
∎

We conclude this subsection by observing that
Lemmas

5.20
and
5.22
imply
Lemma

5.17
.

6
Recursive Equalities

In this section, we formally define the polynomials that our algorithm will evaluate as well as the recursive equalities on which our algorithm relies on: the root/leaf/forget/join equalities.
This is the technical core of our work.

6.1
The definitions of polynomials

Similarly as in
[
27
,
32
,
37
]
, we encode the different choices of
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bar{c}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
as degrees of formal variables
\alpha,\beta,\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}
, so that all the relevant values
\lvert\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss},I,a,b,\bar{c})\rvert
can be stored as coefficients of one polynomial from
\mathbb{Z}[\alpha,\beta,\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}]
.

Definition 6.1
.

For every triad
\mathsf{tss}
of
G
and every index
I=(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F})\in\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{tss})
, we define the polynomial
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{tss}}(I)
as follows:

\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{tss}}(I)=\sum_{a,b,c^{1},\dots,c^{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}}\lvert\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss},I,a,b,(c^{1},\dots,c^{\ell}))\rvert\cdot\alpha^{a}\beta^{b}\gamma_{1}^{c^{1}}\cdots\gamma_{\ell}^{c^{\ell}}.

Moreover, we define its equality variant
\mathcal{P}^{=}_{\mathsf{tss}}(I)
as follows:

\mathcal{P}^{=}_{\mathsf{tss}}(I)=\sum_{a,b,c^{1},\dots,c^{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}}\lvert\mathsf{Part}^{=}(\mathsf{tss},I,a,b,(c^{1},\dots,c^{\ell}))\rvert\cdot\alpha^{a}\beta^{b}\gamma_{1}^{c^{1}}\cdots\gamma_{\ell}^{c^{\ell}}.

Given
\bar{c}=(c^{1},\dots,c^{\ell})\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
, we denote by
\gamma^{\bar{c}}
the monomial
\prod_{i\in[\ell]}\gamma_{i}^{c^{i}}
.

The following lemma bounds the coefficients of these polynomials.
Recall that we assumed that
d_{\varphi}\geqslant 1
, this was done in order to simplify the following upper bounds.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
G
be a graph with at most
n\cdot\mathsf{td}
edges for some value
\mathsf{td}
. For every triad
\mathsf{tss}=(\mathsf{tail},\mathsf{subtree},\mathsf{sheaf})
of
G
and every index
I\in\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{tss})
, we have that every coefficient of the polynomial
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{tss}}(I)
is non-negative and upper-bounded by
(d_{\varphi}+1)^{5n\cdot\mathsf{td}\cdot|\varphi|}=2^{5n\cdot\mathsf{td}\cdot|\varphi|\cdot\log(d_{\varphi}+1)}
.

Proof.

Every partial solution is a quadruple so we bound the number of options for each of the four elements.
For this note that the cardinalities of the sets
\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
,
\mathsf{var}^{E}(\varphi)
, and are upper-bounded by
|\varphi|
.
The size of broom of
\mathsf{tss}
is upper-bounded by
n
and the size of
\mathsf{sheaf}
is upper-bounded by
\lvert E(G)\rvert\leqslant n\cdot\mathsf{td}
by
3.1
.
The first element is a vertex interpretation on
\mathsf{broom}
so there are at most
(2^{n})^{|\varphi|}=2^{n\cdot|\varphi|}
options for it.
Similarly, the second element is an edge interpretation on
\mathsf{sheaf}
so there are at most
(2^{n\cdot\mathsf{td}})^{|\varphi|}=2^{n\cdot\mathsf{td}\cdot|\varphi|}
options.
The third component is a neighborhood function on
\mathsf{broom}
so there are at most
((d_{\varphi}+1)^{n})^{|\varphi|}=(d_{\varphi}+1)^{n\cdot|\varphi|}
possibilities.
Finally, the fourth component maps every element of to a simple subset of
\vec{\mathsf{sheaf}}
, i.e., for every edge
uv\in\mathsf{sheaf}
, at most one arc of form
u^{1}v^{i}
and at most one arc of form
v^{1}u^{j}
belongs to this simple subset for some
i,j\in[d_{\varphi}]
.
Hence, there are at most
(((d_{\varphi}+1)^{2})^{n\cdot\mathsf{td}})^{|\varphi|}=(d_{\varphi}+1)^{2n\cdot\mathsf{td}\cdot|\varphi|}
options for the fourth element and the claim follows.
∎

For our algorithm we will strongly rely on the following polynomials:

Definition 6.3
.

Let
p
be a prime number such that
\mathbb{F}^{*}_{p}
admits the
p_{\varphi}
-th root of unity and let
\omega(p)
denote the smallest (with respect to the natural ordering of integers) such root of unity.
For every triad
\mathsf{tss}
of
G
and every index
I=(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F})\in\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{tss})
, we define the following polynomial

\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{\mathsf{tss}}(I)\equiv_{p}\sum_{q\in\mathtt{PD}(\mathsf{tail})}\omega(p)^{q\cdot\mathtt{pd}}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{tss}}(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},q,\mathtt{F})

(3)

from
\mathbb{F}_{p}[\alpha,\beta,\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}]
.

Note that if we fix all elements of the index apart from
\mathtt{pd}
, then this definition captures the discrete Fourier transform where the domain is
\mathtt{PD}(\mathsf{tail})=\mathsf{tail}\times
.

In the remainder of this section we provide the recursive equalities on which our algorithm relies on.
To this end, we fix an elimination tree
T
of
G
and let
\mathsf{td}
denote its depth.
The direct recursion relying on these equalities would result in an algorithm whose space complexity is polynomial in
n
.
In the next section we will show how, relying on the Chinese remainder theorem, to improve this dependence to be only logarithmic in
n
.
In particular, we will describe which (sufficiently short) prime numbers
p
are used.
Let us recall that for a node
u
of
T
the triads
\mathsf{tss}[u]
and
\mathsf{tss}(u)
are
(\mathsf{tail}[u],\mathsf{subtree}(u),\mathsf{sheaf}(u))
and
(\mathsf{tail}(u),\mathsf{subtree}[u],\mathsf{sheaf}(u))
, respectively.
We define the following shortcuts:

Definition 6.4
.

For every node
u
of
T
, we denote by
\mathsf{Index}(u)
and
\mathsf{Index}[u]
the sets
\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{tss}(u))
and
\mathsf{Index}(\mathsf{tss}[u])
, respectively.
Every notation in the form
\spadesuit_{\mathsf{tss}[u]}(\cdots)
is denoted by
\spadesuit_{u}[\cdots]
. For example, we have
\mathcal{P}_{u}[I]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{tss}[u]}(I)
,
\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{u}[I]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{\mathsf{tss}[u]}(I)
,
\mathsf{Part}_{u}(I)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathsf{Part}_{\mathsf{tss}[u]}(I)
, and
a_{u}[P]\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=a_{\mathsf{tss}[u]}(P)
.

Moreover, every notation in the form
\spadesuit_{\mathsf{tss}(u)}(\cdots)
is denoted by
\spadesuit_{u}(\cdots)
.
For example, we have
\mathcal{P}^{=}_{u}(I)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{P}^{=}_{\mathsf{tss}(u)}(I)
,
\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{u}(I)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{\mathsf{tss}(u)}(I)
, and
b_{u}(P)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=b_{\mathsf{tss}(u)}(P)
.

6.2
Root equalities

Recall that if the domain of some function is empty, we denote this function by
\emptyset
.
Similarly, we denote the unique vertex interpretation on the empty set by
\emptyset
.
Now for simplicity, by
\emptyset^{4}
we denote the tuple
(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset)
.
The following lemma reflects, for a set
\mathtt{F}\subseteq\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
, the relation between the partial solutions where only the equalities from
\mathtt{F}
are possibly “falsified” on the one hand, and the partial solutions where precisely the equalities from
\mathtt{F}
are falsified on the other hand.
In fact, the analogue of this lemma holds for any node of the elimination tree
T
but in our applications we will only use it for the root.

Lemma 6.5
.

For the root
r
of the elimination tree
T
and for every
\mathtt{F}\subseteq\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
, it holds that

\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F})=\sum_{\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathtt{F}}(-1)^{|\mathtt{F}^{\prime}|}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F}\setminus\mathtt{F}^{\prime}).

Proof.

The proof is almost a direct application of the inclusion-exclusion principle.
To see this formally, consider arbitrary but fixed
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bar{c}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
.
For an equality
x\in\mathtt{F}
we define the set

\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c},x)=\{(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c})\,:\,x\in\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})\}.

First, it holds that

\displaystyle\bigcap_{x\in\mathtt{F}}\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c},x)=\{(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c})\,:\,\mathtt{F}\subseteq\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})\}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c})\,:\,\mathtt{F}=\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})\}=\mathsf{Part}_{r}^{=}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c})

where the second equality holds since we have
\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})\subseteq\mathtt{F}
for every
(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
from
\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c})
due to
(Respect-

F

)
together with the fact that both broom and subtree of
\mathsf{tss}(r)
are equal to
V(G)
.
Then by inclusion-exclusion principle (
theorem

3.5
) we get

\displaystyle|\mathsf{Part}_{r}^{=}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c})|=\sum_{\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathtt{F}}(-1)^{|\mathtt{F}^{\prime}|}\cdot\lvert\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c})\setminus\Bigl(\bigcup_{x\in\mathtt{F}^{\prime}}\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c},x)\Bigr)\rvert

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathtt{F}}(-1)^{|\mathtt{F}^{\prime}|}\cdot\lvert\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F}\setminus\mathtt{F}^{\prime},a,b,\bar{c})\rvert

(4)

where the last equality holds due to

\displaystyle\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c})\setminus\Bigl(\bigcup_{x\in\mathtt{F}^{\prime}}\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c},x)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\{(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c})\,:\,x\not\in\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})\text{ for all }x\in\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},a,b,\bar{c})\,:\,\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})\subseteq\mathtt{F}\setminus\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\}=\mathsf{Part}_{r}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F}\setminus\mathtt{F}^{\prime},a,b,\bar{c})

for every
\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathtt{F}
.
Since (
4
) holds for arbitrary
a,b,\bar{c}
, the lemma follows.
∎

Now that we know how to compute the polynomials
\mathcal{P}_{r}^{=}(I)
from the polynomials
\mathcal{P}_{r}(I)
. Observe that given the polynomials
\mathcal{P}_{r}^{=}(I)
, we can decide whether
G\models\varphi
thanks to
Lemma

5.17
.
In the remainder of the section, we prove that
\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{r}(I)\equiv_{p}\mathcal{P}_{r}(I)
for every index
I
. This follows from the fact that
\mathsf{tail}(r)
is empty and thus the DFT-transformation is the identity function (modulo
p
).

Lemma 6.6
.

For the root
r
of the elimination tree
T
and for every prime number
p
such that
\mathbb{F}^{*}_{p}
admits the
p_{\varphi}
-th root of unity and every index
I=(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F})\in\mathsf{Index}(r)
, it holds that
\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{r}(I)\equiv_{p}\mathcal{P}_{r}(I)
.

Proof.

It holds that
\mathsf{tail}(r)=\emptyset
and thus also
\mathsf{tail}(r)\times
is empty.
So the set
\mathtt{PD}(\mathsf{tail}(r))=(\mathbb{Z}_{p_{\varphi}})^{\mathsf{tail}(r)\times}=\mathbb{Z}_{p_{\varphi}}^{\emptyset}
consists of precisely one mapping whose domain is empty.
Since
\mathtt{pd}
is such a mapping, we have
\mathtt{PD}(\mathsf{tail}(r))=\{\mathtt{pd}\}
.
Thus we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{r}(I)

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{\mathsf{tss}(r)}(I)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:def-q}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}\sum_{q\in\mathtt{PD}(\mathsf{tail}(r))}\Bigl(\prod_{(v,C)\in\mathsf{tail}(r)\times}\omega(p)^{q(v,C)\cdot\mathtt{pd}(v,C)}\Bigr)\cdot\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{tss}(r)}(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},q,\mathtt{F})

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}\sum_{q\in\mathtt{PD}(\mathsf{tail}(r))}1\cdot\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{tss}(r)}(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},q,\mathtt{F})\equiv_{p}\sum_{q\in\{\mathtt{pd}\}}1\cdot\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{tss}(r)}(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},q,\mathtt{F})

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{tss}(r)}(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F})\equiv_{p}\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{tss}(r)}(I)\equiv_{p}\mathcal{P}_{r}(I).

∎

So now we may restrict our considerations to the computation DFT-transformed polynomials
\mathcal{Q}_{u}(I)
and
\mathcal{Q}_{u}[I]
for all the nodes
u
of
T
.
To this end, we will sometime prove some equalities about the polynomials
\mathcal{P}_{u}[I]
and
\mathcal{P}_{u}(I)
as intermediary steps in our proofs.

6.3
Leaf equality

We start with the equality for the non-recursive computation at the leaves of
T
:

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
u
be a leaf of
T
and let
I=(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F})\in\mathsf{Index}[u]
.
Further, let
p
be a prime number such that
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{*}
admits the
p_{\varphi}
-th root of unity.
Then it holds that:

\mathcal{Q}_{u}^{p}[I]\equiv_{p}\prod_{e=vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)}\sum_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}\in\mathbb{G}(e,\mathtt{F})}\gamma^{\bar{c}_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}}\cdot\prod_{(X,Y)\in}L(v,w,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y))\cdot L(w,v,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y))

where

\mathbb{G}(e,\mathtt{F})=\{\mathbf{\hat{g}}\,:\,\mathbf{\hat{g}}\text{ is an edge interpretation on }\{e\}\text{ and }\mathbf{\bot}^{E}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})\subseteq\mathtt{F}\},

\bar{c}_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}=(c_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}^{1},\dots,c_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}^{\ell})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}
with
c_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}^{i}=|\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(X_{i})|
if
X_{i}\in\mathsf{var}^{E}(\varphi)
and
c_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}^{i}=0
if
X\in\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
for all
i\in[\ell]
, and

L(v,w,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y))=\begin{cases}\mathtt{alw}(w,(X,Y))\cdot\omega(p)^{\mathtt{pd}(w,(X,Y))}\cdot\beta^{[\mathbb{E}(w,(X,Y))<d_{\varphi}]}&\!\!\text{if }v\in\mathbf{f}(X)\text{\! and }e\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(Y)\\
1&\!\!\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

Since
u
is a leaf, we have
\mathsf{tss}[u]=(\mathsf{tail}[u],\emptyset,\mathsf{sheaf}(u))
and the broom of
\mathsf{tss}[u]
is equal to
\mathsf{tail}[u]
, we will sometimes use implicitly these facts in this proof.
Before we start to reformulate
\mathcal{Q}_{u}^{p}[I]
, we need the following notations and a claim.

Given
e=vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
and an edge interpretation
\mathbf{\hat{g}}
of
\varphi
on
\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
, we denote by
\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}
the function
\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{|_{\{e\}}}
.
We define
\mathbb{G}(\mathtt{F})
as the set of all edge interpretations
\mathbf{\hat{g}}
of
\varphi
on
\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
such that
\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}\in\mathbb{G}(e,\mathtt{F})
for every
e\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
.
Moreover, we denote by
\vec{\mathbb{W}}
the set of all functions
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
that maps each element from to a simple subset of arcs from
\vec{\mathsf{sheaf}}(u)
.
Given
\vec{\mathcal{W}}\in\vec{\mathbb{W}}
and
C\in
, we define
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C,v,w)
as the intersection between
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
and the set of arcs
\{(v^{1},w^{i})\,:\,i\in[d_{\varphi}]\}
.
Since
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
is a simple set of arcs, it follows that
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C,v,w)
contains at most one arc.
Furthermore, we define

\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y),v,w)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\begin{cases}\{\{(v^{1},w^{i})\}\,:\,i\leqslant\mathtt{alw}(w,C)\}&\text{ if }v\in\mathbf{f}(X)\text{ and }e\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(Y)\\
\{\emptyset\}&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Finally, we define
\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})
as the set of all
\vec{\mathcal{W}}\in\vec{\mathbb{W}}
such that
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C,v,w)\in\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},C,v,w)
for every
C\in
and pair
(v,w)
such that
e=vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
.

Claim 6.8
.

For every
y\in\mathtt{PD}(\mathsf{tail}[u])
, we have
(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[I[\mathtt{pd}\to y]]
if and only if the following conditions are satisfied:

•

\mathbf{\hat{f}}=\mathbf{f}
,

•

\hat{\mathbb{E}}=\mathbb{E}
,

•

y=\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}
,

•

\mathbf{\hat{g}}\in\mathbb{G}(\mathtt{F})
,

•

\vec{\mathcal{W}}\in\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})
.

Proof of the claim.

Let
(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[I[\mathtt{pd}\to y]]
for some
y\in\mathtt{PD}(\mathsf{tail}[u])
.
Since the broom of
\mathsf{tss}[u]
is
\mathsf{tail}[u]
, it holds that
\mathbf{\hat{f}}=\mathbf{f}
,
\hat{\mathbb{E}}=\mathbb{E}
and
y=\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}
by
(

f

-consistent)
,
(

E

-consistent)
and
(

pd

-consistent)
.
Consider an arbitrary edge
e=vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
.
Then by
lemma

5.8
, we have
\mathbf{\bot}^{E}(\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e})\subseteq\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{f}_{|_{\emptyset}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\mathbb{E}_{|_{\emptyset}},(\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})_{|_{\emptyset}})\subseteq\mathtt{F}
where the last inclusion holds due to
(Respect-

F

)
.
So we have
\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}\in\mathbb{G}(e,\mathtt{F})
.
As it holds for any
e\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
, we deduce that
\mathbf{\hat{g}}\in\mathbb{G}(\mathtt{F})
.
Let
C=(X,Y)\in
.
Due to
(Edge-consistent)
, if
v\notin\mathbf{f}(X)
or
e\notin\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
, then we have
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C,v,w)=\emptyset
and otherwise, there exists an arc
(v^{1},w^{j})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
.
In the latter case, since
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
is simple and due to
(Respect-

alw

)
, such a value
j
is unique and satisfies
1\leqslant j\leqslant\mathtt{alw}(w,(X,Y))
.
Now observe that
e\notin\mathbf{\hat{g}}(Y)
is equivalent to
e\notin\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(Y)
. Altogether we deduce that
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C,v,w)\in\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},C,v,w)
and we conclude that
\vec{\mathcal{W}}\in\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})
.

For the other direction let
\mathbf{\hat{g}}\in\mathbb{G}(\mathtt{F})
and
\vec{\mathcal{W}}\in\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})
with
y=\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}
.
First, we argue that
P=(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\mathbb{E},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
is a partial solution for
\mathsf{tss}[u]
.
Observe that
P
trivially satisfies
(Domain-respecting)
.
Further, by definition of
\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})
, for every
(X,Y)\in
and edge
e=vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
, we have
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C,v,w)=\{(v^{1},w^{i})\}
(for some
i\in[d_{\varphi}]
) if and only if
v\in\mathbf{f}(X)
and
e\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(e)
. Since
e\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(e)
is equivalent to
e\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}(e)
, we deduce that
P
satisfies
(Edge-consistent)
.
Furthermore, for every
w\in\mathsf{tail}[u]
and
C\in
, if
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
contains an arc
(v^{1},w^{i})
, then by definition of
\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y),v,w)
, we have
i\leqslant\mathtt{alw}(w,C)
.
Since
I
is an index, we have
\mathtt{alw}\leqslant\mathbb{E}
, and thus we have
i\leqslant\mathbb{E}(w,C)
as required by
(Respect-broom-

^
E

)
.
Finally,
P
trivially satisfies
(Respect-subtree-

^
E

)
because
\mathsf{subtree}(u)
is empty. It follows that
P
is indeed a partial solution for
\mathsf{tss}[u]
.

Now it remains to prove that
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[I[\mathtt{pd}\mapsto\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}]]
.
The conditions
(

f

-consistent)
,
(

E

-consistent)
, and
(

pd

-consistent)
are trivially satisfied.
As mentioned above, for every
C\in
and arc
(v^{1},w^{j})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
, we have
j\leqslant\mathtt{alw}(w,C)
, so
(Respect-

alw

)
is satisfied.
The set
\mathsf{subtree}(u)
is empty so
\mathbf{\bot}^{V}(\mathbf{f}_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}(u)}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\mathbb{E}_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}(u)}},(\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}(u)}})=\emptyset\subseteq\mathtt{F}
.
And finally we have

\mathbf{\bot}^{E}(\mathbf{f}_{|_{\emptyset}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\mathbb{E}_{|_{\emptyset}},(\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})_{|_{\emptyset}})=\mathbf{\bot}^{E}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{claim:union-false-set}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{e\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)}\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e})\subseteq\mathtt{F}

where the last inclusion holds due to
\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}\in\mathbb{G}(e,\mathtt{F})
for all
e\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)
so
(Respect-

F

)
holds.
Hence,
P
is indeed compatible with
I[\mathtt{pd}\mapsto\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}]
.
∎

We are now ready to start our reformulation of
\mathcal{Q}_{u}^{p}[I]
.
In the rest of the proof
\omega
denotes
\omega(p)
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{u}^{p}[I]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:def-q}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}\sum_{y\in\mathtt{PD}(\mathsf{tail}[u])}\omega^{\mathtt{pd}\cdot y}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{u}[I[\mathtt{pd}\mapsto y]]

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in\mathtt{PD}(\mathsf{tail}[u])\\
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[I[\mathtt{pd}\to y]]\end{subarray}}\omega^{\mathtt{pd}\cdot y}\cdot\alpha^{a_{u}[P]}\cdot\beta^{b_{u}[P]}\cdot\gamma^{\bar{c}_{u}[P]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{claim:leaf:characterization}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}\sum_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}\in\mathbb{G}(\mathtt{F})}\sum_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}\in\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})}\omega^{\mathtt{pd}\cdot\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}}\cdot\alpha^{a_{u}[(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\mathbb{E},\vec{\mathcal{W}})]}\cdot\beta^{b_{u}[(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\mathbb{E},\vec{\mathcal{W}})]}\cdot\gamma^{\bar{c}_{u}[(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\mathbb{E},\vec{\mathcal{W}})]}.

(5)

The following claim will help us to reformulate the inner product in (
5
).

Claim 6.9
.

Let
P=(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\mathbb{E},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
with
\mathbf{\hat{g}}\in\mathbb{G}(\mathtt{F})
and
\vec{\mathcal{W}}\in\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})
.
Then, we have
a_{u}[P]=0
,

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{u}[P]

\displaystyle=\sum_{e\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}},

\displaystyle b_{u}[P]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,w)\colon\\
vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)\end{subarray}}\sum_{(X,Y)\in}[v\in\mathbf{f}(X)]\cdot[e\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(Y)]\cdot[\mathbb{E}(w,(X,Y))<d_{\varphi}],\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathtt{pd}\cdot\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}

\displaystyle\equiv_{p_{\varphi}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,w)\colon\\
vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)\end{subarray}}\sum_{(X,Y)\in}[v\in\mathbf{f}(X)]\cdot[e\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(Y)]\cdot\mathtt{pd}(w,C).

Proof of the claim.

Since
\mathsf{subtree}(u)=\emptyset
, by definition, we have
a_{u}[P]=0
.
Let
i\in[\ell]
.
If
X_{i}\in\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
, then we have
c^{i}_{u}[P]=\lvert\mathbf{f}(X_{i})\cap\mathsf{subtree}(u)\rvert=0
because
\mathsf{subtree}(u)=\emptyset
.
On the other hand, if
X_{i}\in\mathsf{var}^{E}(\varphi)
, then
c^{i}_{u}[P]=\lvert\mathbf{\hat{g}}(X_{i})\rvert
. Since
\lvert\mathbf{\hat{g}}(X_{i})\rvert=\sum_{e\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)}\lvert\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(X_{i})\rvert
, we deduce that
\bar{c}_{u}[P]=\sum_{e\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}
.
Now, notice that
b_{u}[P]
can be reformulated as follows:

\displaystyle b_{u}[P]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{C\in}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in\mathsf{broom}[u]\colon\\
\mathbb{E}(w,C)<d_{\varphi}\end{subarray}}\lvert\{(v^{1},w^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)\}\rvert=\sum_{C\in}\sum_{(v^{1},w^{i})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)}[\mathbb{E}(w,C)<d_{\varphi}].

Now, observe that by definition, for every
C\in
, we have

\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)=\bigcup_{{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,w)\colon\\
vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)\end{subarray}}}\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C,v,w).

Moreover, by
6.8
and the definition of
\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y),v,w)
, we have

\lvert\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C,v,w)\rvert=[v\in\mathbf{f}(X)]\cdot[e\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(Y)].

We deduce that the equality with
b_{u}[P]
holds.
It remains to prove the equality with
\mathtt{pd}\cdot\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}
.
Remember that by definition, we have

\mathtt{pd}\cdot\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\sum_{(w,C)\in\mathsf{tail}[u]\times}\mathtt{pd}(w,C)\cdot\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}(w,C)\pmod{p_{\varphi}}.

Moreover, we have
\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}(w,C)\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\lvert\{(z^{1},w^{j})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)\,:\,z\in\mathsf{broom}[u]\}\rvert\pmod{p_{\varphi}}
.
We deduce that

\mathtt{pd}\cdot\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}\equiv_{p_{\varphi}}\sum_{C\in}\sum_{(v^{1},w^{j})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)}\mathtt{pd}(w,C).

With the same arguments used for the equality with
b_{u}[P]
, we conclude that the equality with
\mathtt{pd}\cdot\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}
holds as well.
∎

Consider again
P=(\mathbf{f},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\mathbb{E},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
with
\mathbf{\hat{g}}\in\mathbb{G}(\mathtt{F})
and
\vec{\mathcal{W}}\in\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})
.
Recall that
\omega
is the
p_{\varphi}
-th root of unity in
\mathbb{F}^{*}_{p}
so in particular, we have
\omega^{p_{\varphi}}\equiv_{p}1
, we deduce from
6.9
that

\omega^{\mathtt{pd}\cdot\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}}\equiv_{p}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,w)\colon\\
vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)\end{subarray}}\prod_{C\in}\omega^{\mathtt{pd}(w,C)\cdot[v\in\mathbf{f}(X)]\cdot[e\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(Y)]}.

Again from
6.9
, it follows that

\beta^{b_{u}[P]}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(v,w)\colon\\
vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)\end{subarray}}\prod_{C\in}\beta^{[v\in\mathbf{f}(X)]\cdot[e\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(Y)]\cdot[\mathbb{E}(w,C)<d_{\varphi}]}\text{ and }\gamma^{\bar{c}_{u}[P]}=\prod_{e\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)}\bar{c}_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\omega^{\mathtt{pd}\cdot\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}}\cdot\alpha^{a_{u}[P]}\cdot\beta^{b_{u}[P]}\cdot\gamma^{\bar{c}_{u}[P]}

\displaystyle=\prod_{e=vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)}\gamma^{\bar{c}_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}}\prod_{(X,Y)\in}L^{\prime}(v,w,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y))\cdot L^{\prime}(w,v,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y)),

\displaystyle\text{where }L^{\prime}(v,w,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y))

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\omega^{\mathtt{pd}(w,(X,Y))}\cdot\beta^{[\mathbb{E}(w,(X,Y))<d_{\varphi}]}&\text{ if }v\in\mathbf{f}(X)\text{ and }e\in\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}(Y)\\
1&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

By plugging the above in (
5
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{u}^{p}[I]

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}\sum_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}\in\mathbb{G}(\mathtt{F})}\sum_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}\in\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})}\prod_{e=vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)}\gamma^{\bar{c}_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}}\prod_{(X,Y)\in}L^{\prime}(v,w,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y))\cdot L^{\prime}(w,v,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y))

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}\sum_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}\in\mathbb{G}(\mathtt{F})}\lvert\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})\rvert\cdot\prod_{e=vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)}\gamma^{\bar{c}_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}}\prod_{(X,Y)\in}L^{\prime}(v,w,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y))\cdot L^{\prime}(w,v,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y)).

By definition of
\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})
, we have

\lvert\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}})\rvert=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e=vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)\\
C\in\end{subarray}}\lvert\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},C,v,w)\rvert\cdot\lvert\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},C,w,v)\rvert.

Since
\lvert\vec{\mathbb{W}}(\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},C,v,w)\rvert\cdot L^{\prime}(v,w,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y))=L(v,w,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y))
, it follows that

\mathcal{Q}_{u}^{p}[I]\equiv_{p}\sum_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}\in\mathbb{G}(\mathtt{F})}\prod_{e=vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)}\gamma^{\bar{c}_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}}\prod_{(X,Y)\in}L(v,w,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y))\cdot L(w,v,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y)).\\

From the definition of
\mathbb{G}(\mathtt{F})
, we conclude that

\mathcal{Q}_{u}^{p}[I]\equiv_{p}\prod_{e=vw\in\mathsf{sheaf}(u)}\sum_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}\in\mathbb{G}(e,\mathtt{F})}\gamma^{\bar{c}_{\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e}}}\prod_{(X,Y)\in}L(v,w,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y))\cdot L(w,v,\mathbf{\hat{g}}_{e},(X,Y)).\\

∎

6.4
Forget equality

Next we prove an equality reflecting the relation between the partial solutions of
\mathsf{tss}(u)
and of
\mathsf{tss}[u]
for a node
u
:

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
u
be a node of
T
and let
I=(\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw},\mathtt{pd},\mathtt{F})\in\mathsf{Index}(u)
.
Let
\mathcal{J}
denote the set of all tuples
(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})
such that the following hold:

•

\mathbf{f}_{u}
is a vertex interpretation on
\{u\}
,

•

\mathbb{E}_{u}
is a neighborhood function on
\{u\}
,

•

\mathtt{pd}_{u}
is a mod-degree on
\{u\}
, and

•

\mathbf{\bot}^{V}(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})\subseteq\mathtt{F}
.

And let
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
be the set of all tuples
(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{alw}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})
such that
(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})\in\mathcal{J}
and
\mathtt{alw}_{u}
is a neighborhood function on
\{u\}
such that
\mathtt{alw}_{u}\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{u}
.
Given,
J=(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{alw}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
, we denote by
\mathbf{f}_{u}^{+},\mathbb{E}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{alw}_{u}^{+}
and
\mathtt{pd}_{u}^{+}
the functions
\mathbf{f}\cup\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}\cup\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{alw}\cup\mathtt{alw}_{u}
, and
\mathtt{pd}\cup\mathtt{pd}_{u}
, respectively.
Then we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{u}(I)=\sum_{(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{alw}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}S_{\pm}(\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{alw}_{u})\cdot\mathcal{P}_{u}[\mathbf{f}_{u}^{+},\mathbb{E}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{alw}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{pd}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{F}]\cdot\alpha^{a_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}}\cdot\gamma^{\bar{c}_{\mathbf{f}_{u}}},

(6)

with

S_{\pm}(\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{alw}_{u})=\prod_{C\in}\left((-1)^{\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C)-\mathtt{alw}_{u}(u,C)}\cdot\binom{\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C)}{\mathtt{alw}_{u}(u,C)}\right),\quad a_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}C\in\colon\\
\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C)<d_{\varphi}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C),\text{ and }

\bar{c}_{\mathbf{f}_{u}}=(c^{1}_{\mathbf{f}_{u}},\dots,c^{\ell}_{\mathbf{f}_{u}})\in\{0,1\}^{\ell}\text{ such that }c^{i}_{\mathbf{f}_{u}}=\lvert\mathbf{f}_{u}(X_{i})\rvert\text{ if }X_{i}\in\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi),\text{ and }c^{i}_{\mathbf{f}_{u}}=0\text{ if }X_{i}\in\mathsf{var}^{E}(\varphi)

for every
i\in[\ell]
.
Further, for every prime number
p
such that
\mathbb{F}^{*}_{p}
admits a
p_{\varphi}
-th root of unity it holds that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{u}(I)\equiv_{p}

\displaystyle\sum_{(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{alw}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})\in\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}S_{\pm}(\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{alw}_{u})\cdot\alpha^{a_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}}\cdot\gamma^{\bar{c}_{\mathbf{f}_{u}}}\cdot

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p_{\varphi}^{||}}\sum_{\hat{q}\in\mathtt{PD}(\{u\})}\omega(p)^{-\hat{q}\cdot\mathtt{pd}_{u}}\cdot\mathcal{Q}^{p}_{u}[\mathbf{f}_{u}^{+},\mathbb{E}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{alw}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{pd}\cup\hat{q},\mathtt{F}]

(7)

where
\frac{1}{p_{\varphi}^{\lvert\rvert}}
is the multiplicative inverse of
p_{\varphi}^{\lvert\rvert}
in
\mathbb{F}_{p}
.

Proof.

We start by proving the “non-DFT-transformed” equality (
6
).
Before we proceed with a formal proof, we provide an intuition behind it.
In essence, this equality is the application of the inclusion-exclusion principle.
Recall that we have
\mathsf{subtree}[u]=\mathsf{subtree}(u)\dot{\cup}\{u\}
whilst the triads
\mathsf{tss}(u)
and
\mathsf{tss}[u]
share the same broom the same sheaf.
Now the following observations are crucial.
Roughly, the partial solutions of
\mathsf{tss}[u]
and
\mathsf{tss}(u)
differ “only” on two aspects: in the partial solutions of
\mathsf{tss}(u)
, the equalities “falsified by
u
” have to belong to
\mathtt{F}
as well (due to
(Respect-

F

)
), and second, due to
(Respect-subtree-

^
E

)
, the sets in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
have to contain arcs incoming into certain vertices from
u^{1},\dots,u^{d_{\varphi}}
.
The definition of
\mathcal{J}
ensures that
(Respect-

F

)
is satisfied on the vertex
u
.
Thus, when we fix the tuple
(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})\in\mathcal{J}
the computation of the desired cardinality of the set of partial solutions of
\mathsf{tss}(u)
boils down to ensuring that
(Respect-subtree-

^
E

)
holds for
u
.

To simplify the explanation in this sketch, assume that contains only one element
C
.
Thus, we have to ensure that for every
j\in[\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C)]
, the set
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
contains an arc incoming into
u^{j}
.
So if we denote by
P_{j}
the set of partial solutions of
\mathsf{tss}[u]
where
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
contains an arc incoming into
u^{j}
, then what we seek is the value
|\bigcap_{j\in[\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C)]}P_{j}|
.
By inclusion-exclusion principle (see
theorem

3.5
), we can compute this cardinality by considering all possible
S\subseteq[\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C)]
and determining the cardinality of the set of partial solutions where we
forbid
the edges incoming into
u^{j}
for all
j\in S
.
Finally, we observe that for fixed
S
, due to symmetries, in terms of counting, this is the same as to forbid the edges incoming into
u^{\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C)-|S|+1},\dots,u^{\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C)}
, i.e., we can use the function
\mathtt{alw}_{u}
with
\mathtt{alw}_{u}(u,C)=\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C)-|S|
.
Now we formalize this idea.

Given a neighborhood function
\mathbb{E}_{u}
on
\{u\}
, we define
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=\{(C,j)\,:\,C\in,j\in[\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C)]\}.

For every
\mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}
and
J=(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})\in\mathcal{J}
, we define the set
\mathsf{Part}_{u}[J,\mathcal{T}]
of all
P=(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[\mathbf{f}_{u}^{+},\mathbb{E}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{alw}\cup\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{F}]

such that for every
(C,j)\in\mathcal{T}
, there is at least one arc in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
incoming into
u^{j}
.
Notice that we use
\mathtt{alw}\cup\mathbb{E}_{u}
here, because it gives us the least restrictive index of
\mathsf{tss}[u]
in the sense that for every neighborhood function
\mathtt{alw}_{u}
on
\{u\}
such that
\mathtt{alw}_{u}\leqslant\mathbb{E}_{u}
, we have by
5.14
the following inclusion

\mathsf{Part}_{u}[\mathbf{f}_{u}^{+},\mathbb{E}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{alw}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{pd}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{F}]\subseteq\mathsf{Part}_{u}[\mathbf{f}_{u}^{+},\mathbb{E}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{alw}\cup\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{F}].

Given
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bar{c}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
, we also define the set
\mathsf{Part}_{u}[J,\mathcal{T},a,b,\bar{c}]
of all
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[J,\mathcal{T}]
such that
a_{u}[P]=a
,
b_{u}[P]=b
and
\bar{c}_{u}[P]=\bar{c}
.
We aim to prove with the following claims that we have

\mathcal{P}_{u}(I)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})\in\mathcal{J}\\
a,b\in\mathbb{N},\bar{c}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\end{subarray}}\lvert\mathsf{Part}_{u}[\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u},\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{E}_{u}},a,b,\bar{c}]\rvert\cdot\alpha^{a+a_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}}\cdot\beta^{b}\cdot\gamma^{\bar{c}+\bar{c}_{\mathbf{f}_{u}}}.

(8)

We will see later how to prove (
6
) from (
8
) with the inclusion-exclusion principle.

Claim 6.11
.

For every
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}(I)
, there is a unique
J\in\mathcal{J}
such that
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[J,\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}]
.

Proof of the claim.

Let
P=(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}(I)
.
Let
\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u}
and
\mathtt{pd}_{u}
be the restrictions of
\mathbf{\hat{f}},\hat{\mathbb{E}}
and
\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}
on
\{u\}
.
First, observe that
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}(I)
and
(Respect-

F

)
imply the inclusion

\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}}_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}[u]}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}}_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}[u]}},(\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}[u]}})\subseteq\mathtt{F}.

(9)

By applying
Lemma

5.8
on (
9
) with
\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}=\{u\}
and
\mathsf{dom}=\mathsf{subtree}[u]
, we deduce the inclusion

\mathbf{\bot}^{V}(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})\subseteq\mathbf{\bot}^{V}(\mathbf{\hat{f}}_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}[u]}},\hat{\mathbb{E}}_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}[u]}},(\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}[u]}})\subseteq\mathtt{F}.

Thus, we have
J=(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})\in\mathcal{J}
.
Next, we argue that
P\in\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss}[u])
holds because we have
P\in\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss}(u))
.
Indeed, since each of the broom and sheaf is the same for
\mathsf{tss}(u)
and
\mathsf{tss}[u]
, the quadruple
P
satisfies
(Domain-respecting)
,
(Edge-consistent)
, and
(Respect-broom-

^
E

)
for
\mathsf{tss}[u]
too.
Moreover, the subtree of
\mathsf{tss}[u]
(i.e.,
\mathsf{subtree}(u)
) is a subset of the subtree of
\mathsf{tss}(u)
(i.e.,
\mathsf{subtree}[u]
), consequently
P
satisfies
(Respect-subtree-

^
E

)
for
\mathsf{tss}[u]
. Thus, we have
P\in\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss}[u])
.

Now, we show that
P
is compatible with
J^{\prime}=(\mathbf{f}_{u}^{+},\mathbb{E}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{alw}\cup\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{F})
.

•

Recall that
\mathbf{f}_{u}^{+}=\mathbf{f}\cup\mathbf{f}_{u}
and
\mathbf{f}_{u}
is the restriction of
\hat{\mathbf{f}}
on
\{u\}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{f}(X)=\hat{\mathbf{f}}(X)\cap\mathsf{tail}(u)
for every
X\in\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
because
P
is compatible with
I
.
We deduce that
P
satisfies
(

f

-consistent)
for
J^{\prime}
because for every
X\in\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
, we have:

\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)\cap\mathsf{tail}[u]=(\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)\cap\mathsf{tail}(u))\cup(\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)\cap\{u\})=\mathbf{f}(X)\cup\mathbf{f}_{u}(X)=\mathbf{f}^{+}_{u}(X).

•

Recall that
\mathbb{E}_{u}^{+}=\mathbb{E}\cup\mathbb{E}_{u}
. Since
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}(I)
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{u}^{+}(v,C)=\mathbb{E}(v,C)=\hat{\mathbb{E}}(v,C)
for every
v\in\mathsf{tail}(u)
and
C\in
.
As
\mathbb{E}_{u}
is the restriction of
\hat{\mathbb{E}}
on
\{u\}
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{u}^{+}(u,C)=\hat{\mathbb{E}}(u,C)
for every
C\in
. Hence,
P
satisfies
(

E

-consistent)
for
J^{\prime}
. With the same arguments, we can prove that
P
satisfies
(

pd

-consistent)
for
J^{\prime}
.

•

Since
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}(I)
, we know that it satisfies
(Respect-

alw

)
for
J^{\prime}
and every vertex in
\mathsf{tail}(u)
. Moreover,
P
satisfying
(Respect-broom-

^
E

)
(for
\mathsf{tss}(u)
and
\mathsf{tss}[u]
) implies that for every
C\in
and every arc
(v^{1},u^{j})\in\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
, we have
j\leqslant\hat{\mathbb{E}}(u,C)
. As
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(u,C)=\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C)=(\mathtt{alw}\cup\mathbb{E}_{u})(u,C)
, we deduce that
P
satisfies
(Respect-

alw

)
for
J^{\prime}
and the vertex
u
as well.

•

By applying
Lemma

5.8
on (
9
) with
\mathsf{dom}^{\prime}=\mathsf{subtree}(u)
and
\mathsf{dom}=\mathsf{subtree}[u]
, we obtain

\mathbf{\bot}(\mathbf{\hat{f}}_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}(u)}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}}_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}(u)}},(\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})_{|_{\mathsf{subtree}(u)}})\subseteq\mathtt{F}.

It follows that
P
satisfies
(Respect-

F

)
for
J^{\prime}
.

Hence, we have
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[J^{\prime}]
.

Now, we need to prove that
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[J,\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}]
, i.e., for each
(C,j)\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}
, there is at least one arc in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
incoming into
u^{j}
.
Since
P\in\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss}(u))
, from
(Respect-subtree-

^
E

)
, it follows that for every
w\in\mathsf{subtree}[u]
, every
C\in
, and every
j\in[\hat{\mathbb{E}}(w,C)]
, there is at least one arc in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
incoming into
w^{j}
. As
u\in\mathsf{subtree}[u]
, we thus have
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[J,\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}]
.

Finally, it is easy to see that
J
is the unique element of
\mathcal{J}
such that
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[J,\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}]
because of the conditions
(

f

-consistent)
,
(

E

-consistent)
and
(

pd

-consistent)
.
∎

Claim 6.12
.

The collection
(\mathsf{Part}_{u}[J,\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}])_{J\in\mathcal{J}}
is a partition of
\mathsf{Part}_{u}(I)
.

Proof of the claim.

Thanks to
6.11
, to prove this statement, it is sufficient to prove that for every
J\in\mathcal{J}
and
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[J,\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}]
, we have
P\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}(I)
.
Let
J=(\mathbf{f}_{u},\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u})\in\mathcal{J}
and
P=(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})\in\mathsf{Part}_{u}[J,\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}]
.

We start by proving that
P\in\mathsf{Part}(\mathsf{tss}(u))
.
Observe that
P
satisfies
(Domain-respecting)
and
(Edge-consistent)
,
(Respect-broom-

^
E

)
for
\mathsf{tss}(u)
because
P
satisfies these properties for
\mathsf{tss}[u]
and
\mathsf{tss}(u)
and
\mathsf{tss}[u]
have the same broom and sheaf.
It remains to check that
P
satisfies
(Respect-subtree-

^
E

)
for
\mathsf{tss}(u)
.
Observe that
P
satisfies this condition for
\mathsf{tss}[u]
and we have
\mathsf{subtree}(u)=\mathsf{subtree}[u]\setminus\{u\}
.
So it remains to prove that, for every
C\in
and every
j\in[\hat{\mathbb{E}}(u,C)]
, there is at least one arc incoming into
u^{j}
in
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
.
Since
\hat{\mathbb{E}}(u,C)=\mathbb{E}_{u}(u,C)
, this follows from the definition of
\mathsf{Part}_{u}[J,\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{E}_{u}}]
.

It remains to prove that
P
is compatible with
I
. Let
J^{\prime}=(\mathbf{f}_{u}^{+},\mathbb{E}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{alw}\cup\mathbb{E}_{u},\mathtt{pd}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{F})
.

•

Observe that
\mathbf{f},\mathbb{E},\mathtt{alw}
and
\mathtt{pd}
are the restrictions of
\mathbf{f}_{u}^{+},\mathbb{E}_{u}^{+},\mathtt{alw}\cup\mathbb{E}_{u}
and
\mathtt{pd}_{u}^{+}
on
\mathsf{tail}(u)
.
Since
\mathsf{tail}(u)\subseteq\mathsf{tail}[u]
, we deduce that
P
satisfies
(

f

-consistent)
,
(

E

-consistent)
,
(

pd

-consistent)
, and
(Respect-

alw

)
for
I
because it satisfies these properties for
J^{\prime}
.

•

It remains to deal with
(Respect-

F

)
. For
S\in\{\mathsf{subtree}(u),\mathsf{subtree}[u]\}
, we denote by
P_{|_{S}}
the tuple
(\mathbf{\hat{f}}_{|_{S}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}}_{|_{S}},(\mathtt{pd}_{\vec{\mathcal{W}}})_{|_{S}})
.
As
P
