m+1<p_{i}\in\mathcal{O}(p_{\varphi}\cdot m\cdot\log m)
,

5.

for
i\in[m]
,
\mathbb{F}^{*}_{p_{i}}
admits the
p_{\varphi}
-th root of unity, i.e.,
\omega(p_{i})
is well-defined,

6.

for
i\in[m]
, the numbers
p_{i}
,
\omega(p_{i})
, and the inverse of
p_{\varphi}^{||}
in
\mathbb{F}_{p_{i}}
can be computed in
\mathcal{O}(\log p_{\varphi}+\log m)
space and
(m\cdot p_{\varphi}\cdot|\varphi|)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time.

Proof.

The choice of
m
,
\mathsf{M}
, and
\mathsf{A}^{*}
ensures that we have
m\geqslant\mathsf{A}^{*}
and
2^{m}\geqslant\mathsf{M}
.
Further, each of
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}
is larger than
2
so their product is larger than
2^{m}
.
Since
p_{1}
is larger than the
m
-th prime number, we have
p_{1}>m+1
.
By the Prime Number Theorem for Arithmetic Progressions (Theorem 1.5 in
[
4
]
) it holds that
p_{m}\in\mathcal{O}(p_{\varphi}\cdot m\cdot\log m)
.
For
i\in[m]
,
p_{\varphi}
divides the order
p_{i}-1
of
\mathbb{F}^{*}_{p_{i}}
by definition so
\mathbb{F}^{*}_{p_{i}}
admits the
p_{\varphi}
-th root of unity.
The upper bound on
p_{i}
implies that the length of
p_{i}
is in
\mathcal{O}(\log p_{\varphi}+\log m)
.
So to compute
p_{i}
we can iterate through positive integers
p_{\varphi}+1,2\cdot p_{\varphi}+1,3\cdot p_{\varphi}+1,\dots
and for every candidate, check whether it is prime.
Once we find the
(m+i)
-th candidate
p_{i}
satisfying this property, we stop.
Now we can iterate through candidates
1,2,\dots
from
\mathbb{F}^{*}_{p_{i}}
to check whether it is a
p_{\varphi}
-th root of unity: for this we compute (modulo
p_{i}
) all powers of this candidate up to
p_{\varphi}
-th and check whether
p_{\varphi}
-th power is the first power equal to
1
.
And similarly, we can find the inverse of
p_{\varphi}^{||}
:
First, compute the value
p_{\varphi}^{||}
.
Then try all candidates
1,2,\dots
from
\mathbb{F}^{*}_{p_{i}}
and multiply each with
p_{\varphi}^{||}
(modulo
p_{i}
) until we find a value where this product becomes equal to
1
.
All of those computations can be carried out in space linear in the length of
p_{i}
and time polynomial in the sum of
p_{i}
and
||\leqslant|\varphi|
so the claim follows.
∎

So now to apply
lemma

7.2
with
n^{\prime}=m
, it remains to describe how, given
i\in[m]
and a number
s\in\mathbb{F}_{p_{i}}
, to evaluate the polynomial
(\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}))^{\prime}
at
s
modulo
p_{i}
.
We will use the simple fact that the evaluation of a sum (resp. product) of two polynomials is equal to the sum (resp. product) of their evaluations.
We start by proving the following intermediate lemma about the evaluation of
\mathcal{Q}^{p_{i}}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F})
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
r
be the root of the elimination tree
T
and let
i\in[m]
.
Given the prime number
p_{i}
, a vector
\bar{s}\in\mathbb{F}_{p_{i}}^{\ell+2}
of length
\ell+2
with entries from
\mathbb{F}_{p_{i}}
, and a set
\mathtt{F}\subseteq\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
, we can evaluate the polynomial
\mathcal{Q}^{p_{i}}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F})
at the vector
\bar{s}
(modulo
p_{i}
) in time
(2\cdot(d_{\varphi}+1)^{2}\cdot p_{\varphi})^{\ell\cdot(\mathsf{td}+\mathcal{O}(1))}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and space
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}\cdot|\varphi|\cdot(\log{d_{\varphi}}+\log{p_{\varphi}}+\log n+\log{|\varphi|}))
.

Proof.

We recall that by
lemma

7.4
we can compute
\omega(p_{i})
as well as the inverse of
p_{\varphi}^{||}
in
\mathbb{F}_{p_{i}}
in
\mathcal{O}(\log p_{\varphi}+\log m)
space and
(m\cdot|\varphi|)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
time.
To compute the value
\mathcal{Q}^{p_{i}}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F})(\bar{s})\pmod{p_{i}}
, we alternatingly use
lemma

6.10
(
7
) and
lemma

6.15
(
23
) to recurse until we reach a polynomial of form
\mathcal{Q}^{p_{i}}_{u}[I]
for some leaf
u
and some index
I\in\mathsf{Index}[u]
.
Then we use
lemma

6.7
to evaluate this polynomial at
\bar{s}
.
At each level of recursion we store the information about the current recursive call as well as
\mathcal{O}(1)
partial sums and products in
\mathbb{F}_{p_{i}}
.
Now we bound the space and time complexity of this procedure.

Time complexity.

During the recursive evaluation at some node
u
via
lemma

6.10
(
7
), the restriction of the index on
\mathsf{tail}(u)
remains unchanged and we only branch on the restriction of the index restricted on
\{u\}
.
Also the value
\mathtt{F}
remains fixed.
Further, during a recursive evaluation using
lemma

6.15
(
23
), the index remains the same in all recursive calls.
Hence, for every node
w
and every index
I\in\mathsf{Index}[w]
(resp.
I\in\mathsf{Index}(w)
), the evaluation of the polynomial
\mathcal{Q}^{p_{i}}_{w}[I]
(resp.
\mathcal{Q}^{p_{i}}_{w}(I)
) at
\bar{s}
is computed at most once during this process.
We recall that
\mathsf{td}
denotes the depth of
T
so
\lvert\mathsf{tail}[w]\rvert,\lvert\mathsf{tail}(w)\rvert\leqslant\mathsf{td}
holds.
Hence, for
\mathsf{tail}\in\{\mathsf{tail}[w],\mathsf{tail}(w)\}
there are at most
2^{\lvert\varphi\rvert\cdot\mathsf{td}}
vertex interpretations on
\mathsf{tail}
, at most
(d_{\varphi}+1)^{\lvert\varphi\rvert\cdot\mathsf{td}}
neighborhood functions on
\mathsf{tail}
, and at most
p_{\varphi}^{\lvert\varphi\rvert\cdot\mathsf{td}}
mod-degrees on
\mathsf{tail}
.
So each of the sets
\mathsf{Index}[w]
and
\mathsf{Index}(w)
contains at most
(2\cdot(d_{\varphi}+1)^{2}\cdot p_{\varphi})^{\lvert\varphi\rvert\cdot\mathsf{td}}
indexes in which the last coordinate is equal to
\mathtt{F}
.
The tree
T
contains exactly
n
nodes.
So altogether there are at most
2\cdot n\cdot(2\cdot(d_{\varphi}+1)^{2}\cdot p_{\varphi})^{\lvert\varphi\rvert\cdot\mathsf{td}}
recursive calls.
For the recursive equality
lemma

6.10
(
7
) the running time additional to recursive calls is bounded by
(2\cdot(d_{\varphi}+1)\cdot p_{\varphi})^{\mathcal{O}(\lvert\varphi\rvert)}\cdot(\log{p_{i}})^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
:
this follows directly from the sums and products involved in that equality.
And for
lemma

6.15
(
23
) this running time is bounded by
n\cdot(\log{p_{i}})^{\mathcal{O}(1)}

as we simply recurse with the same index to each child and then compute the product of at most
n
resulting values in
\mathbb{F}_{p_{i}}
.
By
3.1
, the graph
G
has at most
n\cdot\mathsf{td}
edges.
So for a (non-recursive) computation via
lemma

6.7
the running time is bounded by
(n\cdot\mathsf{td}\cdot 2^{|\varphi|}\cdot|\varphi|)\cdot(p_{\varphi}+(\log{p_{i}})^{\mathcal{O}(1)})
.
By
lemma

7.4
, we have
\log(p_{i})\in\mathcal{O}(\log p_{\varphi}+\log m)
and the definition of
m
implies
\log{p_{i}}\in\mathcal{O}(\log p_{\varphi}+|\varphi|\cdot(\log n+\log{|\varphi|}+\log d_{\varphi}))
.
Altogether, we obtain that the running time of this process is at most
(2\cdot(d_{\varphi}+1)^{2}\cdot p_{\varphi})^{|\varphi|\cdot(\mathsf{td}+\mathcal{O}(1))}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
.

Space complexity.

At each of
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td})
levels of recursion we store the information about the current recursive call (i.e., the index queried) as well as a constant number of partial sums and products in
\mathbb{F}_{p_{i}}
.
Since during the recursive evaluation at some node
u
, the index “restricted” to
\mathsf{tail}(u)
remains unchanged, at each level of recursion it suffices to store only the restriction of the index to
\{u\}
instead of the whole index.
Again, recall that
\log{p_{i}}\in\mathcal{O}(\log p_{\varphi}+|\varphi|\cdot(\log n+\log{|\varphi|}+\log d_{\varphi}))
so every partial sum and product requires at most that many bits.
Thus, the total space complexity is upper-bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}\cdot|\varphi|\cdot(\log{d_{\varphi}}+\log{p_{\varphi}}+\log n+\log{|\varphi|}))
.
∎
From this we can derive the following:

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
r
be the root of the elimination tree
T
and let
i\in[m]
.
First, the polynomial
(\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}))^{\uparrow}
is well-defined.
Second, given the prime number
p_{i}
, a value
s\in\mathbb{F}_{p_{i}}
, and a set
\mathtt{F}\subseteq\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
, we can evaluate the polynomial
(\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}))^{\uparrow}
at the value
s
(modulo
p_{i}
)
within the following time and space:

•

time
(2\cdot(d_{\varphi}+1)^{2}\cdot p_{\varphi})^{|\varphi|\cdot(\mathsf{td}+\mathcal{O}(1))}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(\ell)}
and

•

space
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}\cdot|\varphi|\cdot(\log{d_{\varphi}}+\log{p_{\varphi}}+\log n+\log{|\varphi|}))
.

Proof.

By
3.1
, the graph
G
has at most
n\cdot\mathsf{td}
edges.
Then by
lemma

5.16
, the degree of every variable in
\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F})
is smaller than
\mathsf{A}
so the polynomial
(\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}))^{\uparrow}
is indeed well-defined.
Then by
7.1
, the evaluation of
(\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}))^{\uparrow}
at
s
is equal to the evaluation of
\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F})
at the vector
\bar{s}
defined as
\bar{s}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=(s,s^{\mathsf{A}^{\ell+1}},(s^{\mathsf{A}^{i}})_{i\in[\ell]})\in\mathbb{F}_{p_{i}}^{\ell+2}
.
So as the first step we compute the vector
\bar{s}
in time
(\lvert\varphi\rvert\cdot d_{\varphi}\cdot n\cdot\mathsf{td})^{\ell+\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot(\log{p_{i}})^{\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|)}
and space
(\log{p_{i}})^{\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|)}
(we remark that this process can be accelerated using exponentiation by squaring but we do not use it since this is not going to be the bottleneck our main algorithm, presented in the next theorem).
Now by
lemma

6.5
the evaluation of
\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F})
at
\bar{s}
boils down to the evaluation of
\mathcal{P}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}^{\prime})
at
\bar{s}
for every
\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathtt{F}
and summing up the results with the correct sign.
So fix
\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathtt{F}
.
By
lemma

6.6
, it holds that
\mathcal{P}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}^{\prime})(\bar{s})\equiv_{p_{i}}\mathcal{Q}^{p_{i}}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}^{\prime})(\bar{s})
.
By
lemma

7.5
we can compute this evaluation in time
(2\cdot(d_{\varphi}+1)^{2}\cdot p_{\varphi})^{|\varphi|\cdot(\mathsf{td}+\mathcal{O}(1))}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and space
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}\cdot|\varphi|\cdot(\log{d_{\varphi}}+\log{p_{\varphi}}+\log n+\log{|\varphi|}))
.
This process is then repeated at most
2^{|\varphi|}
times (for every
\mathtt{F}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathtt{F}
) so the claim follows.
∎

7.2
Wrapping up the model checking algorithm

Now we are ready to prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 7.7
.

There is an algorithm that given a formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
with
\ell
size measurements, a graph
G
and an elimination tree
T
of
G
of depth at most
\mathsf{td}
, runs in time
((d_{\varphi}+1)\cdot p_{\varphi})^{\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|\cdot\mathsf{td})}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(\ell)}
and space
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}\cdot|\varphi|\cdot(\log{d_{\varphi}}+\log{p_{\varphi}}+\log n+\log{|\varphi|}))
and decides whether
G\vDash\varphi
holds.

Proof.

First, by
lemma

4.2
we may assume that
\varphi
is in
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
: otherwise, we can transform it with only a linear blowup in the length and without increasing the values
d_{\varphi}
and
p_{\varphi}
and by increasing the value
\ell
by at most
2
—we remark that this blowup is the reason for
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|\cdot\mathsf{td})
instead of
|\varphi|\cdot(\mathsf{td}+\mathcal{O}(1))
in the exponent of the running time.
Let now
r
denote the root of
T
.
By
lemmas

5.16
and
5.17
, to check if
G
satisfies
\varphi
, it suffices to iterate over all
\mathtt{F}\subseteq\mathsf{EQ}(\varphi)
, all integers
j\in[2\cdot|\varphi|\cdot d_{\varphi}\cdot n\cdot\mathsf{td}]_{0}
, and all tuples
\bar{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{\ell})\in([n\cdot\mathsf{td}]_{0})^{\ell}
and check whether
[\![\varphi]\!]^{(\mathtt{F},\bar{c})}=1
holds and the coefficient in
\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F})
in front of
\alpha^{j}\beta^{j}\gamma^{\bar{c}}
is non-zero.
We recall that
\ell\leqslant|\varphi|
holds.
First of all, note that we can iterate over such candidates and check whether
[\![\varphi]\!]^{(\mathtt{F},\bar{c})}=1
holds in time

\mathcal{O}(2^{|\varphi|}\cdot|\varphi|\cdot d_{\varphi}\cdot n\cdot\mathsf{td}\cdot(n\cdot\mathsf{td}+1)^{\ell})=d_{\varphi}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(\ell)}

(31)

and space
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|\cdot\log n+\log d_{\varphi})
(because
\mathsf{td}
is upper-bounded by
n
).

So now we focus on checking whether the corresponding coefficient is non-zero for fixed values of
j
,
\bar{c}
, and
\mathtt{F}
.
By
lemma

7.6
, the polynomial
(\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}))^{\uparrow}
is well-defined and its degree is upper-bounded by
\mathsf{A}^{*}
.
By
7.1
, checking whether the desired coefficient is non-zero is equivalent to determining whether the coefficient at
x^{j+\Bigl(\sum_{i\in[\ell]}c_{i}\cdot(\mathsf{A}+1)^{i}\Bigr)+j\cdot(\mathsf{A}+1)^{\ell+1}}
in
(\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}))^{\uparrow}
is non-zero.
Note that to decide this, it suffices to compute the bits of this coefficient one by one and check if at least one is non-zero.

By
lemma

6.2
, every coefficient of
\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F})
, and equivalently, of
(\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}))^{\prime}
, is non-negative and upper-bounded by
\mathsf{M}
.
Furthermore, the degree of
(\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}))^{\uparrow}
is less than
\mathsf{A}^{*}
by construction.
We now apply
lemma

7.2
to the polynomial
(\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}))^{\uparrow}
, the value
n^{\prime}=m
,
c=p_{\varphi}
, and the primes
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}
.
By
lemma

7.4
together with observations we just made they satisfy the preconditions of the lemma.
By
lemma

7.6
, for any
i\in[m]
and any
s\in\mathbb{F}_{p_{i}}
we can compute the evaluation
(\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}))^{\prime}(s)\pmod{p_{i}}
in time
\mathsf{time}=((d_{\varphi}+1)\cdot p_{\varphi})^{\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|\cdot\mathsf{td})}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(\ell)}
and space
\mathsf{space}=\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{td}\cdot|\varphi|\cdot(\log{d_{\varphi}}+\log{p_{\varphi}}+\log n+\log{|\varphi|}))
.

lemma

7.2
then implies that we can reconstruct the bits of the coefficient of
(\mathcal{P}^{=}_{r}(\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset,\mathtt{F}))^{\uparrow}
at the monomial
x^{j+\Bigl(\sum_{i\in[\ell]}c_{i}\cdot(\mathsf{A}+1)^{i}\Bigr)+j\cdot(\mathsf{A}+1)^{\ell+1}}
by increasing the running time by a factor of
(p_{\varphi}\cdot m)^{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and using the additional space of
\mathcal{O}(\mathsf{space}+\log{n^{\prime}}+\log c)
.
Repeating this process for every candidate
j,\bar{c},\mathtt{F}
increases the running time by a factor of (
31
) and remains the same space complexity so the claim follows.
∎

8
Extensions of the Logic

8.1
Clique predicate

Here we sketch that it is not difficult to extend our algorithm to additionally handle a predicate of form
\mathsf{clique}(X)
which reflects for a vertex set variable
X
that the subgraph induced by
X
is a clique.
The main tool for this is the
fast subset cover
by Björklund et al.
[
7
]
which we shortly present.
Let
U
be a finite set and
R
be a ring. Let
g_{1},\dots,g_{t}\colon 2^{U}\to R
be set functions, for some integer
t
. For every
i\in[t]
, the
zeta-transform

\zeta g_{i}\colon 2^{U}\to R
of
g_{i}
is defined by

(\zeta g_{i})(Y)=\sum\limits_{X\subseteq Y}g_{i}(X),

and similarly, the
Möbius-transform

\mu g_{i}\colon 2^{U}\to R
of
g_{i}
is given by

(\mu g_{i})(Y)=\sum\limits_{X\subseteq Y}(-1)^{|Y\setminus X|}g_{i}(X).

The
cover product

g_{1}\ast_{c}g_{2}\ast_{c}\ldots\ast_{c}g_{t}\colon 2^{U}\to R
of
g_{1},\dots,g_{t}
is defined as

(g_{1}\ast_{c}g_{2}\ast_{c}\ldots\ast_{c}g_{t})(Y)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}X_{1},\dots,X_{t}\subseteq U\colon\\
X_{1}\cup\dots\cup X_{t}=Y\end{subarray}}g_{1}(X_{1})\cdot g_{2}(X_{2})\cdot\ldots\cdot g_{t}(X_{t}).

The following result of Björklund et al.
[
7
]
is crucial to handle the clique-predicates:

Lemma 8.1

(​​
[
7
]
)
.

Let
U
be a finite set, let
R
be a ring, and let
g_{1},\dots,g_{t}\colon 2^{U}\to R
be set functions for a positive integer
t
.
Then for every
X\in 2^{U}
, it holds that

(g_{1}\ast_{c}g_{2}\ast_{c}\ldots\ast_{c}g_{t})(X)=\mu\bigl((\zeta g_{1})(X)\cdot(\zeta g_{2})(X)\cdot\ldots\cdot(\zeta g_{t})\bigr)(X).

Informally speaking, this result allows to replace the time-consuming combination of
t
subsets of
X
via a pointwise product.

Now we sketch how to use this technique to extend our approach from the previous section to the clique predicate.
The crucial observation is that for every clique in
G
, there is a root-to-leaf path in the elimination tree
T
on which every vertex of this clique lies.
Thus, a clique predicate can be
falsified
in two ways.
Either two vertices of the non-clique come from different subtrees of some node, or they are in the ancestor-descendant relation but there is no edge between them.
Now consider a formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO}_{2}\mathsf{+K}
and let
\mathsf{CLS}(\varphi)\subseteq\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
(stands for “clique set”) denote the set of vertex set variables
X
such that
\mathsf{clique}(X)
occurs in the formula.
We first extend the definition of the index of a triad
\mathsf{tss}
to a set
S\subseteq\mathsf{CLS}(\varphi)
.
The set
S
then represents the vertex set variables
X
such that there is a vertex
v\in\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)\cap\mathsf{subtree}
and a vertex
w\in\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
with
v\neq w
such that
v
and
w
are not adjacent.
So in simple words, the vertex
v
“falsifies” the clique predicate
\mathsf{clique}(X)
.
Furthermore, to also capture the possibility that two vertices land in different subtrees and thus, falsify some clique, we further extend the index by a set
Z\subseteq\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
which reflects all vertex variables
X
satisfying
\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)\cap\mathsf{subtree}\neq\emptyset
.

With this idea we can proceed as follows algorithmically.
At the root, we additionally iterate over all possible
S\subseteq\mathsf{CLS}(\varphi)
to fix the sets of clique-predicates we want to falsify:
with this additional information, we can check whether with this “guess” the formula evaluates to
1
, otherwise we discard the candidate.
Also at the root we iterate over the subsets
Z\subseteq\mathsf{var}^{V}(\varphi)
to fix which vertex sets are non-empty.
At a leaf-node
u
(cf.
lemma

6.7
) the set
\mathsf{subtree}[u]
is empty so we additionally check whether
S
or
Z
in the index is non-empty: in this case, we simply return
0
, otherwise we proceed as before.
At a forget-node (cf.
lemma

6.10
), when we branch over all restrictions of the index to
\{u\}
we proceed as follows.
First, check which cliques, say
S_{u}
, are falsified by
u
in this branch, i.e., for which vertex set variables
X
there exists a vertex
v
such that have
u\in\mathbf{f}_{u}(X)
,
v\in\mathbf{f}(X)
, and
uv\notin E(G)
, and then (1) discard the branch if some clique outside
S
is falsified (i.e., if
S_{u}\not\subseteq S
holds), otherwise (2) allow any subset of
Q\subseteq S_{u}
to also be falsified in
\mathsf{subtree}(u)
, and (3) enforce the remaining cliques in
S\setminus S_{u}
to be falsified in the subtree (i.e., use
Q\cup(S\setminus S_{u})
in the recursive call).
Second, for every vertex set variable
X
with
u\in\mathbf{f}(X)
we can either keep
X
in the set
Z
for the recursive call (to reflect that the vertex set
X
is non-empty also in
\mathsf{subtree}(u)
) or discard it.
This increases the branching by a factor of
2^{\mathcal{O}(\lvert\varphi\rvert)}
.

At a join-node (cf.
lemma

6.15
)
u
with children
v_{1},\dots,v_{t}
we proceed as follows.
First, we iterate over all subsets
S_{2}\subseteq S
: this set then reflects which cliques are falsified due to having two vertices from sets in
\mathsf{subtree}[v_{1}],\dots,\mathsf{subtree}[v_{t}]
.
Second, we iterate over all sets
S_{1}
satisfying
S\setminus S_{2}\subseteq S_{1}\subseteq S
.
The set
S_{1}
reflects which of the cliques are falsified in the same subtree: it has to contain all cliques in
S\setminus S_{2}
but is allowed to further falsify the elements of
S_{2}
.

For a variable
X\in S_{2}
, to satisfy the constraint “
the vertices of
X
occur in at least two of
\mathsf{subtree}[v_{1}],\dots,\mathsf{subtree}[v_{t}]
”, we can take the partial solutions where
the vertices of
X
occur in at least one of
\mathsf{subtree}[v_{1}],\dots,\mathsf{subtree}[v_{t}]
and subtract the ones where
the vertices of
X
occur in exactly one of
\mathsf{subtree}[v_{1}],\dots,\mathsf{subtree}[v_{t}]
.
To compute the number of partial solutions where
the vertices of
X
occur in exactly one of
\mathsf{subtree}[v_{1}],\dots,\mathsf{subtree}[v_{t}]
we rely on the fast subset cover and we additionally equip the polynomials of the children of
u
with a new formal variable
\delta
.
The exponent of
\delta
in the polynomial of the child
v_{i}
reflects whether or not a vertex of
X
occurs in
\mathsf{subtree}[v_{i}]
.
Thus, when we multiply the polynomials of the children of
u
, we can safely discard the partial products where the exponent of
\delta
is larger than one as this corresponds to the case that vertices of
X
occur in at least two children.
On the other hand, fast subset cover ensures that the vertices of
X
appear in
at least
one of
\mathsf{subtree}[v_{1}],\dots,\mathsf{subtree}[v_{t}]
.
Crucially, the same variable
\delta
(whose exponent is bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\lvert\varphi\rvert)
) can be used to correctly keep track of all variables in
S_{2}
simultaneously.
The usage of the variable
\delta
increases the space complexity only by a factor of
\mathcal{O}(\lvert\varphi\rvert)
.
For technical details, we refer to the work of Bergougnoux et al.
[
5
]
where they employ similar ideas relying on fast subset cover with one new formal variable to solve
Independent Set
parameterized by shrubdepth.

As for the set
Z\setminus S_{2}
, we apply fast subset cover to efficiently combine all possibilities for
Z=Z_{1}\cup\dots\cup Z_{t}
where
Z_{i}
reflects the vertex variables that are non-empty in
\mathsf{subtree}[v_{i}]
. Altogether, due to fast subset cover we get a total branching factor of
2^{\mathcal{O}(\lvert\varphi\rvert)}
for a join-node (instead of using the same index for each child (cf.
lemma

6.15
)). With this we obtain our logspace algorithm for
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+K}
:
See
1.3

8.2
Connectivity and acyclicity

Here we sketch how we can extend our results obtained so far to acyclicity and connectivity constraints.
For this, let
\varphi
be a formula from
\mathsf{NEO}_{2}\mathsf{+ACK}
.
By
lemma

4.2
we may assume that
\varphi
is in
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO}_{2}\mathsf{+ACK}
.
And let
G
be a graph and
T
an elimination forest of
G
of depth at most
\mathsf{td}
.
We carry out some transformations of
\varphi
following the ideas of Bergougnoux et al.
[
6
]
.
First, we bring the quantifier-free part of
\varphi
in disjunctive normal form to obtain the equivalent formula
\psi
.
Now
\psi
is of form
\exists\bar{X}\psi_{1}\lor\psi_{2}\lor\dots\lor\psi_{t}
where
\exists\bar{X}
is a sequence of existential quantifiers and each
\psi_{i}
is a conjunction of (possibly negated) set equalities, size measurements, connectivity and acyclicity constraints, and clique constraints.
And the task of model checking
\varphi
in a graph
G
is equivalent to checking if there exists an index
i\in[t]
with
G\vDash\exists\bar{X}\psi_{i}
.
Observe that we have
t\leqslant 2^{\mathcal{O}(\lvert\varphi\rvert)}
.
Also for every
i\in[t]
, we have
\lvert\psi_{i}\rvert\in\mathcal{O}(\lvert\varphi\rvert)
,
p_{\psi_{i}}\leqslant p_{\varphi}
, and
d_{\psi_{i}}\leqslant d_{\varphi}
.
This is because every set equality, every size measurement, and every and connectivity or acyclicity constraint occurring in
\varphi
occurs at most once in
\psi_{i}
, possibly (un)negated, and on the other hand, every such element occurring in
\psi_{i}
has to occur in
\varphi
as well.
So we fix a value
i\in[t]
and focus on checking
G\vDash\exists\bar{X}\psi_{i}
.

Connectivity

A subgraph induced by a vertex set
X
is disconnected if and only if we can partition
X
into two non-empty sets with no edges between them.
So for a vertex set variable
X
, the constraint
\lnot\mathsf{con}(X)
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\exists A\exists B\exists C\exists Z_{1}\exists Z_{2}\,

\displaystyle\neg(Z_{1}=\emptyset)\land\neg(Z_{2}=\emptyset)\land Z_{1}\cup Z_{2}=X\land Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}=A\land\emptyset=A\land\mathtt{E}=B\land

\displaystyle N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(Z_{1},B)=C\land C\cap Z_{2}=\emptyset.

We remark that even though this equivalent formulation might seem unnecessarily complicated, it ensures that after the replacements carried out next, the formula will still be in
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO}_{2}\mathsf{+ACK}
.
A similar remark will apply to edge sets.
Similarly, an edge set
Y
induces a disconnected graph if and only the set, say
Z
, of endpoints of
Y
can be partitioned into two non-empty sets
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
such that no edges in
Y
run between
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
.
So for an edge set variable
Y
, the constraint
\lnot\mathsf{con}(Y)
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\exists A\exists B\exists C\exists Z\exists Z_{1}\exists Z_{2}\,

\displaystyle\lnot(Z_{1}=\emptyset)\land\lnot(Z_{2}=\emptyset)\land Z_{1}\cup Z_{2}=Z\land Z_{1}\cap Z_{2}=A\land\emptyset=A\land\mathtt{V}=B\land

\displaystyle N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(B,Y)=Z\land N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(Z_{1},Y)=C\land C\cap Z_{2}=\emptyset.

As a next step we therefore replace as sketched above all occurrences of
\lnot\mathsf{con}(X)
in
\psi_{i}
where
X
is a vertex or an edge set variable.
During each such replacement we introduce new vertex and edge set variables.
After that, we push all existential quantifiers to the front to obtain a formula equivalent to
\psi_{i}
of form
\exists\bar{X}\xi^{\prime}
where
\xi^{\prime}
is a quantifier-free formula in disjunctive normal form and in which all constraints of form
\mathsf{con}(X)
are not negated.
For simplicity, we still denote this equivalent formula by
\psi_{i}
.

So to check if
G\vDash\psi_{i}
, we can discard the connectivity constraints and search for a “solution” where each of the required sets is connected.
Note that the formula
\psi_{i}
is still in
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO}_{2}\mathsf{+ACK}
.
Now we sketch how to extend the framework presented in the paper so far with the Cut&Count technique by Cygan et al.
[
17
]
to ensure, with high probability, that for every constraint
\mathsf{con}(X)
in
\psi_{i}
, the vertex set
\mathsf{con}(X)
is connected.
For now assume that the original formula
\varphi
did not contain acyclicity constraints, we consider them later.

Before describing the application of Cut&Count
[
17
]
to our setting, let us sketch the main idea behind it. Suppose we are looking for a vertex set
X
with a certain property
\alpha
such that, additionally, the subgraph of
G
induced by
X
is connected.
A partition
(X^{L},X^{R})
of a vertex set
X
is called a
consistent cut
if there is no edge in
G
with one endpoint in
X^{L}
and the other in
X^{R}
.
In other words, every connected component of
G[X]
is contained in one of
X^{L}
and
X^{R}
.
Hence, there are
2^{k}
consistent cuts of
X
where
k
denotes the number of connected components of
G[X]
.
And this number is divisible by
4
if and only if
X
induces a disconnected subgraph of
G
.
Thus, if we may, for some reason, assume that there exists at most one vertex set
X
with the property
\alpha
, then the task of becomes equivalent to counting, modulo 4, the pairs
(X^{L},X^{R})
of disjoint vertex sets such that
X^{L}\cup X^{R}
satisfies
\alpha
and there are no edges between
X^{L}
and
X^{R}
.
In other words, we replaced the time-consuming task of ensuring the connectivity to counting certain pairs of vertex sets with “simpler” constraints.

This idea can naturally be generalized to multiple connectivity constraints: as long as we may assume that there exists a unique “candidate solution” and the task involves connectivity constraints on vertex sets
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{r}
of the candidate solution, we can eliminate all connectivity constraints by counting the combinations of consistent cuts of each of
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{r}
.
Then deciding the existence of a solution where all of these
r
sets are connected is equivalent to checking whether the resulting number is not divisible by
2^{r+1}
(i.e., whether for none of these sets the number of consistent cuts is divisible by four).

Similarly, for an edge set
Y
, the endpoints of
Y
are precisely the vertices in
V_{Y}\mathrel{\vbox{\hbox{\scriptsize.}\hbox{\scriptsize.}}}=N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(V(G),Y)
, and this can be formalized in the logic. So to check connectivity of the edge set
Y
we can similarly count the consistent cuts of
V_{Y}
in the graph
(V_{Y},Y)
.
For this reason, in the remainder we assume that for every constraint
\mathsf{con}(X)
in the formula,
X
is a vertex set variable, and the edge set variables can be handled similarly.

The standard tool used to guarantee that, with high probability, the solution is unique (if it exists) is the classic Isolation lemma:

Theorem 8.2

(Isolation lemma,
[
30
]
)
.

For an integer
n
, let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq 2^{[n]}
be a non-empty set family over the universe
[n]
. For each
i\in[n]
, choose a weight
w(i)\in[2n]
uniformly and independently at random. Then with probability at least
1/2
there exists a unique set of minimum weight in
\mathcal{F}
.

Now we are ready to explain how to incorporate the required changes in our algorithm from
section

7
to handle the connectivity constraints.
Let

L=2\cdot\lvert\psi_{i}\rvert\cdot n\cdot(1+\mathsf{td}+(d_{\psi_{i}}+1)+\mathsf{td}\cdot 2\cdot d_{\psi_{i}}),

this value will be used as the largest weight.
Recall that any partial solution in
section

7
has form
(\mathbf{\hat{f}},\mathbf{\hat{g}},\hat{\mathbb{E}},\vec{\mathcal{W}})
where the elements of this tuple satisfy the conditions from
definition

5.11
.
Thus we can view every partial solution as a subset of a universe of size
2^{\lvert\psi_{i}\rvert\cdot n\cdot(1+\mathsf{td}+(d_{\psi_{i}}+1)+\mathsf{td}\cdot 2\cdot d_{\psi_{i}})}
which consists of the following elements:

•

at most
\lvert\psi_{i}\rvert\cdot n
pairs of form
(X,v)
where
X
is a vertex set variable and
v
is a vertex of
G
,

•

at most
\lvert\psi_{i}\rvert\cdot n\cdot\mathsf{td}
pairs of form
(Y,e)
where
Y
is an edge set variable and
e
is an edge of
G
,

•

at most
\lvert\psi_{i}\rvert\cdot n\cdot(d_{\psi_{i}}+1)
triples of form
(C,v,i)
with
C\in
,
v
being a vertex of
G
, and
i\in[d_{\psi_{i}}]_{0}
,

•

and at most
\lvert\psi_{i}\rvert\cdot n\cdot\mathsf{td}\cdot 2\cdot d_{\psi_{i}}
pairs of form
(C,\vec{e})
where
C\in
and
\vec{e}\in\vec{A}(\vec{G}_{\varphi})
.

So first, in our algorithm, we sample the following weight functions:

1.

For every vertex set variable
X
, the weight function
w_{X}\colon V(G)\to[L]
.

2.

For every edge set variable
Y
, the weight function
w_{Y}\colon E(G)\to[L]
.

3.

For every
C\in
, the weight function
w^{\hat{\mathbb{E}}}_{C}\colon V(G)\times[d_{\psi_{i}}]_{0}\to[L]
.

4.

And for every element
C\in
, the weight function
w^{\vec{\mathcal{W}}}_{C}\colon\vec{A}(\vec{G}_{\varphi})\to[L]
.

For every weight function and every element in its domain, we sample the weight of this element independently at random from the range of the values of the weight function.
We naturally define a weight of a partial solution as the sum of the weights of all “building blocks” of a partial solution, we restrict the weighting of vertices to
\mathsf{subtree}
only.
In the next argument, we refer to the proof of
theorem

7.7
.
The choice of the weights together with
theorem

8.2
now imply the following.
If the union of the sets

\mathsf{Part}_{r}^{=}(\emptyset^{4},\mathtt{F},j,\bar{c},j)
over all
j\in\mathbb{N}
,
\bar{c}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
,
\mathtt{F}\subseteq\mathsf{EQ}(\mathtt{F})
with
[\![\psi_{i}]\!]^{(\mathtt{F},\bar{c})}

restricted to partial solutions respecting all connectivity constraints is non-empty, then it contains an element of a unique weight with probability at least
1/2
.
So now instead of counting partial solutions, we can count combinations of partial solutions and connected cuts of variables
X
such that
\psi_{i}
contains the constraint
\mathsf{con}(X)
.
Since connected cuts of a vertex set
X
can be easily modeled as

\exists A\exists B\exists X^{L}\exists X^{R}\ X^{L}\cup X^{R}=X\land X^{L}\cap X^{R}=\emptyset\land\mathtt{E}=A\land N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(X^{L},A)=B\land B\cap X^{R}=\emptyset,

(32)

we now replace every constraint of form
\mathsf{con}(X)
by such a formula, and then push all existential quantifiers to the front.
For simplicity, we still denote the arising formula by
\psi_{i}
.
Now we run our algorithm from the previous section with the following changes.

1.

First, randomly generate the weight functions as described above and store them. Let us emphasize that we
do not
weight the variables representing the sides of the connected cut.

2.

Then, extend all polynomials
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{Q}
by a new formal variable, say
\tau
, whose exponent keeps track of the weight of the partial solutions. Similarly to the definition of
\bar{c}
, we restrict the weight of vertex set variables to
\mathsf{subtree}
only.

3.

At the leaf computation, when the edge
e
is assigned to an edge set variable
Y
, we additionally multiply the polynomial by
\tau^{w_{Y}(e)}
. We proceed similarly for the arcs added to
\vec{\mathcal{W}}(C)
for some
C\in
.

4.

At a forget computation of a vertex
u
, similarly to the multiplication by
\gamma^{\bar{c}}
reflecting that
u
now belongs to the subtree, we multiply by
\tau^{w}
for the total weight
w
of
u
in all vertex set variables it is assigned to together with the weights of its values in
\mathbb{E}_{u}
.

5.

At the root, additionally to iterating through the values
j,\bar{c},\mathtt{F}
(cf. the proof of
theorem

7.7
), we iterate over all possible weights.
Note that the largest possible weight is at most

\lvert\psi_{i}\rvert\cdot n\cdot(1+\mathsf{td}+1+2\cdot\mathsf{td})\cdot L\in\mathcal{O}(n+\lvert\psi_{i}\rvert+d_{\psi_{i}})^{\mathcal{O}(1)}.

For each of those combinations, we reconstruct the corresponding coefficient of the polynomial and check whether it is divisible by
2^{k+1}
where
k
denotes the number of connectivity constraints in
\psi_{i}
.
If we find at least one combination where the coefficient is not divisible by
2^{k+1}
, then we output that the formula is satisfiable.

This algorithm does not give false positives and gives false negatives with probability at most
1/2
.
Finally, we analyse how these adaptations influence the running time and the space complexity.

1.

We reduce the model checking of
\varphi
to the problem of model checking at most
2^{\lvert\varphi\rvert}
formulas
\psi_{i}
such that the length of
\psi_{i}
is upper-bounded by
\mathcal{O}(\lvert\varphi\rvert)
,
d_{\psi_{i}}
is upper-bounded by
d_{\varphi}+1
, and
p_{\psi_{i}}
is upper-bounded by
p_{\varphi}
.

2.

To generate and store the weight functions we need space polynomial in
n
,
d_{\varphi}
, and
\lvert\varphi\rvert
.

3.

The largest exponent of any formal variable of the root polynomial
\mathcal{P}
is upper-bounded by a polynomial in
n
,
d_{\varphi}
, and
\lvert\varphi\rvert
so the bit length of each prime number we use (cf.
section

7
) remains logarithmic in
n
,
d_{\varphi}
, and
\lvert\varphi\rvert
.

Altogether we obtain the following intermediate result:

Theorem 8.3
.

There is a Monte-Carlo algorithm that given a formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
without acyclicity constraints, a graph
G
and an elimination tree
T
of
G
of depth at most
\mathsf{td}
, runs in time
((d_{\varphi}+1)\cdot p_{\varphi})^{\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|\cdot\mathsf{td})}\cdot n^{\mathcal{O}(\ell)}
and space polynomial in
n+\lvert\varphi\rvert+d_{\varphi}+p_{\varphi}
and decides whether
G\vDash\varphi
holds.
The algorithm cannot give false positives and may give false negatives with probability at most
1/2
.

Acyclicity

Now we briefly sketch how to approach the acyclicity constraints following a similar but slightly more involved idea of Cut&Count
[
17
]
.
Observe that for a vertex set
X
the subgraph
G[X]
is not acyclic if and only if there exists a non-empty subset
Y
of
X
such that
G[Y]
induces a subgraph of min-degree at least
2
.
For this reason, we replace every constraint of form

\lnot\mathsf{acy}(X)
with a vertex set variable
X
in
\psi_{i}
by

\exists A\exists B\exists Y\ Y\cap X=Y\land\lnot(Y=\emptyset)\land\mathtt{E}=A\land N^{\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}}(Y,A)=B\land Y\cap B=Y,

and the push all quantifiers to the front.
Similarly, if
X
is an edge set variable, we replace the constraint
\lnot\mathsf{acy}(X)
by

\exists A\exists B\exists Y\ \mathtt{V}=A\land Y\cap X=Y\land\lnot(Y=\emptyset)\land N^{\mathbb{N}^{+}}(A,Y)=B\land N^{\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}}(B,Y)=B

and the push all quantifiers to the front.
This is valid because an edge set
X
is not acyclic if it contains a non-empty subset
Y
which induces a subgraph of degree at least two (
A
is simply the whole vertex set and
B
is the set of endpoints of
Y
).
For simplicity we still denote the arising formula by
\psi_{i}
and observe that it is in
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO}_{2}\mathsf{+ACK}
.
We remark that this replacement ensures
d_{\psi_{i}}\leqslant d_{\varphi}+1
.
Now all acyclicity and connectivity constraints are non-negated and
\psi_{i}
is in disjunctive normal form so we may discard those constraints from
\psi_{i}
and instead, search for a solution which additionally satisfies them.

For this we follow the idea by Bodlaender et al.
[
8
]
.
We artificially insert a universal (i.e., adjacent to all vertices in
V(G)
) vertex
v^{*}
into the graph
G
and obtain the graph
G^{*}
.
Let
E^{*}
denote the set of edges incident with
v^{*}
.
We can easily obtain an elimination forest of
G^{*}
of depth at most
\mathsf{td}+1
by making
v^{*}
the new root and making the root of
T
to the unique child of
v^{*}
.
For a vertex set
X
by
E[X]
we denote the set of edges induced by
X
and by
E^{*}_{X}
we denote the edges from
E^{*}
with an endpoint in
X
.
Then the following lemma is easy to verify:

Lemma 8.4

(​​
[
8
]
Subsection 3.4)
.

A vertex set
X\subseteq V(G)
is acyclic in
G
if and only if there exists a set
E_{X}
satisfying
E[X]\subseteq E_{X}\subseteq E[X]\cup E^{*}_{X}
such that the graph
(X\cup\{v^{*}\},E_{X})
is a tree.

The graph
(X\cup\{v^{*}\},E_{X})
is a tree if and only if (1) it is connected and (2) the number of edges in
E_{X}
is precisely
|X|
(this is because a tree on
|X|+1
vertices has
|X|
edges).
Crucially, observe that if this graph is connected, then the number of edges is at least
|X|
.
So to ensure acyclicity it suffices to ensure connectivity and that the number of edges is no larger than
|X|
.
It is not difficult to formulate an analogous criterion for acyclicity of an edge set so we focus on vertex set variables in the remainder.

We sketch what changes need to be made to incorporate this idea in our model checking algorithm.
First, for every constraint
\mathsf{acy}(X)
occurring in
\psi_{i}
, we introduce an existentially quantified variable
E_{X}
.
Furthermore, we replace the constraint
\mathsf{acy}(X)
similarly to (
32
) to model the connected cuts of
X\cup\{v^{*}\}
with the only difference that we now have
X^{L}\cup X^{R}=X\cup\{v^{*}\}
—note that we can easily transform this condition into
\mathsf{core}\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]
.

The vertex
v^{*}
and the edges in
E^{*}
are “transparent” to the algorithm except for the treatment of the acyclicity constraints: by this we mean that the algorithm does not assign the vertex
v^{*}
to any vertex sets other than the sides of the connected cuts, and similarly the edges in
E^{*}
are only allowed to be assigned to some of the edge set variables of form
E_{X}
.
Additionally, we introduce two new formal variables
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
.
The exponent of
\rho_{1}
keeps track of the sum of cardinalities of the sets
E_{X}
and
\rho_{2}
keeps track of the sum of the cardinalities of the sets
X
over all vertex set variables
X
for which we enforce acyclicity.
At the leaf computation, when considering an edge, say
vw
, such that both
v
and
w
belong to
\mathbf{f}(X)
, the edge
vw
is necessarily placed in
\mathbf{\hat{g}}(E_{X})
.
On the other hand, we only allow such edges as well as the edges from
E^{*}
with an endpoint in
\mathbf{\hat{f}}(X)
to be placed in
E_{X}
.
And we multiply by
\rho_{1}
whenever such an edge is added to some
E_{X}
.
Then at the root we only consider the monomials in which the exponents of
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
are equal.
Except for these changes, we proceed as described earlier for connectivity: in particular, we weight every variable
E_{X}
to ensure the uniqueness of a solution with high probability.
As before we do not weight the sides of connected cuts.
In the end, we check if there is a monomial such that the corresponding coefficient is not divisible by
2^{r+1}
where
r
is the total number of connectivity constraints: those are both the original connectivity constraints of
\psi_{i}
together with connectivity constraints we introduced for acyclicity constraints.
Note that the exponent of the variables
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
can be of order
\Theta(q\cdot n)
where
q
denotes the number of acyclicity constraints in
\varphi
.
This is the reason behind the increase by a factor of
n^{\mathcal{O}(q)}
in the running time compared to
theorem

8.3

With this we obtain the following theorem:
See
1.4

9
Conclusions

We would like to conclude with some open questions.
The exponent of
n
in the running time of our model checking algorithm depends on the numbers
\ell
and
q
of size measurements and acyclicity constraints, respectively, in the formula.
So the first natural question is whether this dependence can be avoided.
For example, in the runtime of the model checking for
\mathsf{DN}
in
[
6
]
, the exponent of
n
depends only in the number of size measurements
|X|\prec m
where
m
is not a constant.

The next open question is whether we can derandomize and/or improve the space complexity of our model checking algorithm for
\mathsf{NEO_{2}}[\mathsf{FRec}]\mathsf{+ACK}
(i.e., when connectivity and acyclicity constraints are allowed) to become logarithmic in
n
.

Also, it can be observed that if
d_{\varphi}=1
, then our algorithm actually counts the models of
\varphi
.
So we are able to count, for example, dominating sets or independent sets: in fact, for these problems, for each integer
t
, we can count the number of solutions of size
t
.
However, in general, the same model of the formula yields multiple partial solutions at the root (due to the component
\vec{\mathcal{W}}
of the partial solution).
So is it possible to extend our algorithm for model counting?

Further, our logic cannot capture, for example,
Triangle Packing
. However, it is not difficult to use the techniques presented in this paper to solve this problem, and, more generally, handle the constraint of form “the edge set
Y
induces a disjoint union of cliques”.
So it is also reasonable to ask if there is a natural generalization of our logic (ideally not using too many ad-hoc operators) which places all problems under the same umbrella.

Appendix A
Discrete Fourier Transforms for Polynomials

In this appendix, we carefully check that
Theorems

3.3
and
3.4
holds for polynomials as well.
We recall that
D
is a finite set,
r
is a positive integer, and
p
is a prime such that
\mathbb{F}_{p}
admits the
r
.th root of unity
\omega
.
For
y,q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
we define
y+q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
as
(y+q)(d)\equiv_{r}y(d)+q(d)
for every
d\in D
.
For
h,g\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
we define also
h\cdot g\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
via
(f\cdot g)(y)\equiv_{p}h(y)\cdot g(y)
for every
y\in D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
.
In the following, we use
\mathsf{DFT}
and
\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}
as shortcuts for
\mathsf{DFT}_{D,r,p}
and
\mathsf{DFT}_{D,r,p}^{-1}
, respectively.

Lemma A.1
.

For
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
h^{1},\dots,h^{k}\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}[\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}]
,
we have

\mathsf{DFT}(h^{1}+\dots+h^{k})\equiv_{p}\mathsf{DFT}(h^{1})+\dots+\mathsf{DFT}(h^{k})

(33)

and

\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h^{1}+\dots+h^{k})\equiv_{p}\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h^{1})+\dots+\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h^{k}).

(34)

Proof.

Let
y\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
be arbitrary:
First, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathsf{DFT}(h^{1}+\dots+h^{k})(y)\equiv_{p}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{q\cdot y}(h^{1}+\dots+h^{k})(q)

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{q\cdot y}(h^{1}(q)+\dots+h^{k}(q))\equiv_{p}\Bigl(\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{q\cdot y}(h^{1}(q)\Bigr)+\dots+\Bigl(\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{q\cdot y}(h^{k}(q)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}(\mathsf{DFT}(h^{1}))(y)+\dots+(\mathsf{DFT}(h^{k}))(y)\equiv_{p}(\mathsf{DFT}(h^{1})+\dots+\mathsf{DFT}(h^{k}))(y).

And similarly

\displaystyle\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h^{1}+\dots+h^{k})(y)\equiv_{p}\frac{1}{r^{|D|}}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{-q\cdot y}(h^{1}+\dots+h^{k})(q)

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}\frac{1}{r^{|D|}}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{-q\cdot y}(h^{1}(q)+\dots+h^{k}(q))\equiv_{p}\frac{1}{r^{|D|}}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{-q\cdot y}(h^{1}(q)+\dots+h^{k}(q))

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}\Bigl(\frac{1}{r^{|D|}}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{-q\cdot y}h^{1}(q)\Bigr)+\dots+\Bigl(\frac{1}{r^{|D|}}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{-q\cdot y}h^{k}(q)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h^{1}))(y)+\dots+(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h^{k}))(y)\equiv_{p}(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h^{1})+\dots+\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h^{k}))(y).

∎

For a vector
\bar{a}=(a^{1},\dots,a^{\ell})\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
and
h\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
we define the mapping
h\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}[\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}]
as

\Bigl(h\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)(y)\equiv_{p}h(y)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}

for all
y\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
h\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
,
\bar{a}=(a^{1},\dots,a^{\ell})\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
. Then it holds that

\mathsf{DFT}\Bigl(h\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)\equiv_{p}\mathsf{DFT}(h)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}

(35)

and

\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(h\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)\equiv_{p}\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}

(36)

Proof.

Let
y\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
be arbitrary:
First, it holds that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DFT}\Bigl(h\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)(y)\equiv_{p}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{q\cdot y}\Bigl(h\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)(q)\equiv_{p}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{q\cdot y}h(q)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}\Bigl(\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{q\cdot y}h(q)\Bigr)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\equiv_{p}\mathsf{DFT}(h)(y)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}.

And similarly,

\displaystyle\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(h\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)(y)\equiv_{p}\frac{1}{r^{|D|}}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{-q\cdot y}\Bigl(h\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)(q)\equiv_{p}\frac{1}{r^{|D|}}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{-q\cdot y}h(q)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}\Bigl(\frac{1}{r^{|D|}}\sum_{q\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}}\omega^{-q\cdot y}h(q)\Bigr)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\equiv_{p}\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h)(y)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}.

∎

Further, for all
\mathbf{a}=(a^{1},\dots,a^{\ell})\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}
we define the functions
h_{\bar{a}}\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}
in such a way that

h(y)\equiv_{p}\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}h_{\bar{a}}(y)\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}.

holds for every
y\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
—since every polynomial is uniquely determined by the coefficients at its monomials, these functions are well-defined.
By definition we then have

h\equiv_{p}\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}h_{\bar{a}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}

(37)

Now we are ready to prove the two main results about DFTs for polynomials from
\mathbb{F}_{p}[\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}]
by relying on their analogues for
\mathbb{F}_{p}
:

Theorem A.3
.

For every
h\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}[\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}]
it holds that

\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(\mathsf{DFT}(h))\equiv_{p}h\text{ and }\mathsf{DFT}(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h))\equiv_{p}h.

(38)

Proof.

It holds that:

\displaystyle\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(\mathsf{DFT}(h))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:function-into-addends}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(\mathsf{DFT}\Bigl(\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}h_{\bar{a}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:dft-linear}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\mathsf{DFT}\Bigl(h_{\bar{a}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:dft-variables}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\mathsf{DFT}(h_{\bar{a}})\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:inv-dft-linear}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\mathsf{DFT}(h_{\bar{a}})\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:inv-dft-variables}}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(\mathsf{DFT}(h_{\bar{a}}))\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:inverse-dft}}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}h_{\bar{a}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:function-into-addends}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}h.

And similarly,

\displaystyle\mathsf{DFT}(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:function-into-addends}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}\mathsf{DFT}(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}h_{\bar{a}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:inv-dft-linear}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}\mathsf{DFT}\Bigl(\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(h_{\bar{a}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:inv-dft-variables}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{DFT}\Bigl(\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h_{\bar{a}})\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:dft-linear}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\mathsf{DFT}\Bigl(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h_{\bar{a}})\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:dft-variables}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\mathsf{DFT}(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(h_{\bar{a}}))\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:inverse-dft}}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}h_{\bar{a}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{i}^{a^{i}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:function-into-addends}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}h.

∎

And it also holds that

Theorem A.4
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{N}
, let
h^{1},\dots,h^{t}\colon(D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r})\to\mathbb{F}_{p}[\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{\ell}]
, and let
y\in D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}
. Then it holds that

(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(\mathsf{DFT}(h^{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathsf{DFT}(h^{t})))(y)\equiv_{p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{1},\dots,y^{t}\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}\\
y^{1}+\dots+y^{t}\equiv_{r}y\end{subarray}}h^{1}(y^{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot h^{t}(y^{t}).

Proof.

\displaystyle(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}(\mathsf{DFT}(h^{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathsf{DFT}(h^{t})))(y)\equiv_{p}(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathsf{DFT}(h^{i})\Bigr))(y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:function-into-addends}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathsf{DFT}\Bigl(\sum_{\bar{a}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a_{i}^{j}})\Bigr)\Bigr))(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:dft-linear}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{t}\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\mathsf{DFT}\Bigl(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}_{i}}\Bigr)\Bigr))(y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:dft-variables}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{t}\sum_{\bar{a}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}(\mathsf{DFT}(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}})\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}_{i}})\Bigr))(y)\equiv_{p}(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\sum_{\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{t}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\Bigl(\mathsf{DFT}(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}})\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}_{i}}\Bigr)\Bigr))(y)

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\sum_{\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{t}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{\sum_{i=1}^{t}a^{j}_{i}}\Bigr)\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathsf{DFT}(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}})\Bigr)\Bigr))(y)

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{t}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\colon\\
\bar{a}_{1}+\dots+\bar{a}_{t}=\bar{a}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{\sum_{i=1}^{t}a^{j}_{i}}\Bigr)\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathsf{DFT}(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}})\Bigr)\Bigr))(y)

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{t}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\colon\\
\bar{a}_{1}+\dots+\bar{a}_{t}=\bar{a}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}}\Bigr)\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathsf{DFT}(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}})\Bigr)\Bigr))(y)

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}

\displaystyle(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{t}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\colon\\
\bar{a}_{1}+\dots+\bar{a}_{t}=\bar{a}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathsf{DFT}(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}})\Bigr)\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}}\Bigr))(y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:inv-dft-linear}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}

\displaystyle(\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{t}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\colon\\
\bar{a}_{1}+\dots+\bar{a}_{t}=\bar{a}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathsf{DFT}(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}})\Bigr)\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}}\Bigr))(y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:inv-dft-variables}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}

\displaystyle(\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\Bigl(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{t}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\colon\\
\bar{a}_{1}+\dots+\bar{a}_{t}=\bar{a}\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathsf{DFT}(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}})\Bigr)\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}}\Bigr))(y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:inv-dft-linear}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}

\displaystyle(\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{t}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\colon\\
\bar{a}_{1}+\dots+\bar{a}_{t}=\bar{a}\end{subarray}}\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathsf{DFT}(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}})\Bigr)\Bigr)\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}})(y)

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{t}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\colon\\
\bar{a}_{1}+\dots+\bar{a}_{t}=\bar{a}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(\Bigl(\mathsf{DFT}^{-1}\Bigl(\prod_{i=1}^{t}\mathsf{DFT}(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}})\Bigr)\Bigr)(y)\Bigr)\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:fourier-convolution-result}}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{t}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\colon\\
\bar{a}_{1}+\dots+\bar{a}_{t}=\bar{a}\end{subarray}}\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\dots,y_{t}\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}\colon\\
y_{1}+\dots y_{t}\equiv_{r}y\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}}(y_{i})\Bigr)\Bigr)\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}}

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}

\displaystyle\sum_{\bar{a}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{t}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}\colon\\
\bar{a}_{1}+\dots+\bar{a}_{t}=\bar{a}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\dots,y_{t}\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}\colon\\
y_{1}+\dots y_{t}\equiv_{r}y\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\Bigl(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}}(y_{i})\cdot\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}_{i}}\Bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\dots,y_{t}\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}\colon\\
y_{1}+\dots y_{t}\equiv_{r}y\end{subarray}}\sum_{\bar{a}_{1},\dots,\bar{a}_{t}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\Bigl(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}}(y_{i})\cdot\Bigl(\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}_{i}}\Bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\equiv_{p}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\dots,y_{t}\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}\colon\\
y_{1}+\dots y_{t}\equiv_{r}y\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\sum_{\bar{a}_{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{\ell}}\Bigl(h^{i}_{\bar{a}_{i}}(y_{i})\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\gamma_{j}^{a^{j}_{i}}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:function-into-addends}}}{{\equiv_{p}}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\dots,y_{t}\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}\colon\\
y_{1}+\dots y_{t}\equiv_{r}y\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}h^{i}(y_{i})\equiv_{p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{1},\dots,y_{t}\colon D\to\mathbb{Z}_{r}\colon\\
y_{1}+\dots y_{t}\equiv_{r}y\end{subarray}}h^{1}(y_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot h^{t}(y_{t})

∎
